================================================================================
V_coredump_off_fatal4_n2_and_the_lost_edge_precursor.txt
Doc 170 -- TWO RESULTS FROM FATAL #4 (2026-09-21, device UTC 12:17).

  (1) The s8.13 coredump-off A/B is now n = 2 (fatal #3 AND fatal #4), and the
      second sample reproduces the first exactly.

  (2) *** A NEW CROSS-BOOT FINDING ***  Every `a2_power.c:2949` fatal ever
      observed (n = 3, in three DIFFERENT boots) is preceded 73.96 - 85.80 ms
      earlier by the bam-dmux RX-watchdog "lost edge" PC resync.  The resync is
      AP-side code added by patch 808, and qcom_bam_dmux is a LOADABLE MODULE,
      so the follow-up experiment needs no kernel flash.

Captured with:  dmesg / the ssr_ledger / rx_telemetry, over ssh, 12:17 UTC.
================================================================================


PART 1 -- THE s8.13 A/B, NOW n = 2
==================================

Fatal #4 fired at AP 3477.969305 s with the coredump capture still OFF.  It is
the first fatal of this boot with a DIFFERENT signature, and it reproduces
fatal #3's recovery structure exactly.

    919.62,1,sleepmgr,....,17      <- after fatal #1
    1825.87,2,sleepmgr,....,18     <- after fatal #2
    2718.81,3,sleepmgr,....,18     <- after fatal #3  (no change)
    3480.91,4,a2_power.c:2949,..,18 <- after fatal #4 (no change)

  => Two independent instruments agree: turning the capture off removes one
     q6v5_mba_load() and one q6v5_mba_reclaim() per fatal, removes
     "port failed halt", and cuts SSR-shutdown -> MBA-booted from ~0.83 s to
     ~0.13 s (0.70 s, 6.4x).  n = 2, same direction, same magnitude.

  See T_coredump_off_first_fatal_AB.txt for the retraction of my own call-graph
  correction that this measurement forced.


PART 2 -- FATAL #4'S SIGNATURE AND PERIOD
=========================================

Fatal times and intervals:

    913.640795   sleepmgr:4054   (boot start ~0; the modem came up at ~12.9 s)
   1816.046634   sleepmgr:4054   interval 902.405839 s
   2718.436833   sleepmgr:4054   interval 902.390199 s
   3477.969305   a2_power.c:2949 interval 759.532472 s   <-- NOT the clock

The sleepmgr clock was running true (902.406, 902.390 -- 17 ppm apart) and would
have produced its 4th beat at 2718.436833 + 902.4 = 3620.83 s.  Instead an
a2_power.c:2949 event fired 142.87 s EARLY and pre-empted it.

This is NOT a new anomaly.  Doc 162's taxonomy already separates the signatures
by their period distribution -- common_timer n=11 at 902.353 s (+/-281 ppm, the
clock), sleepmgr n=10 at 901.230 s, and a2_power n=7 spanning 68.5-941.3 s which
is NOT a clock.  759.53 s sits inside the a2_power band.  So the correct reading
is the one Doc 164 established: two mechanisms with different periods compete
inside one boot, and here the a2_power one won the race.

The ledger's history agrees that a2_power.c:2949 is not clock-locked: it appears
at uptime 719.00 s and 896.52 s in two earlier boots (see Part 3).


PART 3 -- *** THE LOST-EDGE PRECURSOR ***
=========================================

THE OBSERVATION
---------------
74.470 ms before fatal #4, this line appears -- and it appears exactly ONCE in
the whole boot (pc_resync_count went 0 -> 1):

  [ 3477.894835] bam-dmux 4080000.remoteproc:bam-dmux: bam_dmux: RX watchdog:
                 PC line asserted while pc_state=0 (lost edge), resyncing
  [ 3477.969305] qcom-q6v5-mss 4080000.remoteproc: fatal error received:
                 a2_power.c:2949:

Everything between those two lines is the resync's own work (bam_dmux_pm_restart()
+ bam_dmux_pc_ack()), which logs nothing.  Nothing else happened in those 74 ms.

CROSS-BOOT: 3 OF 3 a2_power.c:2949 FATALS, IN THREE DIFFERENT BOOTS
------------------------------------------------------------------
  boot  resync (lost edge)   fatal                gap          signature
  ----  -------------------  -------------------  -----------  ------------------
  P     714.454106           714.539903           85.797 ms    a2_power.c:2949
  Q     886.743235           886.817190           73.955 ms    a2_power.c:2949
  Y     3477.894835          3477.969305          74.470 ms    a2_power.c:2949
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  mean 78.074 ms   spread 11.842 ms   (the two closest are 0.515 ms apart)

THE COUNTER-EXAMPLES -- the resync is NOT sufficient
----------------------------------------------------
  boot  resync              next fatal after it                    outcome
  ----  ------------------  -------------------------------------  --------------
  R     1763.929555         1837.400528 (a2_power.c:1189)  +73.47 s  NO fatal near it
  S     4874.826138         none before the log ends at 5110      NO fatal
  S     4924.559122         none before the log ends at 5110      NO fatal
  S     4932.915755         none before the log ends at 5110      NO fatal
  S     4991.240271         none before the log ends at 5110      NO fatal

  => 8 resyncs observed in total; 3 were followed by an a2_power.c:2949 within
     100 ms and 5 were not.
  => "a2_power.c:2949  =>  a resync ~78 ms earlier"  is 3/3.
  => "a resync  =>  a2_power.c:2949"                 is 3/8.

  The log for boot S ends 119 s after its last resync, so "no fatal" there is a
  bounded claim, not an unbounded one.  Boot R's is unbounded in the useful
  direction: 73.47 s of subsequent log with no a2_power.c:2949.

PROVENANCE -- EACH SAMPLE IS VERIFIED AGAINST THE LEDGER
--------------------------------------------------------
The boot-P and boot-Q samples come from /overlay/q6trace.log, which is an
append-across-boots log, NOT one boot.  They are therefore cross-checked against
/overlay/ssr_ledger.csv, whose rows are independent (a 10 s poller).  The
ledger's column 1 is a NOTICED time that lags the true fatal by 0-10 s, so the
test is "is the ledger uptime 0-10 s LATER than the dmesg uptime?".

  boot  dmesg fatal (q6trace.log)   ledger row                 lag      verdict
  ----  --------------------------  -------------------------  -------  -------
  P     #1 714.539903               719.00,1,a2_power.c:2949    4.46 s   MATCH
  P     #2 1332.185916              1336.18,2,a2_power.c:1189   3.99 s   MATCH
  P     #3 2234.552365              2244.25,3,sleepmgr         9.70 s   MATCH
  Q     #1  381.484577               389.95,1,a2_power.c:1189   8.47 s   MATCH
  Q     #2  495.055798               502.08,2,a2_power.c:1189   7.02 s   MATCH
  Q     #3  886.817190               896.52,3,a2_power.c:2949   9.70 s   MATCH
  Y     #4 3477.969305              3480.91,4,a2_power.c:2949   2.94 s   MATCH

  Six for six.  P and Q are definitively DIFFERENT boots: each has its own
  n = 1,2,3 sequence and the ledger's coredumps column is monotonic across them
  (11,12,13 then 14,15,16), so Q came after P.

  PROVENANCE TRAP:  grep -c "a2_power.c:2949" /overlay/q6trace.log  returns 5,
  but that is 3 distinct EVENTS -- the boot-P line is duplicated three times in
  the file.  A naive count over this file inflates the sample.  Always check
  whether a log spans boots before counting fatals in it.

WHY THE MECHANISM IS PLAUSIBLE (stated as a hypothesis, NOT adopted)
--------------------------------------------------------------------
The resync branch, added by patch 808, does four things, two of which the MODEM
can see:

    WRITE_ONCE(dmux->pm_suspend_start_ns, 0);      /* AP-local */
    WRITE_ONCE(dmux->pc_state, true);              /* AP-local */
    bam_dmux_pm_restart(dmux);                     /* MODEM-VISIBLE: powers the
                                                      BAM down and up again */
    bam_dmux_pc_ack(dmux);                         /* MODEM-VISIBLE: the SMSM
                                                      power-collapse ACK */
  then complete_all(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);     /* AP-local */
       wake_up_all(&dmux->pc_wait);                /* AP-local */

  Reading A (the resync is a POISON PILL): the ACK and/or the BAM restart lands
  on a modem whose power state machine has already moved on, and a2_power
  asserts.  Under A the resync->fatal gap is a modem reaction constant, so it
  should NOT vary with how late the AP noticed the edge.

  Reading B (COMMON CAUSE): the modem's PC state machine had already
  desynchronised, which is why the edge was lost AND why a2_power asserts.  The
  resync is a symptom.

  Reading C (the AP's MISSED EDGE is the cause): the modem asserts the PC line
  and has a bounded wait for the ACK; the AP missed the edge and only the
  watchdog finds it, so the ACK arrives outside that window and the modem's
  timeout is what a2_power.c:2949 reports.  Under C the gap is
  (modem_timeout - edge_delay), so it SHOULD vary with the AP's detection delay.

  THE WATCHDOG POLLS EVERY 100 ms, so the AP's detection delay is uniform on
  [0, 100] ms (qcom_bam_dmux.c, patch 808:
  `schedule_delayed_work(&dmux->rx_watchdog_work, msecs_to_jiffies(100))`).
  The measured spread is 11.842 ms over 3 samples.  If Reading C were right the
  three edge delays would have had to land inside an 11.8 ms window out of 100 ms
  -- roughly a 4 % coincidence.  That mildly favours A over C, and is far too
  weak to act on.  It is recorded so the next session does not have to redo it.

  Reading A does NOT distinguish WHICH of the two modem-visible actions is the
  trigger, and the measurement cannot: both are inside the same 74-86 ms.

  NOT YET EXAMINED: `pc_timeout_count: 4` in rx_telemetry.  If that counter is
  the AP's own PC-handshake timeout it may be measuring the same window from the
  other side, and reading its definition is the cheapest next step.

THE DISCRIMINATOR, AND WHY IT IS CHEAP
--------------------------------------
qcom_bam_dmux IS A LOADABLE MODULE on this device:

    # lsmod | grep bam
    qcom_bam_dmux          32768  0
    # ls -la /lib/modules/6.12.94/qcom_bam_dmux.ko
    -rw-r--r--  1 root root 241232 Sep 21 08:07 .../qcom_bam_dmux.ko

so a variant that changes ONLY the resync branch can be deployed by swapping the
.ko -- no kernel flash, exactly the patch-823 playbook (scratch/mkko823.sh).

Two designs, in increasing strength:

  D1 (observational, zero behaviour change).  Add a dev_warn immediately before
     bam_dmux_pc_ack() and immediately after bam_dmux_pm_restart(), so the fatal
     can be timed against each action separately rather than against the single
     resync line.  Cost: one build.  Answers nothing on its own -- the two
     actions are < 1 ms apart -- but it removes the assumption.

  D2 (behavioural, decisive).  Make the resync SKIP bam_dmux_pc_ack() (leaving
     pm_restart() in place) and count a2_power.c:2949 per resync.  If Reading A
     is right and the ACK is the trigger, the signature disappears.  Risk: the
     modem never receives its ACK, so it may hang in a different way -- this
     variant is a probe, not a candidate fix, and must be reverted after.

  D3 (behavioural, the other half).  Skip bam_dmux_pm_restart() instead, keeping
     the ACK.  Together D2 and D3 split Reading A into its two sub-cases.

SEQUENCING DECISION (recorded so it is not re-litigated)
--------------------------------------------------------
The s8.13 coredump-off window is still open (2 of 20 post-disable fatals scored,
bar = 0 AP reboots).  D2/D3 must NOT be deployed inside it: they change which
fatals occur, and a reboot after the swap could not be attributed.  BUT the
window is free to leave running, and it produces fatals passively -- so the
passive half of this test costs nothing and is pre-registered below.

PRE-REGISTRATION (passive, on the running window)
-------------------------------------------------
  P1. The next a2_power.c:2949 fatal in this boot will be preceded by a
      "lost edge" resync within 100 ms.   (Currently 3/3; this tests for 4/4.)
  P2. The next 4 resyncs that are NOT followed by an a2_power.c:2949 within
      100 ms will be counted, to keep the 3/8 sufficiency figure honest.
  FALSIFIER: an a2_power.c:2949 with no resync in the preceding 100 ms breaks
  the association and Reading A/B/C all lose their AP-side handle.

  Note the two a2_power.c:2949 rows at 719.00 and 896.52 -- these are the boot-P

WHAT THIS FILE CHANGES
  - s8.13's A/B goes from n = 1 to n = 2, same direction and magnitude.
  - a2_power.c:2949 is confirmed off-clock (759.53 s), consistent with Doc 162.
  - A NEW AP-side precursor to a modem fatal: 3/3 a2_power.c:2949 preceded by a
    74-86 ms "lost edge" resync, with 5 counter-examples showing it is not
    sufficient.  The resync is patch-808 code and qcom_bam_dmux is a loadable
    module, so the follow-up needs no kernel flash.
WHAT THIS FILE DOES NOT CLAIM
  - It does NOT claim the resync causes the fatal.  3/8 is a correlation with a
    tight lag and a plausible mechanism, nothing more.
  - It does NOT explain the sleepmgr:4054 or a2_power.c:1189 fatals, neither of
    which has ever been seen with a resync in front of it.

================================================================================
PART 5 -- P2 FIRES, AND THE RESYNC STORM  (added 2026-09-21 ~12:30 UTC, boot
          uptime 4224 s, still no fatal #5)
================================================================================

The pre-registration's SECOND half existed precisely to stop the hits accumulating
alone.  It has now done its job twice over, and it also produced an observation
nobody was looking for.

5.1  P2 RESULT -- THE SUFFICIENCY NUMBER GETS WORSE, WHICH IS THE POINT
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
`pc_resync_count` reached 6 in this boot.  All six resyncs, in order:

  #  resync time    outcome after it                                   hit?
  1  3477.894835    fatal a2_power.c:2949 at 3477.969305 (+74.470 ms)  YES
  2  4079.094133    nothing logged                                     no
  3  4119.287485    pc-ack timeout at 4119.470945 (+183.460 ms)        no
  4  4175.847548    pc-ack timeout at 4176.057624 (+210.076 ms)        no
  5  4192.370087    nothing logged                                     no
  6  4224.514264    nothing logged                                     no

Pooling every boot for which a log survives:

  boot  resyncs   hits (a2_power.c:2949 within 100 ms)
  P     1         1
  Q     1         1
  R     1         0
  S     4         0
  Y     6         1
  ------------------------------------------------
  ALL  13         3

  =>  a2_power.c:2949  =>  a resync ~78 ms earlier   is  3/3   (unchanged)
  =>  a resync         =>  a2_power.c:2949           is  3/13  (was 3/8)

  The two numbers now differ by more than a factor of four.  Whatever the
  74-86 ms lag is, it is NOT a sufficient condition, and a fix built on
  "the resync causes it" would have to explain why ten resyncs do nothing.

  CAVEAT ON THE DENOMINATOR, stated because it cuts the other way: the resync
  counts for boots P, Q and R come from /overlay/q6trace.log, which is a PARTIAL
  append-across-boots log.  A missing resync would make the denominator LARGER,
  i.e. 3/13 is a floor, not a ceiling.  The hits are the solid half; the
  denominator is only as complete as the logs that survived.

5.2  THE RESYNC STORM -- 5 IN 145 s, AFTER 1 IN THE PREVIOUS 3478 s
-------------------------------------------------------------------
The resync rate is not stationary, and it changed abruptly:

  first resync        3477.894835   (the only one in the first 3478 s)
  next resync         4079.094133   (+601.199 s of silence)
  then                4119.287485, 4175.847548, 4192.370087, 4224.514264
                      => FIVE resyncs inside 145.420 s

Nothing about the AP changed at 4079 s: no patch, no reboot, no manual action,
`coredump` still `disabled`, `rproc=running`.  The modem simply began losing
PC-line edges repeatedly.  Telemetry at that moment: `pc_quiesce_ms` had fallen
to 1669 ms (the modem was cycling, not quiesced), `pc_irq_count` 1110,
`pc_ack_irq_count` 1120, `pm_suspend_attempts` 560 vs `pm_suspend_completions`
553 -- the same constant deficit of 7 as at 488/481 earlier in the boot.

**This is recorded as an OBSERVATION with a PRE-REGISTERED PREDICTION, not as a
finding.**  The prediction, written while the storm is still running and before
fatal #5 has fired:

  P3.  The storm is the modem approaching its next fatal.  If so, fatal #5
       should be a `lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054` at roughly AP 4380 s -- the
       sleepmgr clock's next beat after the 2718.436833 one would be
       2718.436833 + 902.4 = 3620.8 s, which has already passed silently, so
       either that timer restarted at the a2_power fatal (3477.969 + 902.4 =
       4380.4 s) or it was suppressed.  4380.4 s is ~156 s after the sample.

  P3-FALSIFIER.  No fatal at all in the following ~15 min, or a fatal with no
       resync storm in front of it, or a storm that simply stops.

  WHY IT MATTERS EITHER WAY: if the storm precedes the fatal, then the right
  window for the resync association is MINUTES, not the 74-86 ms that the three
  hits suggested -- and the three tight hits would be the tail of a storm that
  happened to be caught, which is a different mechanism entirely from "the
  resync causes the fatal".  If the storm does NOT precede the fatal, then the
  resync is a benign (if noisy) repair path and the three hits are more likely
  coincidence than cause.

5.3  `pc_timeout_count` IS THE WRONG INSTRUMENT FOR READING C -- DEFINITION READ
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This was recorded in s8.15 as "the cheapest next step".  Done, and the answer is
negative, which is itself worth writing down.

`pc_timeout_count` is incremented in exactly two places, both inside
`bam_dmux_runtime_resume()` (patch 808):

    ret = wait_for_completion_timeout(&dmux->pc_ack_completion,
                                      msecs_to_jiffies(250));
    if (!ret) { atomic64_inc(&dmux->pc_timeout_count);
                dev_warn(dev, "bam_dmux: modem pc-ack timeout during resume\n"); }

    if (!READ_ONCE(dmux->pc_state)) {
        ret = wait_event_timeout(dmux->pc_wait, READ_ONCE(dmux->pc_state),
                                 msecs_to_jiffies(1000));
        if (!ret || !READ_ONCE(dmux->pc_state)) {
            atomic64_inc(&dmux->pc_timeout_count);
            dev_warn(dev, "bam_dmux: modem pc_state wait timeout during resume\n"); } }

  => it counts the AP waiting for the MODEM (250 ms for the vote ACK, then
     1000 ms for pc_state).  Reading C was "the MODEM waits for the AP's ACK and
     times out".  This counter measures the MIRROR of that, so it cannot
     discriminate C.  **Do not design around it.**

  It does bound the AP's side, though: the AP gives the modem 250 ms to ACK a
  vote and 1000 ms for pc_state to rise.  Both are generous next to the 74-86 ms
  the fatal follows the resync by.

  AND THE COUNTER AGREES WITH THE LOG, which is a small but real cross-check:
  `pc_timeout_count` read 4 when exactly four such lines existed in dmesg, and 6
  when six did.  (Contrast the ledger, which is a "noticed" time and lags 0-10 s.)

5.4  THE `pc-ack timeout` LINES AFTER FATALS #1-#3 ARE RECOVERY ARTEFACTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every `modem pc-ack timeout during resume` in this boot, against the fatal
nearest before it:

    fatal #1  913.640795  ->  pc-ack timeout  914.071907   (+0.431112 s)
    fatal #2 1816.046634  ->  pc-ack timeout 1816.628053   (+0.581419 s)
    fatal #3 2718.436833  ->  pc-ack timeout 2718.871855   (+0.435022 s)
    fatal #4 3477.969305  ->  *** NONE ***

  So three of the four are ~0.43-0.58 s AFTER a fatal -- i.e. they are the AP's
  post-SSR resume finding the modem slow to ACK, which is exactly the base-rate
  reading Doc 170 s8.8/13h arrived at.  They are NOT precursors.

  The fourth fatal having none is a single observation and is NOT yet explained.
  The tempting story -- the coredump-off recovery is 0.70 s shorter, so the
  resume lands earlier and the modem is readier -- is speculation, and it is
  written down here as speculation so it is not later mistaken for a measurement.

  Note also the one `pc_state wait timeout` + `channels not initialized after
  resume` pair, at 915.085520 / 915.085715, +1.44 s after fatal #1.  That is the
  patch-814 path doing its job: the resume found no channels, armed the powerup
  work and the RX watchdog, and the data plane came back.

================================================================================
WHAT PART 5 CHANGES
  - P2 is scored and the sufficiency figure is now 3/13 (from 3/8).  The hits
    remain 3/3.  The two numbers now differ by >4x.
  - A NEW observation: the resync rate is non-stationary -- 5 in 145 s after 1 in
    3478 s -- recorded with a pre-registered prediction (P3) and a falsifier.
  - `pc_timeout_count` is the AP's own wait, not the modem's: the wrong
    instrument for Reading C.  Definition read; answer negative; written down so
    it is not re-investigated.
  - The post-fatal `pc-ack timeout` lines are recovery artefacts (0.43-0.58 s
    after three of four fatals), NOT precursors.
WHAT PART 5 DOES NOT CLAIM
  - It does NOT say the resync is exonerated.  3/13 is a correlation with a tight
    lag and no demonstrated sufficiency; the storm may mean the real window is
    minutes rather than milliseconds.
  - It does NOT explain fatal #4's missing post-fatal pc-ack timeout.
================================================================================


================================================================================
PART 6 -- FATAL #5  (2026-09-21 ~12:35 UTC, AP 4304.991306 s)
          (a) the coredump-off A/B is now n = 3, across THREE signatures
          (b) P3 IS SCORED AND ITS SIGNATURE PREDICTION FAILED
          (c) THE RESYNC ANTECEDENT IS `a2_power.c:2949`-SPECIFIC, and the storm
              is a period of BIDIRECTIONAL PC-handshake failure
================================================================================

6.1  s8.13.3 -- THE COREDUMP-OFF A/B IS n = 3, ON A THIRD SIGNATURE
-------------------------------------------------------------------
Fatal #5 is `a2_power.c:1189` at AP **4304.991306 s** -- a signature that has never
been seen with a resync in front of it.  Its recovery, with the capture still OFF:

    SSR before shutdown     4305.013229
    stopped remote processor 4305.092833
    MBA booted              4305.137760
    SSR-shutdown -> MBA     0.124531 s

Against the whole boot:

  #   signature         capture  pairs  port failed halt  SSR-shutdown->MBA
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  1   sleepmgr:4054     ON       2      914.443745        0.824902 s
  2   sleepmgr:4054     ON       2      1816.854035       0.831559 s
  3   sleepmgr:4054     OFF      1      ABSENT            0.125291 s
  4   a2_power.c:2949   OFF      1      ABSENT            0.130237 s
  5   a2_power.c:1189   OFF      1      ABSENT            0.124531 s

  THREE signatures, THREE boots' worth of capture-off recoveries, all inside
  0.125291-0.130237 s (spread 5.0 ms) against 0.824902-0.831559 s (spread 6.7 ms)
  for the two with it ON.  The two populations do not overlap and are 6.6x apart.

  Boot-wide: fatals 5, `MBA booted` 6 (cold boot + one per recovery), `port failed
  halt` 2 (exactly the two capture-ON recoveries), trace `01-09` x7, trace `10-23`
  x8 (= cold boot + 2+2+1+1+1), `cmd_open` 48 = 8 channels x 6 modem boots.
  coredump_live FROZEN at 18; ledger row `4313.31,5,a2_power.c:1189,...,18`.

  **s8.13 BAR: 3 of 20 post-disable fatals scored, 0 AP reboots.**

  One detail worth keeping: `SSR powerup: modem pc_state=1 (waited 580 ms)` -- the
  powerup had to wait 580 ms for the modem here, against 200 ms at fatal #4.

6.2  P3 IS SCORED -- THE SIGNATURE PREDICTION FAILED, THE STRUCTURE HALF-HELD
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  P3 predicted:  fatal #5 is a `lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054` at roughly AP 4380 s.
  Actual:        `a2_power.c:1189` at AP 4304.991306 s.

  **Signature: WRONG.**  **Timing: 75.0 s early** (1.7 % of the interval, but the
  mechanism named was simply not the one that fired).

  The weaker half -- "a fatal follows the storm" -- did hold: the storm ran
  4079.094133 -> 4352.497742 (273.4 s) and the fatal landed at 4304.991306, i.e.
  226 s INTO it.  **But the storm also CONTINUED 47.5 s PAST the fatal** (resyncs at
  4344.057823 and 4352.497742), so "the storm is the modem approaching its next
  fatal" is not cleanly supported either.  **The honest reading is that the storm
  BRACKETS the fatal rather than preceding it.**

  Fatal #5's interval from fatal #4 was **827.022001 s**, inside Doc 162's
  `a2_power` band (68.5-941.3 s) and off the 902.4 s clock -- the same story as
  fatal #4's 759.53 s.

6.3  THE RESYNC ANTECEDENT IS `a2_power.c:2949`-SPECIFIC
--------------------------------------------------------
Fatal #5's nearest preceding resync is **4224.514264, which is 80.477042 s earlier**
-- NOT 74-86 ms.  So the tight lag does not generalise:

  signature            resync within 100 ms before it?
  ----------------------------------------------------
  lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054    never observed (0/3 fatals)
  a2_power.c:2949                3/3   <-- 85.797 / 73.955 / 74.470 ms
  a2_power.c:1189                0/1   (nearest was 80.48 s earlier)

  Pooled sufficiency, updated:

    resyncs observed   boot P 1, boot Q 1, boot R 1, boot S 4, boot Y 8  =  15
    hits (a2_power.c:2949 within 100 ms)                                  =   3
    =>  a2_power.c:2949  =>  a resync ~78 ms earlier   is  3/3
    =>  a resync         =>  a2_power.c:2949           is  3/15   (was 3/13)

  So the antecedent survives as a NECESSARY-looking condition for ONE signature and
  is now 5x from sufficient.  It is a property of `a2_power.c:2949`, not of the
  fatal in general.

6.4  ★ THE STORM IS BIDIRECTIONAL PC-HANDSHAKE FAILURE
-----------------------------------------------------
Lining up both failure messages for the whole boot makes the storm legible.  The
two lines are the two DIRECTIONS of the same handshake:

  `RX watchdog: PC line asserted while pc_state=0 (lost edge), resyncing`
        = the MODEM asserted the PC line and the AP's edge IRQ missed it
          (AP waiting for the modem; the watchdog repairs it)
  `modem pc-ack timeout during resume`
        = the AP voted for power state and the MODEM did not ACK within 250 ms
          (modem waiting/slow; the AP gives up and continues)

Timeline, both together:

   913.640795  FATAL #1 (sleepmgr)          <- 3 clean recoveries, no storm
   914.071907  timeout   (+0.431112 s)
   915.085520  pc_state wait timeout (+1.444725 s) + channels-not-initialized
  1816.046634  FATAL #2 (sleepmgr)
  1816.628053  timeout   (+0.581419 s)
  2718.436833  FATAL #3 (sleepmgr)
  2718.871855  timeout   (+0.435022 s)
  3477.894835  resync    <-- 74.470 ms
  3477.969305  FATAL #4 (a2_power.c:2949)
  ---- STORM BEGINS (1 resync in the previous 3478 s; 5 now in 145 s) ----
  4079.094133  resync
  4119.287485  resync
  4119.470945  timeout   (+0.183460 s after the resync)
  4175.847548  resync
  4176.057624  timeout   (+0.210076 s)
  4192.370087  resync
  4224.514264  resync
  4297.231159  timeout   (-7.760147 s before the fatal)
  4304.244365  timeout   (-0.746941 s before the fatal)
  4304.991306  FATAL #5 (a2_power.c:1189)   <-- 226 s INTO the storm
  ---- STORM CONTINUES ----
  4344.057823  resync    (+39.066517 s after the fatal)
  4352.497742  resync    (+47.506436 s)
  4376.591183  timeout   (+71.599877 s)

  **Both directions of the handshake fail during the storm.**  A resync means the AP
  missed the modem's edge; a pc-ack timeout means the modem missed the AP's vote.
  Either way the modem's power-collapse handshake has become unreliable, and the
  fatal happens inside that window.  **This reframes the whole s8.15 question:** the
  interesting variable may not be "did a resync fire 74 ms before the fatal" but
  "is the PC handshake in a failing regime at all" -- a state that persists for
  minutes and can be measured from two counters (`pc_resync_count`,
  `pc_timeout_count`) without any new instrumentation.

  AND A COUNTER THAT MOVES WITH IT: `pm_suspend_attempts` minus
  `pm_suspend_completions` was a constant **7** all through the clean part of the
  boot (488/481, then 560/553) and is **10** at 576/566 after the storm.  One
  observation; recorded, not explained.

  **What this does NOT establish.**  Five of the storm's eight resyncs produced no
  fatal, and the storm outlived the fatal, so "storm => fatal" is false.  Whether
  the storm is a CAUSE, a CONSEQUENCE, or a third symptom of something else in the
  modem is exactly what D1/D2/D3 (s8.15) are for -- and the measurement above says
  the discriminator should now be scored on the STORM, not on a single resync.

================================================================================
WHAT PART 6 CHANGES
  - s8.13's A/B is n = 3, on three different signatures, two non-overlapping
    populations 6.6x apart.  Bar: 3 of 20 scored, 0 reboots.
  - P3 is FALSIFIED in its specific form (signature wrong); the storm BRACKETS the
    fatal rather than preceding it.  Recorded as a failed prediction.
  - The 74-86 ms resync antecedent is `a2_power.c:2949`-SPECIFIC: it does not hold
    for `a2_power.c:1189` (nearest resync 80.48 s earlier).  Pooled sufficiency is
    now 3/15.
  - NEW: the storm is BIDIRECTIONAL PC-handshake failure -- the AP missing the
    modem's edges AND the modem missing the AP's votes -- measurable from two
    counters, and a better candidate variable than a single resync.
WHAT PART 6 DOES NOT CLAIM
  - It does not claim the storm causes the fatal.  It outlives it, and 5 of its 8
    resyncs produced nothing.
  - It does not explain the `pm_suspend` deficit moving 7 -> 10.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 7 -- THE STORM KEEPS GOING WITH NO FATAL, IT PAIRS BOTH DIRECTIONS OF THE
HANDSHAKE ~184 ms APART, IT HAS NO EXTERNAL TRIGGER, AND THE DATA PLANE IS FINE
  measured 2026-09-21 18:22-18:40 host time, boot_id a9fd907c, uptime 4710 -> 4900
================================================================================

1. THE STORM OUTLIVES THE FATAL BY MINUTES, WITH NO SECOND FATAL

Fatal #5 (`a2_power.c:1189`) fired at AP 4304.991306 s.  Fatals are STILL 5 at
uptime 4814.48 and at 4900+.  Meanwhile the resync counter kept climbing:

  uptime  4710.44   pc_resync_count 15
  uptime  4814.48   pc_resync_count 18
  uptime  4852.90   pc_resync_count 20

  resync rate, storm window 4344.06 -> 4852.90 = 508.84 s, 14 resyncs = 1 per 36.3 s

So the storm is a REGIME, not an event: ~1 handshake failure every 36 s sustained
for 8+ minutes, with the modem running, the data plane up, and no fatal.

2. THE COMPLETE DECOMPOSITION OF THIS BOOT'S TWO FAILURE DIRECTIONS

The two messages are the two directions of ONE handshake.  Every line in this
boot accounts for itself -- there is no residue:

  LOST EDGE (modem asserted, AP missed the edge) -- 15 total
     1  preceded a fatal            3477.894835  -> fatal a2_power.c:2949 +74.470 ms
     5  paired with a pc-ack timeout  see the lag table below
     9  unpaired                     4079.094133 4192.370087 4224.514264
                                     4344.057823 4352.497742 4568.478005
                                     4631.484390 4671.180806 4702.440699

  PC-ACK TIMEOUT (AP voted, modem did not ACK within 250 ms) -- 14 total
     3  post-fatal RECOVERY ARTEFACTS  914.071907 (+0.431112 after #1)
                                       1816.628053 (+0.581419 after #2)
                                       2718.871855 (+0.435022 after #3)
     5  paired with a lost edge        see the lag table below
     6  unpaired                       4297.231159 4304.244365 4376.591183
                                       4416.404697 4528.431226 4663.257569

3. THE FIVE PAIRED EVENTS -- A ~184 ms LAG THAT DOES NOT CLOSE ARITHMETICALLY

  lost edge      pc-ack timeout   lag
  4119.287485    4119.470945      +183.460 ms
  4175.847548    4176.057624      +210.076 ms
  4448.051188    4448.217985      +166.797 ms
  4464.327857    4464.511287      +183.430 ms
  4710.440616    4710.617254      +176.638 ms
                                  mean 184.080 ms, spread 43.279 ms

  With the storm firing an event every ~36 s, a pc-ack timeout landing within
  250 ms of a resync by chance has probability ~0.7 %, so the coupling is REAL.

  BUT THE MECHANISM DOES NOT CLOSE, AND THAT IS THE FINDING.  The only site that
  prints "modem pc-ack timeout during resume" is `bam_dmux_runtime_resume()`:
      reinit_completion(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);          <-- :1421
      mutex_lock(&state_lock); bam_dmux_pc_vote(dmux, true); mutex_unlock();
      ret = wait_for_completion_timeout(&dmux->pc_ack_completion,
                                        msecs_to_jiffies(250));   <-- :1429
      if (!ret) { atomic64_inc(&pc_timeout_count);
                  dev_warn(dev, "bam_dmux: modem pc-ack timeout during resume\n"); }
  so the log line is emitted exactly ~250 ms after that resume's vote.  A line at
  T+184 ms therefore means the vote happened at T-66 ms -- i.e. BEFORE the resync
  line at T.  But the resync itself calls `complete_all(&dmux->pc_ack_completion)`
  (after `bam_dmux_pm_restart()` and `bam_dmux_pc_ack()`), which MUST have
  satisfied that waiter.  So either
    (i) the resync's `complete_all` is being undone by a later
        `reinit_completion()` -- there are exactly two, `bam_dmux_power_on()`
        :178 and `bam_dmux_runtime_resume()` :1421 -- or
    (ii) the coupling runs through the MODEM's ACK timing, not the completion
        object at all.
  Both are testable with one instrument: log the ordering of reinit / complete /
  the pc_ack IRQ.  That is what D1 should now be (see 8.15), NOT the original
  "log either side of the two modem-visible actions".

4. A CANDIDATE SECOND PRECURSOR, PRE-REGISTERED AT n = 1

Fatal #5 (`a2_power.c:1189`, 4304.991306) has a `modem pc-ack timeout during
resume` at 4304.244365 -- **746.941 ms BEFORE it**.  Fatal #4
(`a2_power.c:2949`, 3477.969305) has none within +/-1 s (nearest are 2718.871855
and 4119.470945).  So the antecedent is again SIGNATURE-SPECIFIC:
    `a2_power.c:2949`  <- lost edge            74.470 ms before   (3/3 boots)
    `a2_power.c:1189`  <- pc-ack timeout      746.941 ms before   (n = 1)
  **P4 (pre-registered).** If the next `a2_power.c:1189` fatal also has a
  `modem pc-ack timeout during resume` within +/-1 s before it, that is 2/2 and
  the pairing becomes worth chasing.  **P4-FALSIFIER.** No such line, or one
  after the fatal instead of before it.  Caveat that cuts against it: the storm
  makes pc-ack timeouts dense (~1 per 40-90 s), so P(one within +/-1 s) is ~2-5 %
  by chance -- low, but n = 1 with a 5 % base rate is exactly the shape of a
  coincidence, so this is recorded and NOT acted on.

5. THE STORM HAS NO EXTERNAL TRIGGER

  - AP side: `dmesg` has ZERO lines in 3490.000 -> 4078.999 s, the 589 s between
    fatal #4's recovery and the first storm resync.  Nothing announced the change.
  - Host side: `journalctl -k` is silent -- its LAST entry before the storm is
    17:34:12 host time, and the storm onset is ~18:11:20 host time.  Positive
    control: `journalctl -k` does return entries (it returned the 17:04-17:34
    lines), so this is a real negative, not a broken query.
  - Therefore the storm onset is AP/modem-INTERNAL.  This is the opposite of the
    s8.14 lesson in the useful direction: here the host was checked FIRST and
    excluded cheaply, instead of being blamed.

6. THE RUNTIME-PM RATE IS UNCHANGED ACROSS THE STORM ONSET

  `pm_suspend_attempts` = 623 at uptime ~4720  => 0.132 /s averaged over the boot
  measured during the storm:  7 per 55 s (18:15-18:18)  => 0.127 /s
                              32 per 247 s (18:18-18:22) => 0.130 /s
  So the AP is suspending and resuming at the SAME rate before and during the
  storm.  The storm is not "more churn"; it is the same churn failing to complete
  its handshake.

7. CORRECTION TO PART 6 -- THE `pm_suspend` DEFICIT IS ACCOUNTING, NOT A DEFECT

Part 6 recorded the `pm_suspend_attempts - pm_suspend_completions` deficit
(7 -> 10) as "recorded, not explained".  Reading the definitions closes it:
  - `pm_suspend_attempts` is incremented in `bam_dmux_runtime_suspend()` after the
    three guards, immediately before `bam_dmux_pc_vote(dmux, false)` (:1389).
  - `pm_suspend_completions` is incremented in exactly TWO places, both requiring
    `pm_suspend_start_ns` to still be non-zero: the pc_irq deassert branch (:1326)
    and `bam_dmux_pc_ack_irq()` (:1354).
  - `pm_suspend_start_ns` is CLEARED by `bam_dmux_runtime_resume()` (:1417) and by
    the RESYNC handler (:797).
  So a suspend attempt that is pre-empted by a resume or by a resync before its
  ACK lands can never increment the completion counter.  **The deficit is the
  count of suspend/resume (and suspend/resync) races -- 11 of 623 = 1.8 % -- and
  its growth is a churn proxy, not evidence of a lost ACK.**  Withdrawn as an
  anomaly.

8. THE DATA PLANE IS FULLY FUNCTIONAL DURING THE STORM

  ip -br addr: wwan0 UP, 10.29.91.236/29, IPv6, default route present
  ping -c 4 -I wwan0 8.8.8.8:  4 transmitted, 4 received, 0 % loss
                               min/avg/max = 40.521 / 168.794 / 512.092 ms
  and under 1 Hz traffic (S1, below) replies arrive 1:1.

  The max RTT of 512 ms is high, but with n = 4 it is not evidence of anything.
  **The storm is NOT the data stall.**  That is worth stating plainly because the
  storm looks alarming and the stall is the user's actual complaint.

9. WHAT THIS DOES TO THE THREE READINGS OF s8.15

  Reading (A) -- "the resync is a poison pill, so a resync should precede a fatal
  with a constant modem reaction gap" -- is now strongly DISFAVOURED: 15 resyncs
  in this boot, only 1 preceded a fatal, and 8+ minutes of storm produced no
  fatal at all with the data plane working.  Readings (B)/(C) -- a failing PC
  handshake REGIME that the resyncs are symptoms of -- remain open and are the
  better fit.  Necessity (3/3 for `a2_power.c:2949`) survives; sufficiency is
  now 3/20 or worse as this boot's denominator grows.

10. S1 -- PRE-REGISTERED, RUNNING: DOES 1 Hz TRAFFIC SUPPRESS THE STORM?

  A lost edge requires `pc_state == 0` and the PC line asserted, i.e. it can only
  happen on the way OUT of a quiesced state.  If that is the mechanism, then
  traffic that keeps the modem awake should SUPPRESS the storm -- and the storm
  and the data stall would be the same defect family (the idle->active handshake)
  seen from two sides.  S1 = 600 consecutive 1 Hz pings with the two counters
  sampled once a second (`/overlay/s1.track`), pre-registered as:
    P5.  resync rate during S1 falls to <= 20 % of the 1-per-36 s pre-S1 rate.
    P5-FALSIFIER.  resync rate stays within 2x of 1-per-36 s during S1.
  Early indication (first ~20 s, NOT a result): resync went 18 -> 20 in 44 s, so
  the storm was NOT immediately suppressed.  If P5 is falsified, the storm is
  independent of quiescence and readings (B)/(C) get stronger still.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 8 -- S1 IS SCORED AND P5 IS CONFIRMED: 1 Hz TRAFFIC SUPPRESSED THE STORM
AND EVERY SUSPEND FOR 364 s -- AND A FATAL FIRED ANYWAY, ON THE CLOCK, INSIDE
THAT WINDOW.  Plus fatal #6, which makes the s8.13 A/B n = 4.
  measured 2026-09-21 18:24:06 -> 18:41 host time, boot a9fd907c, uptime 4844 -> 5503
================================================================================

1. S1 -- PRE-REGISTERED AS P5, AND IT IS CONFIRMED

P5: "the resync rate during S1 falls to <= 20 % of the 1-per-36.3 s pre-S1 rate."
P5-FALSIFIER: "the rate stays within 2x of 1 per 36.3 s during S1."

S1 = 600 consecutive 1 Hz pings (`ping -i 1 -c 600 8.8.8.8`) with
`rx_telemetry` sampled once a second into `/overlay/s1.track` (621 samples).

  traffic window (S1 start 4844.38 -> the SSR killed the ping at 5208.72)
    duration                        364.34 s
    pc_resync_count                 20 -> 20      (0 new resyncs)
    pm_suspend_attempts             636 -> 636    (0 new suspend attempts)
    pc_timeout_count                19 -> 19      (0 new timeouts)
    pc_state=0 samples              0 of 365      (pc_state=1 and quiesce_ms=0 throughout)
    ping replies                    364 of 364    (0 % loss)

  EXPECTED in that window at the pre-S1 rates:
    resyncs  364.34 / 36.3 = 10.0    observed 0     Poisson p(0|10.0) = 4.5e-5
    suspends 364.34 x 0.132 = 48.1   observed 0

  post-traffic window (5209 -> 5502.90, 293.9 s, NO traffic because the ping had died)
    resyncs  20 -> 27  = 7  => 1 per 42.0 s   (pre-S1: 1 per 36.3 s)
    suspends 636 -> 673 = 37 => 0.126 /s      (pre-S1: 0.132 /s)

  **So S1 is a clean A/B/A inside ONE run, and the two arms are the traffic and
  the absence of traffic -- not any change I made.** The storm stopped for
  exactly as long as the traffic ran and came back at the pre-S1 rate the moment
  it stopped.

2. THE CRITICAL READING, BECAUSE THE FIRST GLANCE AT THE TAIL LOOKS LIKE A FAILURE

The final line of `/overlay/s1.track` reads
   5502.90 replies=364 resync=27 pctimeout=24 pc_state=0 quiesce_ms=249 susp=673
i.e. resync 20 -> 27 and susp 636 -> 673.  **Read carelessly that is "the storm
came back, so P5 failed."** It is not: `ping -c 600 -i 1` was **killed at
seq=363** by the fatal's SSR -- the last line of `/overlay/s1.ping` is
`ping: sendto: Network unreachable` with **no summary**, and 364 packets at 1 Hz
from 4844.38 ends at **5208.4**, i.e. 0.3 s before fatal #6 at 5208.721361.  The
SSR re-established the bearer with a NEW IP (10.29.91.236/29 -> 10.132.56.209/30),
so the ping's socket died.  **After that there was no traffic, and the storm
returned.**  The 293.9 s post-traffic arm is the CONTROL arm of the A/B/A.

**Lesson: read the counters AND the instrument's own health before scoring.** A
tail that mixes a suppressed phase and an un-suppressed phase is not one sample.

3. AND THE FATAL FIRED INSIDE THE SUPPRESSION WINDOW, WHICH SEPARATES TWO THINGS

**Fatal #6 at AP 5208.721361 s -- INSIDE the traffic window, while
`pm_suspend_attempts` had been frozen at 636 for 364 s.** So:

  - the STORM  is AP-runtime-PM-GATED      (no suspends => no resyncs, 0/365 samples quiesced)
  - the FATAL  is NOT AP-runtime-PM-gated  (it fired on schedule with the modem never suspended)

**This is the cleanest separation the corpus has between the two, and it kills
the idle-avoidance mitigation for the FATALS while keeping it for the STORM.**

4. FATAL #6 -- AND THE CLOCK FITS TO 19 ms

  [ 5208.721361] fatal error received: lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054:
  [ 5208.871637] MBA booted without debug policy, loading mpss
  [ 5209.728398] remoteproc0: remote processor 4080000.remoteproc is now up

  modem uptime at fatal = 5208.721361 - 4306.434988 (the previous "is now up")
                        = 902.286373 s
  Doc 162's clock figure is 902.267 s of modem uptime (112 ppm, n = 4).
  **902.286373 is 19.4 ms from it.**  And the signature is the one Doc 164's
  short band is ALWAYS paired with (`lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054`).

  Modem-uptime periods for every fatal in this boot (fatal time - previous
  "is now up"):
     #1  900.717032   #2  901.003830   #3  900.981619
     #4  758.672780   #5  826.311383   #6  902.286373
  **The three sleepmgr beats are 900.717 / 901.004 / 900.982 / 902.286 -- three
  of them within 0.29 s of each other and the fourth 1.3 s above.  Recorded as a
  possible second clock mode, NOT concluded (n = 1 for the 902.29 mode in this
  boot, and the modem-uptime figure depends on the `is now up` line, which is a
  different instrument from the fatal line).**

5. FATAL #6 SCORED AGAINST THE s8.13 BAR -- n = 4, AP SURVIVED, 4 of 20

  fatal -> MBA booted windows: 0.824902 / 0.831559 (capture ON)
                               0.125291 / 0.130237 / 0.124531 / 0.127373 (capture OFF)
  four capture-OFF samples now span **0.124531-0.130237 s (spread 5.7 ms)**
  against **0.824902-0.831559 s (spread 6.7 ms)** for the two capture-ON.
  Still two non-overlapping populations, still ~6.6x apart.

  boot-wide: **6 fatals / 7 `MBA booted` (cold boot + one per recovery) /
  2 `port failed halt`** (still exactly the two capture-ON) / trace `01` x8,
  `10` x9 / `cmd_open` 56 = 8 channels x 7 modem boots.  `rproc=running`,
  attr `disabled`, and the ledger row is
  `5218.41,6,lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054,6,6,6,6,6,6,6,6,6,6,6,6,0,1,18`
  -- **t0..t9 all present, and `coredumps` STILL 18** (fatal #6 produced no dump).

  **A CAVEAT THE DATA FORCES: the A/B is on the `SSR before shutdown` ->
  `MBA booted` interval, NOT on fatal -> `is now up`.**  Fatal #5's full
  fatal->up time was 1.443682 s -- as long as the capture-ON recoveries -- even
  though its coredump-sensitive interval was 0.124531 s.  The extra time is the
  mpss load AFTER `MBA booted` and it varies independently.  **Do not use
  fatal->up as the A/B metric; use `SSR before shutdown`->`MBA booted`.**

6. WHAT S1 DOES NOT ESTABLISH

  - It does not show traffic suppresses the FATALS -- it shows the opposite.
  - It does not establish the storm is HARMFUL.  364 pings at 1 Hz through the
    storming regime had **0 % loss**; the storm is visible in the counters, not
    in the data plane.
  - One run, one boot.  The A/B/A is within-run, which is the strongest form
    available, but n = 1 for the suppression event.

================================================================================
PART 9 -- CORRECTION TO PART 7 s5: THE "NO EXTERNAL TRIGGER" CLAIM IS UNSAFE, AND
THE DEVICE HAS A USERSPACE WATCHDOG THAT WRITES THE BAM-DMUX PM KNOBS WITH NO LOG
  found 2026-09-21 18:55 host time, same boot a9fd907c, uptime ~5890
================================================================================

1. WHAT PART 7 s5 CLAIMED, AND WHY IT WAS NOT ENOUGH

Part 7 s5 concluded "the storm has no external trigger" from two negatives:
  (a) the AP's `dmesg` has ZERO lines in 3490.000 -> 4078.999 s (the 589 s before
      the first storm resync), and
  (b) the host's `journalctl -k` is silent (last entry 17:34:12 vs an onset of
      ~18:11:20 host time), with a positive control proving the query works.

**Both negatives are true. Neither is sufficient, because a userspace action can
change the modem's power behaviour WITHOUT producing a kernel log line.** I
checked the kernel and the host; I did not check the DEVICE'S OWN USERSPACE
AUTOMATION. That is the same mistake s8.10 made about `qcom-carrier-autocfg`.

2. WHAT IS ACTUALLY RUNNING: `/usr/sbin/modem-bearer-watchdog` (485 lines)

  uci show modem-watchdog
    modem-watchdog.general.enabled='1'          stall_timeout='60'
    modem-watchdog.general.check_interval='10'  cooldown_seconds='60'
    modem-watchdog.keepalive.enabled='0'        interval='60'  target='openwrt.org'
    modem-watchdog.recovery.ssr_enabled='1'     <-- DEFAULT ON

  And its body (line numbers in the shipped script):

    :111-118  BAM_CTRL=.../4080000.remoteproc:bam-dmux/power/control
              BAM_DELAY=.../4080000.remoteproc:bam-dmux/power/autosuspend_delay_ms
              if [ "$(cat $BAM_CTRL)"  != "auto" ]; then echo auto > $BAM_CTRL; fi
              if [ "$(cat $BAM_DELAY)" != "1000" ]; then echo 1000 > $BAM_DELAY; fi
    :271      Stage 1 recovery = `qmicli ... --wds-go-dormant`  (resets the RRC channel)
    :299,351  Stage 2 recovery = `ubus call network.interface.modem down` / `up`
              -- which WOULD change the bearer IP
    :403-416  Stage 3 recovery = `echo stop > $RPROC_STATE` -- a FULL REMOTEPROC SSR,
              gated on recovery.ssr_enabled, default 1

  **:111-118 is the important one. It ENFORCES `power/control = auto` and
  `autosuspend_delay_ms = 1000` on the bam-dmux device EVERY 10 s, and a sysfs
  write produces NO dmesg line and NO log line.** `autosuspend_delay_ms = 1000`
  means the modem runtime-suspends one second after traffic stops -- and the
  AP-runtime-PM suspend/resume cycle is EXACTLY the mechanism s8.15.4 just showed
  gates the storm. So the one knob that controls the storm's mechanism is
  re-asserted every 10 s by a userspace script that my checks could not see.

  VERIFIED CURRENT STATE (logroll, below): `auto/1000/suspended`. In steady state
  the guards mean no write occurs, so the watchdog is NOT actively changing
  anything -- but it WILL revert a change, and it is the reason the value is
  pinned at 1000 rather than whatever the driver or a DT property chose.

3. THE ONSET IS NOW UNRECOVERABLE, WHICH IS THE REAL PROBLEM

  `logread` is a **~15-minute ring buffer**. At the time of this check its
  earliest line was **12:43:29**, and the storm onset was **~12:27** device time.
  **So the question "did the watchdog act at 4079 s?" can no longer be answered
  for this boot**, and `grep -c` over the buffer for
  `modem-stall-watchdog|modem-keepalive|Stage [123]` returns **0** -- which is
  evidence about the last 15 minutes only, not about the onset.

  **CORRECTED STATEMENT, replacing Part 7 s5:** *no trigger is visible in dmesg or
  on the host; the device additionally runs a watchdog that can write the
  bam-dmux PM knobs, reset the RRC channel, bring the bearer down/up (changing the
  IP) and perform a full remoteproc SSR; its own log is a 15-minute ring buffer
  and has already rotated past the onset.  The trigger question is therefore OPEN,
  not answered.*  The AP-side and host-side negatives stand as far as they go.

4. THE MISSING INSTRUMENT IS NOW DEPLOYED: `/overlay/logroll.sh`

  Written and running (ssh held open), and added to `/etc/rc.local` with a
  pidfile guard so it survives a reboot (backup: `/overlay/rc.local.bak.logroll`,
  `sh -n` verified before the move).  It does two things into ONE file,
  `/overlay/logroll_<boot_id>.log`:
    * `logread -f` filtered of dropbear/authpriv noise, `sync` after every line;
    * a 1 Hz sample of `power/control` + `autosuspend_delay_ms` + `runtime_status`,
      writing a `PMKNOB` line ONLY when the triple changes.

  **It works, and it is already better than what it replaces** -- first lines:
    === BOOT a9fd907c uptime=5895.82 2026-09-21T12:56:37Z ===
    2026-09-21T12:56:37Z uptime=5895.85 PMKNOB auto/1000/suspended (was )
    ... bam_dmux: RX watchdog: PC line asserted while pc_state=0 (lost edge), resyncing
    2026-09-21T12:56:38Z uptime=5897.21 PMKNOB auto/1000/resuming (was auto/1000/suspended)
    2026-09-21T12:56:40Z uptime=5898.48 PMKNOB auto/1000/active   (was auto/1000/resuming)
    2026-09-21T12:56:43Z uptime=5901.57 PMKNOB auto/1000/suspended (was auto/1000/active)

  **It interleaves the userspace log, the kernel log AND the PM state machine on
  one timeline** -- which is what was needed to answer s8.15.3 s5's question, and
  it also gives the s8.13 window a userspace-side attribution instrument it did
  not have (s8.14 had to reconstruct a reboot's cause from the host after the
  fact; this records the device's own side going forward).

  **LIMITATION, stated because it is the same trap twice:** logroll starts NOW, so
  it cannot answer the question for the onset that motivated it.  It is an
  instrument for the NEXT onset, not a retroactive fix.

5. FIRST DATA FROM S2 (still running) -- THE STORM'S COST IS LATENCY, NOT LOSS

  `s2.log` after 14 pings (one per 15 s):
    5872.04 5872.11 rtt=42.807  resync=35/35 pctimeout=32/32
    5887.23 5887.80 rtt=551.417 resync=35/35 pctimeout=33/34   <-- +1 timeout
    5902.88 5902.93 rtt=40.868  resync=36/36 pctimeout=35/35

  **Every ping replied (no loss), but the ONE ping during which a `pc-ack timeout`
  fired took 551.417 ms against a ~41-43 ms baseline.**  The resync delta was 0
  for that ping; the TIMEOUT delta was 1.  So the correct pairing for the
  first-packet-after-idle case is with `pc_timeout_count`, not `pc_resync_count`
  -- which also means **P6 as pre-registered (pair on the resync delta) was the
  wrong predicate**; the analysis must pair on the timeout delta.
  n = 1 so far; S2 runs 80 pings and will be scored on completion.

PART 10 -- FATAL #7 (n=5), THE STORM'S ONSET IS THE BOOT'S FIRST LOST EDGE,
           AND THE ANDROID DRIVER IS THE GROUND TRUTH WE NEVER DIFFED
=============================================================================

1. s8.13 FIFTH SAMPLE -- fatal #7, AP survived, new minimum interval

   Fatal #7: `[ 6110.407029] fatal error received: lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054:`
   (the clock signature).  Predicted at AP 6111.007 from fatal #6 + 902.286;
   observed 6110.407 -- a 600 ms miss over a 902 s period (665 ppm).

     clock delta fatal#6 -> fatal#7 = 6110.407029 - 5208.721361 = 901.685668 s

   Recovery (all lines present, none missing):

     6110.407029  fatal error received
     6110.415183  handling crash #7
     6110.423287  recovering
     6110.430574  SSR before shutdown: scheduling teardown work   <-- t0
     6110.436413  SSR teardown T0 scheduled
     6110.443982  T2 tx_retry cancelled
     6110.451727  T3 tx_wakeup cancelled
     6110.460920  T4 state_lock acquired
     6110.468787  executing serialized asynchronous SSR teardown
     6110.468863  T5 rx released
     6110.477957  T6 tx released
     6110.484535  T7 power_off returned
     6110.491224  T8 work done
     6110.498843  T9 flush returned
     6110.505792  wwan0at0 disconnected   (at1, qmi0 follow)
     6110.507386  q6v5-trace 01..09
     6110.507629  stopped remote processor
     6110.508508  q6v5-trace 10..23
     6110.549744  MBA booted without debug policy, loading mpss  <-- t1
     6110.852136  bam_dmux: modem pc-ack timeout during resume

     t1 - t0 = 6110.549744 - 6110.430574 = **0.119170 s**

   **That is a NEW MINIMUM for the capture-OFF population.**  Previous
   capture-OFF span was 0.124531-0.130237 s (n=4, spread 5.7 ms); this is
   5.4 ms below the old minimum.  The capture-ON population is
   0.824902-0.831559 s.  Still two non-overlapping populations, still ~7x
   apart, now **n = 5 on five signatures in five recoveries**.

   No `port failed halt` -- confirming again that the two `port failed halt`
   lines in this boot are exactly the two capture-ON recoveries.

   Post-SSR recovery artefact, 5th sample: `pc-ack timeout` at
   6110.852136 = **+0.302392 s** after `MBA booted`.  Prior samples:
   +0.431112 / +0.581419 / +0.435022 / none.

   Boot-wide after fatal #7: 7 fatals / 8 `MBA booted` / 2 `port failed halt`
   / trace `01` x8, `10` x9 / `cmd_open` 64 = 8 channels x 8 modem boots /
   `coredump_live` frozen at 18.

   **s8.13 bar: 5 of 20 post-disable fatals scored, 0 AP reboots.**

2. THE STORM'S ONSET -- AP 3477.894835 IS THE FIRST LOST EDGE *OF THE BOOT*

   `dmesg | grep -c "lost edge"` = 39, `pc_resync_count` = 40 (one resync did
   not log).  The FIRST one is

     [ 3477.894835] bam-dmux: RX watchdog: PC line asserted while pc_state=0
                   (lost edge), resyncing

   and `handling crash #4` is at [ 3477.976314] -- **+81.479 ms later**, inside
   the known 74-86 ms `a2_power.c:2949` antecedent band.

   CONTROL, because "the first X in dmesg" is normally a ring-buffer artefact
   (trap 16): `dmesg | wc -l` = 889 and the FIRST line is
   `[ 0.000000] Booting Linux on physical CPU 0x0` -- **the ring did not wrap**.
   `dmesg | grep "handling crash"` shows crashes #1..#8 all present, the
   earliest at [ 913.648811].  So the absence of earlier lost edges is real.

   Therefore, within this boot:

     before AP 3477.894835 :  0 lost edges in 3477 s
     after  AP 3477.894835 : 39 lost edges in 2600 s

   At ~1 suspend per 6.9 s (see s3 below) that is roughly **0 failures in
   ~500 suspend cycles, then ~5% forever**.  P(0 failures in 504 | p=0.05)
   ~ 1e-11, so this is a genuine step change, not a tail.

   **And the storm then survives crashes #5, #6, #7 AND #8** -- four SSRs.
   It is not created by an SSR and not reset by one.  It is a persistent
   AP-side/driver-side state established at AP 3477.89.

   NOTE THE DIRECTION: the first storm event *precedes* fatal #4 by 81 ms.
   So the storm did not follow the fatal; the fatal's own antecedent is the
   storm's first member.  Both are downstream of whatever happened at 3477.89.

3. THE PM CHURN ITSELF IS ANDROID-PARITY -- THE FAILURE RATE IS THE DEFECT

   Telemetry at uptime 6118.48:
     pm_suspend_attempts: 766     pm_resume_attempts: 766
     pc_vote_tx_count: 766        pc_unvote_tx_count: 766
     pc_resync_count: 40          pc_timeout_count: 44
     pc_irq_count: 1514           pc_ack_irq_count: 1526

   So the AP runtime-suspends bam-dmux **766 times in 6118 s = 1 per 8.0 s**
   (instantaneous rate across samples: 1 per 6.9 s).  Each cycle is one
   A2 power vote/unvote pair.

   **Android does 918 modem power-collapses in 54 min = 1 per 3.5 s with 0%
   ping loss and no fatal.**  So *suspending is not the defect*.  The defect
   is that OpenWrt's handshake fails ~5% of the time:

     pc_resync_count / suspend cycles = 40/766 = 5.2%
     pc_timeout_count / suspend cycles = 44/766 = 5.7%

   This is the reframe that makes the Android comparison the right next move.

4. *** THE ANDROID GROUND TRUTH -- AND A SELF-CORRECTION ***

   The Android driver is present in this tree at

     GitIgnore/android_kernel_zte_msm8916/drivers/soc/qcom/bam_dmux.c   (2798 lines)
     GitIgnore/android_kernel_zte_msm8916/drivers/soc/qcom/bam_dmux_private.h (182)

   vs the OpenWrt driver
     openwrt/build_dir/.../linux-6.12.94/drivers/net/wwan/qcom_bam_dmux.c (2700)

   *** SELF-CORRECTION, MADE IMMEDIATELY ***
   My first reading was that Android uses SMSM and OpenWrt uses a GPIO IRQ.
   **That is WRONG.**  The OpenWrt driver is *also* SMSM-based:

     qcom_bam_dmux.c:102-103   struct qcom_smem_state *pc, *pc_ack;
                               u32 pc_mask, pc_ack_mask;
     qcom_bam_dmux.c:268       qcom_smem_state_update_bits(dmux->pc, ...)

   Both sides drive the handshake through shared-memory state bits with an
   IRQ notification.  Recorded here because it is exactly the "read the
   definition, not the name" trap, and I walked into it in the first minute.

   The ORDERING is also equivalent -- both clear the completion, then set the bit:

     Android  ul_wakeup():       INIT_COMPLETION(ul_wakeup_ack_completion);
                                 power_vote(1);
     OpenWrt  bam_dmux_pc_vote(): reinit_completion(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);   :267
                                 qcom_smem_state_update_bits(dmux->pc, ...);      :268

   **FOUR STRUCTURAL DIFFERENCES REMAIN, and they are the deliverable:**

   | | Android (bam_dmux.c) | OpenWrt (qcom_bam_dmux.c) |
   |---|---|---|
   | wakeup-ack wait | `UL_WAKEUP_TIMEOUT_MS = 2000` (:237, used :1913/:1925/:1933) | **250 ms** (:2052) |
   | previous down-ack | `wait_for_ack` flag set in `ul_powerdown()` (:1700), waited in `ul_wakeup()` (:1912-1919) | **absent** |
   | recovery timer | `UL_TIMEOUT_DELAY = 1000` (:235) + `ul_timeout_work` -> `ul_timeout()` (:1776) | **absent** |
   | vote policy | reference-counted, on demand (`ul_ondemand_vote` :1743/:1747; up on TX, down 1 s after last TX) | **unconditional on every runtime-PM resume** |
   | `a2_pc_disabled` path | `wait_for_dfab` + `vote_dfab`/`unvote_dfab` + wakelock (:1697, :1879) | `return -EBUSY` from suspend (:2010) -- never suspends |

   The OpenWrt resume path, for the record (:2043-2060):

     reinit_completion(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);
     mutex_lock(&dmux->state_lock);
     bam_dmux_pc_vote(dmux, true);          /* -> reinit_completion() AGAIN, :267 */
     mutex_unlock(&dmux->state_lock);
     ret = wait_for_completion_timeout(&dmux->pc_ack_completion, 250 ms);
     if (!ret) { atomic64_inc(&dmux->pc_timeout_count);
                 dev_warn("modem pc-ack timeout during resume"); }
     if (!READ_ONCE(dmux->pc_state)) {
             ret = wait_event_timeout(dmux->pc_wait, pc_state, 1000 ms);
             if (!ret) { atomic64_inc(&dmux->pc_timeout_count);
                         dev_warn("modem pc_state wait timeout during resume"); } }

   Note `reinit_completion()` appears TWICE on this path (:2043 and :267).

   **SCOPE THIS HONESTLY.**  OpenWrt *logs* the 250 ms timeout and then
   *proceeds* -- the next step waits for `pc_state` for a further 1000 ms.  So
   the 250 ms timeout may be benign noise.  This is a CANDIDATE with a stated
   falsifier, not a proven root cause:

     FALSIFIER: if the `pc_state wait timeout` string never appears in dmesg
     while `pc_timeout_count` rises, the 250 ms ack wait is the only failing
     wait, and widening it to Android's 2000 ms is the minimal comparative
     test.  If BOTH strings appear, the fix is not a constant.

   TO CHECK: `dmesg | grep -c "pc_state wait timeout"`.

5. NEW SIGNATURE `a2_task.c:3179` -- A CASCADE AT 120.76 s

     [ 6231.169902] fatal error received: a2_task.c:3179:
     [ 6231.176595] handling crash #8

     delta from fatal #7 = 6231.169902 - 6110.407029 = **120.762873 s**

   Far outside the 902 s clock.  **The first non-clock fatal of this boot.**
   `a2_task.c` is a THIRD `a2_*` file (previously only `a2_power.c`; the
   corpus also has `lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c`).

   Its ANTECEDENT IS A NEGATIVE.  The window 6160 -> 6231.17 contains:

     6166.025341  pc-ack timeout
     6168.511911  lost edge
     6168.745439  pc-ack timeout
     6185.251889  pc-ack timeout
     6190.905260  pc-ack timeout

     6218.398418  pc-ack timeout

   Nearest PC event = 12.77 s before, NOT the 74-86 ms band.  So the tight

     sleepmgr                0/3
     a2_power.c:2949         3/3
     a2_power.c:1189         0/1
     a2_task.c:3179          0/1   <-- new

   The device was `runtime_status: active` across it -- consistent with the
   fatal not being PM-gated (s8.15.4).

   STORM DENSITY AROUND IT (telemetry 6118.48 -> 6278.00, dt=159.5 s):

     pc_timeout  44  -> 51    +7   = 30%   (baseline 5.7%)

   **A 6x jump -- but it does NOT survive as a precursor.**  See s7.

6. S2 SCORED (partial, 41/80) -- EVERY LOSS IS AN SSR OUTAGE, NONE IS THE STORM

     LOST at 6115.36   fatal #7 SSR (fatal 6110.407; wwan0 back 6129.51)
     LOST at 6130.44   fatal #7 SSR (ping crossed the ifup)
     LOST at 6226.85   fatal #8 SSR (fatal 6231.17; 5.0 s full timeout)
     LOST at 6246.98   returned in 10 ms with `quiesce_ms=12594`
                       -- impossible for a real timeout, so ping failed
                       instantly: the interface was DOWN

   **All four losses are SSR outages.**  Between SSRs, at 15 s idle, the data
   plane is intact.  The storm's cost is LATENCY: 400-550 ms spikes
   (416.227 / 551.417 / 398.144 / 431.556 ms) against a ~41-43 ms baseline,
   each paired with a `pc_timeout_count` increment.

   P6 PREDICATE, CONFIRMED WRONG AS PRE-REGISTERED: the 551.417 ms ping had

   TIMEOUT delta.**

   PATCH-812 REGRESSION CHECK: PASS.  At uptime 6278:
     tx_defer_queued 771, tx_defer_submitted 771, tx_defer_preserved 751,
     tx_defer_wiped 0, tx_defer_wiped_live 0, tx_sweep_guard_hits 0.
   97.4% preserved, zero destroyed -- over 5 SSRs.

7. NEGATIVE RESULT -- THE STORM RATE DOES NOT PREDICT THE FATAL

   The storm_sampler's 55 s time series (uptime 4345.9 -> 6285.5) was reduced
   to per-sample deltas.  Two things fall out:

   (a) S1 IS INDEPENDENTLY CORROBORATED BY A SECOND INSTRUMENT.  Six
       consecutive samples, 4900.0 -> 5177.6 (~332 s), have
       `dresync=0, dtimeout=0` AND `susp` frozen at 636/625.  S1 ran 1 Hz
       traffic for 364.34 s and froze the PM; a completely separate sampler
       sees the same freeze.

   (b) The pre-fatal-#8 elevation (4 timeouts per 55 s at samples 6119.3 and
       6174.7) is **within the range seen elsewhere in the same series**:
       dtimeout = 3 at 5675.8, 3 at 5897.8, 3 at 6063.9, 4 at 6119.3.
       dresync never exceeds 3; dtimeout never exceeds 4, anywhere.

   **So the elevated rate before fatal #8 is NOT distinctive, and the storm
   rate does NOT predict the fatal.**  Recorded as a negative: the 6x figure
   in s5 is real but is not a precursor signal.  (The 6119.3 sample also
   straddles fatal #7's own SSR, which inflates it.)

8. logroll.sh's FIRST CATCH -- AND ITS INSTRUMENT LIMITATION

   logroll was deployed for exactly this and it worked: it captured the
   userspace side of fatal #8's SSR on one file.

     daemon.err  qcom-time-daemon: [QMI-TIME] Modem SSR / Disconnect detected
                 (node=0 port=11). Resetting state machine.        x14 lines
     daemon.notice ModemManager: [modem7] port 'wwan0qmi0' no longer controllable, reprobing
     daemon.notice netifd: Network device 'wwan0' link is down
     daemon.notice [2593]: [modem8] simple connect state (8/10): bearer
     daemon.notice [2593]: [modem8/bearer18] address: 10.155.30.209/30
     daemon.notice netifd: successfully connected the modem at Bearer/18
     user.notice modem-blue-led: LTE interface up: blue IDLE (1000ms)

   PMKNOB timeline across the fatal:
     13:02:15Z uptime=6233.42  auto/1000/suspended (was active)
     13:02:29Z uptime=6247.96  auto/1000/active    (was suspended)
   **The autosuspend churn resumes ~19 s after the SSR.**

   **It did NOT reveal a userspace trigger.**  No `Stage 1/2/3`, no
   `modem-stall-watchdog`, no `keepalive` line in the window.  So s8.15.5's
   "the trigger question is OPEN" stands -- but one candidate
   (`modem-bearer-watchdog` acting in this window) is now a NEGATIVE observed
   with the instrument in place.

   *** INSTRUMENT LIMITATION -- STATED, NOT HIDDEN ***
   The `logread -f` stream in the file is BATCHED, not interleaved in real
   time with the PMKNOB stream.  In the head of the file, 40 consecutive
   PMKNOB lines (12:56:37 -> 12:59:24) appear BEFORE the kernel lines from
   12:56:38 -> 12:59:00.  **So the PMKNOB stream is real-time and the logread
   stream lags in bursts; the two are NOT on one true timeline in the file.**
   Cross-stream timing (e.g. "the watchdog wrote the knob 3 s before the
   storm") must NOT be read off this file without first re-deriving the
   logread lag from the `[ uptime]` field inside each kernel line, which IS
   authoritative.

=============================================================================
PART 11 -- THE FALSIFIER ANSWERED, THE CASCADE, AND THE 13.24 s RPM VOTE STALL
=============================================================================

1. THE FALSIFIER IS ANSWERED -- AND IT POINTS AT THE 250 ms CONSTANT

   dmesg | grep -c "modem pc-ack timeout during resume"        -> 51
   dmesg | grep -c "modem pc_state wait timeout during resume" ->  2
   dmesg | grep -c "channels not initialized after resume"     ->  2
   pc_timeout_count                                            -> 53 = 51 + 2

   **96.2% of the timeout events are the 250 ms wait for the modem's `pc-ack`
   line, not the 1000 ms `pc_state` wait.**  So the comparative fix (Android's
   `UL_WAKEUP_TIMEOUT_MS = 2000`) is a constant, not a redesign -- and the
   falsifier I stated BEFORE checking is not falsified.

2. THE WIRING, READ OUT OF THE PROBE (qcom_bam_dmux.c:2497-2521)

   dmux->pc_irq = platform_get_irq_byname(pdev, "pc");        /* modem's A2 PC line */
   pc_ack_irq   = platform_get_irq_byname(pdev, "pc-ack");    /* modem's ACK line  */
   dmux->pc     = devm_qcom_smem_state_get(dev, "pc", &bit);      /* AP writes the vote */
   dmux->pc_ack = devm_qcom_smem_state_get(dev, "pc-ack", &bit);  /* AP writes its ack  */
   init_completion(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);
   complete_all(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);                    /* :2521 -- starts COMPLETE */

   and the SMSM decode at :2315-2316:
     "SMEM item 85 is the legacy SMSM shared-state block: word 1 is
      SMSM_MODEM_STATE, bit 1 is SMSM_A2_POWER_CONTROL."

   So `modem pc-ack timeout during resume` means exactly: **the AP voted (wrote
   the `pc` bit) and the modem did not assert its `pc-ack` line within 250 ms.**
   Android gives that same ack 2000 ms.

   Both handlers complete the same completion:
     bam_dmux_pc_irq()     :1977  complete_all(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);
     bam_dmux_pc_ack_irq() :1997  complete_all(&dmux->pc_ack_completion);

3. FATAL #9 -- AND THE CASCADE

   [ 6397.771058] fatal error received: a2_power.c:1189:
   [ 6397.778302] handling crash #9

   delta fatal #8 -> #9 = 6397.778302 - 6231.176595 = 166.601707 s

   Recovery was clean and PATCH 814'S REBUILD FIRED AND SUCCEEDED:

     6397.771058  fatal a2_power.c:1189
     6397.793072  SSR before shutdown            <-- t0
     6397.920651  MBA booted                     <-- t1  => t1-t0 = 0.127579 s
     6398.492369  remote processor is now up
     6398.694059  SSR powerup: modem pc_state=1 (waited 200 ms)
     6398.695965  SSR powerup: successfully reinitialized BAM channels and rings
     6399.041495  wwan0at0 attached
     6399.111817  CMD_OPEN on channels 0..7

   0.127579 s is INSIDE the capture-OFF band (0.119170-0.130237 s), no
   `port failed halt`, AP survived.  **s8.13: 6 of 20 scored, 0 reboots.**
   WARNING: the window now spans TWO regimes (1 idle-clock fatal + 3 cascade
   fatals); that must be stated whenever the bar is quoted.

   FATAL #10:
   [ 6580.507441] fatal error received: a2_task.c:3179:
   [ 6580.514178] handling crash #10
   delta fatal #9 -> #10 = 182.729139 s

   THE FULL SEQUENCE:

     #6  5208.721361  lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054   +903.723
     #7  6110.407029  lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054   +901.686   <-- the 902 s clock
     #8  6231.169902  a2_task.c:3179                +120.763   <-- cascade begins
     #9  6397.771058  a2_power.c:1189               +166.602
     #10 6580.507441  a2_task.c:3179                +182.729

   **After fatal #7 the modem has NOT returned to the 902 s clock**, and
   `a2_task.c:3179` repeats (#8 and #10).  This is a different operating
   regime.

4. *** THE 13.24 s RPM VOTE STALL -- THE STRONGEST MODEM-SIDE EVIDENCE SO FAR ***

   `rpmring -t -n 500000 -i 4` -> /overlay/rpm9.txt, pulled and analysed with
   the tracked `rpm_track.py`:

     blocks=910 records=40709 wall=172.300 s (236.3 rec/s)
     measured RPM tick rate = 19.1998 MHz (vs 19.200 assumed; -0.00%)
     resource names: ldoa 8124, bslv 3788, bmas 2866, req. 1478, clk1 1153,
                     clk0 1127, smpa 1038, clk2 998, clka 856
     deep-sleep / rail resources: NONE

   The normal pattern is a 9-record vote cycle repeating ~26-34 times/s:

     00000144
     000000d0 00000001 000004f8        <- sequence number, increments every cycle
     000000cb                          <- txn-open
     000000d1 00000001 616f646c        <- req, resource "ldoa"
     000000d4 / 000000d5 <same res>    <- res-request
     000000d2 00000001 00716572        <- txn-begin, "req."
     00000143 ffffffff fffff77d
     000000cd                          <- txn-close

   Then, at AP 6469.8 -> 6483.1, the RPM wrote EXACTLY ONE cycle in 13.26 s
   (normally ~350).  THE RPM'S OWN TIMESTAMP PROVES THE RPM WAS ALIVE:

     last record before : ee55a433
     first record after : fd8610b1
     delta = 254266494 ticks / 19.2e6 = 13.243 s
     wall-clock gap                   = 13.264 s

   The two agree to 21 ms.  So the RPM's clock ran while it logged nothing
   => **the stall is UPSTREAM of the RPM, i.e. in the MODEM.**  This is the
   live signature of the RE claim that the Q6 power-collapse vote chain
   stalls in `rpm.sync` (0xc08bebd0), whose RPM flush loops have no timeout --
   and here the stall RECOVERED.

   The AP saw the same event from its side:

     6469.070335  RX watchdog: PC line asserted while pc_state=0 (lost edge), resyncing
     6469.330305  modem pc-ack timeout during resume
     6478.290311  modem pc-ack timeout during resume
     S2 6468.94   rtt = 911.398 ms  (LARGEST RTT of the whole S2 run; prior max 551.417)

   One modem stall, seen as a 13.24 s RPM silence AND a 911 ms delayed ping --
   two independent instruments, one event.

   *** WHAT THIS DOES NOT ESTABLISH ***
   The capture spans ONE stall, and it happened AFTER fatal #9.  **At fatal #9
   itself the RPM did NOT go silent** -- the gaps around 6397.8 were
   0.35-0.59 s (the RPM was BUSY, logging the SSR).  So it is NOT established
   that this stall is the 902 s fatal's mechanism.  One sample each way.
   The capture is still running across fatal #11; the discriminating question
   is: does a 13 s silence PRECEDE a fatal, or is it an independent recurring
   stall?

   Multi-second RPM silences are not rare in this capture: 7.076, 3.890, 3.620,
   2.529, 2.748, 3.020, 3.233, 4.241, 3.079, 5.794, 13.264, 3.086, 2.303,
   3.230 s.  So the modem regularly stops voting for seconds at a time.

5. S2 FINAL -- 56 SAMPLES, 7 LOSSES, TWO WITH NO dmesg CORRELATE AT ALL

     6115.36, 6130.44  fatal #7's SSR
     6226.85, 6246.98  fatal #8's SSR (one in flight, one interface-down)
     6368.31, 6388.34  *** NO dmesg CORRELATE -- silent data-plane loss ***
     6408.39           fatal #9's SSR (returned in 10 ms, quiesce_ms=7404)

   dmesg 6360-6397 contains NOTHING but the fatal itself -- no lost edge, no
   pc-ack timeout.  So the two unexplained losses are NOT storm events and NOT
   SSR outages: the data plane was silently dropping packets while the modem
   was already in the cascade.  **A new, unexplained failure mode, n = 2.**

   Marker appended: "S2 STOP at uptime=6600.88 fatals=10"

6. THE CORRECTION TO MY OWN s8.17 NEGATIVE

   I first dismissed the pre-fatal-#8 elevation as "within range".  Two errors:
   (i) the baseline 40/766 = 5.2% is a LIFETIME average, diluted by the 3477 s
   before the storm existed; the per-sample rate is dresync 1-3 against
   susp 7-9 per 55 s = ~15-40%.  (ii) `dtimeout = 4` at 6119.3 and 6174.7 IS
   the maximum of the whole 33-sample series and is first reached exactly in
   the two samples before fatal #8.
   Corrected statement: the post-onset failure rate is ~15-40%, not 5%; the
   pre-fatal window sits at the top of the observed range but is NOT separated
   from it.  NOT established as a precursor.

7. THE CASCADE IS BOUNDED -- 3 BEATS -- AND THE MODEM RETURNS TO THE CLOCK.
   MY FIRST READING OF THIS EXPERIMENT WAS CONFOUNDED AND IS WITHDRAWN.

   MEASUREMENT THAT SETTLES IT:

     [ 7483.839750] fatal error received: lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054:
     [ 7483.847914] handling crash #11

     delta fatal #10 -> #11 = 7483.839750 - 6580.507441 = 903.332309 s
     predicted from fatal #10's modem boot: AP ~7482.8 s  => miss 1.04 s
     signature = the CLOCK one.

   Recovery: `SSR before shutdown` 7483.863268 -> `MBA booted` 7483.983180
             = 0.119912 s (inside the capture-OFF band 0.119170-0.130237 s)
             no `port failed halt`; patch 814's rebuild fired
             (`successfully reinitialized BAM channels and rings`)
             AP survived.
   => s8.13 bar: 7 of 20 scored, 0 AP reboots.
      boot-wide: 11 crashes / 12 MBA booted / 2 port failed halt /
                 cmd_open 96 = 8 x 12.

   *** SO THE CASCADE SELF-TERMINATED AFTER THREE BEATS AND THE MODEM WENT
   BACK TO THE 902 s CLOCK. ***  Operationally this matters: the cascade is
   NOT a permanent degradation.  And it means the "902 s clock" is really
   "902 s of MODEM UPTIME" -- a cascade is a bounded departure from it, and
   the next reload restarts the clock.

   *** THE CONFOUND -- MY OWN ERROR, WITHDRAWN ***
   I stopped the traffic at uptime 6600.88 and, seeing 0 fatals in the next
   434.6 s against cascade intervals of 120.8/166.6/182.7 s, wrote that "the
   cascade is traffic-dependent" with P ~ 6%.
   ** THE CASCADE'S LAST FATAL WAS #10 AT AP 6580.507441 -- 20.4 s BEFORE I
   REMOVED THE TRAFFIC. **  The intervention was applied AFTER the event it
   claimed to explain.  The claim is WITHDRAWN.  (Trap 19.)

   WHAT SURVIVES, AND IS STILL WORTH HAVING -- the storm ran at FULL RATE
   across the 434.6 s of no-traffic no-fatal:

     6600.88 -> 7035.44 (434.6 s), NO traffic:
       pc_resync_count      60 -> 70   (+10 = 1 per 30.6 s)
       pc_timeout_count     63 -> 70   (+7)
       pm_suspend_attempts 840 -> 879  (+39 = 1 per 7.8 s)
       => 26% resync and 18% timeout failure rates
          (the post-onset band, NOT the 5.2% lifetime figure)
       25 lost-edge / pc-ack lines in dmesg since 6600

   => the storm is NOT SUFFICIENT for a fatal.  Same conclusion as s8.15.4
   reached, now with 434.6 s of concurrent observation instead of a 364 s
   window that happened to contain one.

   *** THE ONE THING STILL NOT DISTINGUISHABLE ***
   The cascade's 4th beat would have been due at AP ~6580.5 + ~199 ~ 6779.5 s,
   and traffic was removed at 6600.9 -- 174 s BEFORE that.  So
   "the cascade ended naturally at #10" and "the traffic removal suppressed a
   4th beat" are BOTH consistent with everything observed.  (The clock origin
   being fatal #10's boot does NOT discriminate: with a suppressed beat there
   would be no reload to move the origin.)
   STATED, NOT RESOLVED.  Distinguishing them needs a run where the traffic is
   removed BEFORE a cascade starts.

   *** The RPM capture must not be stopped: the ring turns over in ~6 s. ***
