PART 12 -- THE RPM STALL DOES NOT PRECEDE A FATAL, AND THE STORM IS BOUNDED
           (added 2026-09-21 ~19:10 local / 13:25 UTC, boot a9fd907c, AP 8156.67 s)
================================================================================

This part closes the question PART 11 left open and records a second bounded
episode.  Both are negatives/limits, and both were obtained by leaving an
instrument RUNNING past the point where it had already produced a result.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. PROVENANCE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Device capture: /overlay/rpm9.txt   (rpmring -t -n 500000 -i 4, pid 14908)
   Host snapshot : scratch/rpm11/rpm11.txt   (scp'd at AP ~7782 s)

     $ wc -l scratch/rpm11/rpm11.txt
     431383
     $ head -2 scratch/rpm11/rpm11.txt
     RPM9 START uptime=6343.17 fatals=8
     # rpmring ring=0x29dc58 words=2048 hdr=0x29dc00 hwords=22 samples=500000 interval=4ms
     # TRACK start t=6343208927874 counter=0x03ccdae0 pos=215

   Analyser: the corpus tool, unchanged
     Docs/Modem Stability/evidence/150_rpm_track/rpm_track.py

     blocks=7627 records=423704 wall=1451.045 s  (292.0 rec/s)
     measured RPM tick rate = 19.1997 MHz (vs 19.200 MHz assumed; -0.00%)

   Two purpose-written helpers (new, kept in scratch/rpm11/):
     analyze.py   -- quiet periods on the absolute AP timeline, fatals overlaid
     enrich.py    -- time-fraction vs event-fraction enrichment test

   The capture covers AP 6343.30 -> 7794.35 s, i.e. fatal #9 (6397.771),
   fatal #10 (6580.507) and fatal #11 (7483.840).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
2. THE ANSWER -- NO
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ python3 scratch/rpm11/analyze.py scratch/rpm11/rpm11.txt | tail -5

   === fatals vs nearest preceding quiet period >= 1 s ===
     AP   6397.771  #9  a2_power.c:1189              nearest quiet   1.268 s
                     at AP 6396.092 ( +1.679 s before fatal)
     AP   6580.507  #10 a2_task.c:3179               nearest quiet   1.145 s
                     at AP 6580.291 ( +0.216 s before fatal)
     AP   7483.840  #11 lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054  nearest quiet   3.141 s
                     at AP 7476.482 ( +7.358 s before fatal)

   *** ALL THREE ARE THE NORMAL CADENCE FOR THEIR ERA ***
   (the RPM's inter-burst gap is ~1.267 s around 6390, ~1.12 s around 6580,
    and 2.3-3.6 s around 7480 -- see s4 below)

   The three gaps >= 8 s in the whole 1451 s, and what is inside them:

     $ awk 'NR>3 && $3+0>=8' scratch/rpm11/quiet.txt
       6469.802   6483.065   13.264        288      9
       6563.623   6572.133    8.510        288      9
       7044.160   7055.040   10.880        992     31

     $ python3 scratch/rpm11/enrich.py ... (s5 of its output)
     the three gaps >= 8 s:
       6469.802 -> 6483.065  13.264 s  (9 recs)
         AP events inside: ['6478.290 pc-ack timeout']
       6563.623 -> 6572.133   8.510 s  (9 recs)
         AP events inside: ['6563.800 lost edge', '6564.023 pc-ack timeout',
                            '6566.210 pc-ack timeout']
       7044.160 -> 7055.040  10.880 s  (31 recs)
         AP events inside: ['7044.180 lost edge', '7052.390 pc-ack timeout']

   => NONE is immediately before a fatal.  The 13.264 s stall sits 72 s AFTER
      fatal #9 and 97 s BEFORE fatal #10.  All three RECOVER, and the modem ran
      on for a further 97 / 8.4 / 428 s without a fatal.

   Fatal #11 itself fires at the END of a 2.504 s gap (7481.353 -> 7483.857),
   which is BELOW that era's cadence:

     $ awk 'NR>3 && $2+0>=7440 && $2+0<=7500' scratch/rpm11/quiet.txt
       7441.369   7444.446    3.078       2016     63
       7449.379   7452.586    3.207        480     15
       7455.052   7455.791    0.740      13216     94  delta>=ring
       7457.343   7459.616    2.273        288      9
       7459.641   7462.175    2.535         96      3
       7465.384   7468.584    3.200       2016     63
       7473.341   7476.482    3.141       2016     63
       7481.353   7483.857    2.504       1408     44   <-- fatal #11 fires HERE
       7483.857   7484.426    0.570        192      6
       7488.112   7488.810    0.698       1728     54

   *** CONCLUSION: THE 13.24 s RPM VOTE STALL IS AN INDEPENDENT, RECURRING,
       SELF-RECOVERING MODEM-SIDE EVENT (~1 per 480 s in this capture).  IT IS
       NOT THE FATAL'S MECHANISM. ***
   PART 11's "one sample each way" is now three samples, all pointing the same
   way, and it confirms the scoping PART 11 already claimed: the stall is a
   LATENCY/RECOVERY defect (like the s8.16 250 ms handshake), not a stability one.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
3. WHY "QUIET PERIOD" MUST NOT BE READ AS "STALL" -- THE LOG IS BURSTY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   This is the trap that would have manufactured a false positive.

   The RPM does not write at a steady rate; it writes in BURSTS, and the
   inter-burst gap CHANGES over the capture:

     AP 6343-6352   ~470-830 records/s        (near-continuous)
     AP 6373-6397   9 records per 1.267 s     (one 9-record vote cycle at a time)
     AP 6615+       63 records per ~3.1 s     (seven vote cycles at a time)

   From rpm_track.py's per-second histogram the rate is 470-830 rec/s in the
   first ten seconds and then falls by 25-40x for the rest of the capture.

   $ awk 'NR>3' scratch/rpm11/quiet.txt | awk '{s+=$3} END {print s}'
   (sum of all gaps >= 500 ms) / 1451.0 = 0.5046
   *** 50.5 % OF THE ENTIRE CAPTURE IS INSIDE A GAP >= 0.5 s ***

   *** THE DISCARDED FIRST ANALYSIS, RECORDED SO IT IS NOT REPEATED ***
   My first pass divided each gap by the median of ALL consecutive gaps.  Most
   consecutive "# NEW" blocks are ~4 ms apart, so that median is ~0.03 s, and
   every >= 500 ms gap scored 30-400x -- i.e. it "found" a stall in almost every
   second of the capture.  It was discarded.  (Trap 20.)
   The correct baseline is the LOCAL cadence, and it must be re-measured per era.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
4. A MODEST, REAL ENRICHMENT -- AND THE DIRECTION IS NOT ESTABLISHED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ python3 scratch/rpm11/enrich.py scratch/rpm11/rpm11.txt \
                                   scratch/rpm11/events.txt

   capture 6343.30..7794.35 = 1451.0 s; 60 of 159 events in capture

     T(s)  time_frac  event_frac   enrich  #events  #gaps
      0.5     0.5046      0.7833     1.55       47    325
      1.0     0.4556      0.6333     1.39       38    231
      2.0     0.3890      0.5667     1.46       34    161
      3.0     0.3256      0.5167     1.59       31    123
      5.0     0.0610      0.1500     2.46        9     12
      8.0     0.0225      0.1000     4.44        6      3

   per-kind placement in gaps >= 2 s:
     lost edge               18/35  =  51.4%
     pc-ack timeout          16/25  =  64.0%

   => the AP's handshake events DO cluster in RPM gaps, but only modestly at
      ordinary thresholds.  The 4.44x at >= 8 s rests on 6 events in 3 gaps:
      SUGGESTIVE, NOT ESTABLISHED.

   *** DIRECTION IS NOT ESTABLISHED. ***
   Two readings fit equally: (a) the RPM stall delays the modem's PC-ack, which
   the AP then reports as a timeout and a lost edge; or (b) the AP's PC-vote
   churn is what the RPM stalls ON.  The events inside the three stalls are not
   all at the same phase -- 6563.800 and 7044.180 sit 0.02-0.18 s AFTER the
   stall opens, while 6478.290 and 7052.390 sit 8.2-8.5 s INTO it -- which does
   not discriminate.  STATED, NOT RESOLVED.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
5. CAPTURE-QUALITY CAVEAT -- 49 RING OVERRUNS, 22 664 RECORDS LOST
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   rpmring emits a "# TRACK OVERRUN" line whenever the RPM writes more than the
   8 KiB ring between its 4 ms polls:

     # TRACK OVERRUN t=6399122970069 counter=0x03d0ee00 delta=54592 bytes (1706 records > ring)
     # TRACK OVERRUN t=6403326082862 counter=0x03d35c20 delta=14976 bytes (468 records > ring)
     # TRACK OVERRUN t=6403428791198 counter=0x03d39740 delta=11296 bytes (353 records > ring)
     ... 49 such events, 22 664 records total

   Those are windows where the RPM was writing FURIOUSLY (a fatal's SSR teardown)
   and the tool could not keep up.  They do NOT affect the negative in s2 -- an
   overrun is the OPPOSITE of a silence -- but the capture is therefore NOT
   perfectly lossless, and any future "the RPM wrote nothing here" claim must be
   checked against BOTH the # TRACK OVERRUN line AND the counter delta:
   *** a gap whose counter delta is >= 8192 B is a ring turnover, not a silence. ***

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
6. THE STORM IS BOUNDED TOO -- AND A MODEM RELOAD DID NOT RESTART IT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The storm's onset was AP 3477.894835 (PART 10).  The sampler's last samples:

     7394.0  resync= 79 (d+1)  pctimeout= 78 (d+0)  susp=924/890  fatals=10
     7449.4  resync= 81 (d+2)  pctimeout= 78 (d+0)  susp=931/897  fatals=10
     7504.7  resync= 81 (d+0)  pctimeout= 78 (d+0)  susp=936/902  fatals=11
     7560.0  resync= 81 (d+0)  pctimeout= 78 (d+0)  susp=943/909  fatals=11
     7615.3  resync= 81 (d+0)  pctimeout= 78 (d+0)  susp=949/915  fatals=11

   The last lost-edge line in dmesg:
     [ 7417.446456] ... RX watchdog: PC line asserted while pc_state=0 (lost edge), resyncing

   Live verification at uptime 8156.67:
     $ cat /sys/devices/platform/soc@0/4080000.remoteproc/4080000.remoteproc:bam-dmux/rx_telemetry
     pc_resync_count: 81          <- FROZEN (identical at 7449.4 and at 8156.67)
     pc_timeout_count: 78         <- FROZEN
     pm_suspend_attempts: 1018    <- CLIMBING (924 at 7394.0; +94 across the silence)
     runtime_status: active
     $ cat /sys/class/remoteproc/remoteproc0/state
     running
     $ dmesg | grep -c "fatal error received"
     11                            <- fatal #11 (7483.840) came AND went inside it

   *** STORM EPISODE: AP 3477.894835 -> 7417.446456 = 3939.55 s ***
   *** THEN 739 s SILENT ACROSS ~94 SUSPENDS, INCLUDING ACROSS FATAL #11's
       FULL MODEM RELOAD (ports re-attached 7488.1, 8 CMD_OPENs, runtime active,
       ZERO resyncs). ***

   *** THIS REFUTES "THE STORM IS AP-runtime-PM-GATED" AS s8.15.4 STATED IT. ***
   S1 froze pc_resync_count AND pm_suspend_attempts together and inferred the
   storm is gated by AP runtime-PM.  But freezing both is EQUALLY consistent
   with "traffic suppresses suspends" and "traffic suppresses resyncs" -- the
   inference was never tested in the other direction.  This tests it: the AP
   suspends 94 more times with ZERO resyncs.
   => THE CHURN IS NECESSARY BUT NOT SUFFICIENT.  The gating is NOT simply
      "a suspend can produce a resync".

   WHAT s8.15.4 ESTABLISHED AND STILL STANDS:
     - 1 Hz traffic suppresses the storm in practice (the S1 A/B/A is intact);
     - idle-avoidance does NOT suppress the FATALS (fatal #6 fired inside S1's
       window with pm_suspend_attempts frozen; fatal #11 fired inside this one).

   WHAT IS OPEN INSTEAD -- the storm needs a THIRD condition:
     (a) the modem's own state -- but the storm began 81.5 ms before crash #4
         and SURVIVED four SSRs, so it is not purely per-modem-boot; and fatal
         #11's reload did not restart it;
     (b) the cascade regime -- but the storm ran 836.9 s PAST the cascade's last
         fatal (#10, 6580.507);
     (c) a specific PATTERN of suspends rather than their rate -- UNTESTED, and
         the natural next instrument (log WHICH suspends are followed by a
         resync, not how many).

   *** A 739 s SILENCE IS NOT PROOF THE STORM IS OVER. ***
   A lull cannot be distinguished from an end until a further onset is observed.
   The storm_sampler and logroll.sh are both still running and will catch it.
   What the silence DOES prove is that the churn rate alone does not sustain it.

7. CORRECTION -- S2 IS 58 SAMPLES / 9 LOSSES, AND THE SILENT CLASS IS n = 3
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ grep -c "rtt="      /overlay/s2.log   ->  58
   $ grep -c "rtt=LOST"  /overlay/s2.log   ->  9

   The earlier "56 samples, 7 losses ... n = 2" was wrong on BOTH figures.  Two
   LOST lines (6576.27, 6596.37) were written after the scoring, and a third
   loss (6130.44) was misclassified.

     uptime    wall    class             cause
     6115.36  0.01 s  interface DOWN    fatal #7's SSR -- ports attached 6112.87
     6130.44  5.03 s  SILENT            17.6 s AFTER the SSR completed
     6226.85  5.05 s  pre-fatal         4.3 s before fatal #8; pc-ack 6218.398
     6246.98  0.01 s  interface DOWN    fatal #8's SSR (quiesce_ms=12594)
     6368.31  5.00 s  SILENT            no dmesg correlate within +/-19 s
     6388.34  5.01 s  SILENT            no dmesg correlate within +/-19 s
     6408.39  0.01 s  interface DOWN    fatal #9's SSR (quiesce_ms=7404)
     6576.27  5.01 s  pre-fatal         4.2 s before fatal #10; pc-ack 6566.210
     6596.37  0.01 s  interface DOWN    fatal #10's SSR (quiesce_ms=12055)

   The class is read off the wall time, not guessed: a loss is "interface DOWN"
   when t_end - t_start ~ 10 ms, because `ping -c 1 -W 5` CANNOT time out in
   10 ms -- the ICMP failed on the spot because the interface was down.
   quiesce_ms is non-zero on EXACTLY those four samples (431 / 12594 / 7404 /
   12055) and zero on every other loss.  A genuine timeout takes the full ~5 s.

   *** s8.18's CLASSIFICATION OF 6130.44 IS REFUTED. ***
   s8.18 recorded it as "ping crossed the ifup".  The dmesg timeline:
     [ 6111.869030] modem pc_state wait timeout during resume
     [ 6111.869113] channels not initialized after resume
     [ 6111.927153] remoteproc remoteproc0: remote processor ... is now up
     [ 6112.389773] wwan wwan0: port wwan0at0 attached
     [ 6112.393756] wwan wwan0: port wwan0at1 attached
     [ 6112.869333] wwan wwan0: port wwan0qmi0 attached
   The ping fired at 6130.44 -- 17.6 s AFTER the interface was back -- and then
   timed out for the full 5.03 s.  Nothing in dmesg within +/-19 s: the nearest
   handshake event is 6111.869 (18.6 s before) and the nearest after is
   6150.125 lost edge (19.7 s later).
   => same class as 6368.31/6388.34: a REAL 5 s data-plane timeout with NOTHING
      in dmesg -- no lost edge, no pc-ack timeout, no fatal, no SSR.
   *** A NEW, UNEXPLAINED FAILURE MODE, n = 3. ***

   COUNTER ACCOUNTING IS NOW EXACT (s8.16's falsifier, re-measured on the full ring):
     pc_resync_count  81  ==  dmesg "lost edge"                 81
     pc_timeout_count 78  ==  dmesg "pc-ack timeout" 76
                            + dmesg "pc_state wait timeout"  2   = 78  (100.0 %)
   s8.16 recorded 51 + 2 = 53 (96.2 %).  The two missing pc-ack lines were RING
   INCOMPLETENESS at scoring time, not a second mechanism.  The falsifier
   survives at 100 %.

================================================================================
PART 14 -- CORRECTION: THE RPM CAPTURE WAS **NOT** RUNNING WHILE PART 12 WAS
           WRITTEN, AND `nohup` DOES NOT EXIST ON THIS DEVICE
           (added 2026-09-21 ~19:45 local / 13:45 UTC, boot a9fd907c, AP ~8660 s)
================================================================================

Part 12 section 8 said "LEAVE THE RPM CAPTURE RUNNING", and the Doc 170 section 10
bullet said "the capture is still running and should stay running".  *** BOTH
WERE WRONG: THE CAPTURE HAD ALREADY EXITED. ***  I asserted it instead of
checking it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. HOW IT ENDED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ ps w | grep "[r]pmring"          -> (nothing)
   $ ls -l /overlay/rpm9.txt
   -rw-r--r--    1 root     root      49722962 Sep 21 13:40 /overlay/rpm9.txt
   $ grep -c "^R " /overlay/rpm9.txt  ->  660382
   $ tail -4 /overlay/rpm9.txt
   R 00200000 ff53e444 00000025 000000db 00000001 616f646c 00000012 0000042d
   R 00200000 ff53e49b 00000025 000000d2 00000001 00716572 00000000 00000000
   R 00200000 ff53e856 00000025 000000ff 00000143 ffffffff fffff77d 00000000 00000000
   R 00200000 ff53e912 00000025 000000cd 00000000 00000000 00000000 00000000

   The last line is a COMPLETE 8-word record closing a vote cycle (0xcd
   txn-close), so the run exited CLEANLY at its sample limit -- it did not crash.

   *** THE UNITS TRAP: `-n` COUNTS POLLS, NOT RECORDS. ***
   The header reads `samples=500000 interval=4ms`.  At ~4.5 ms per poll that is
   ~2250 s of wall time; the capture started at AP 6343.17 and the file stops at
   ~AP 8600 s.  So "500 000" was a poll budget, and the run ended roughly 2.3x
   sooner than a reader would guess from "500 000 records" (it wrote 660 382
   records).  *** Read the tool's own units before reasoning about its lifetime. ***

   Consequence: the negative in Part 12 section 2 is UNAFFECTED -- all three
   fatals (#9, #10, #11) and all three >= 8 s stalls were inside the captured
   window, which is what the claim rests on.  What was wrong was only the
   forward-looking statement about the instrument's availability.

   *** CONFIRMED AT THE TASK LEVEL (not inferred from the file) ***
   The launcher was still a live background task, and it reported its exit:

     Command : ssh root@192.168.8.1 'echo "RPM9 START uptime=$(...) fatals=$(...)"
               > /overlay/rpm9.txt; exec /overlay/rpmring -t -n 500000 -i 4
               >> /overlay/rpm9.txt 2>&1'
     Status  : completed
     Duration: 35m 57s = 2157 s
     Stdout  : (no output)      Stderr: (no output)

   The arithmetic closes: the header reads `uptime=6343.17`, which is
   13:04:05 UTC; + 2157 s = 13:40:02 UTC, and `ls -l /overlay/rpm9.txt` reports
   mtime **13:40** -- to the minute.  So the run ended on its POLL BUDGET
   (2157 s / 500 000 polls = **4.31 ms per poll**, against the `-i 4` nominal),
   with **no error on either stream**.  It did not crash, and it was not killed.
   Note also that this launcher was an ssh session held open as a background
   task -- i.e. it was NOT daemonised, which is a second reason it could not
   outlive its budget.  The replacement IS daemonised (`start-stop-daemon -b`),
   and it has been re-verified alive and growing after a later ssh session
   exited: pid 25898, `/overlay/rpm10.txt` 2 313 080 -> 2 663 140 bytes.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
2. RESTART -- AND A DEVICE QUIRK THAT MADE THE FIRST ATTEMPT SILENT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   First attempt (FAILED, and it failed SILENTLY):

     $ setsid nohup /overlay/rpm10_launch.sh < /dev/null > /dev/null 2>&1 &
     $ sleep 4; ps w | grep "[r]pmring"     -> (nothing)
     $ head -4 /overlay/rpm10.txt
     head: /overlay/rpm10.txt: No such file or directory

   Diagnosis:

     $ which nohup   -> (nothing)      *** nohup DOES NOT EXIST ON THIS DEVICE ***
     $ which setsid  -> /usr/bin/setsid
     $ which start-stop-daemon -> /sbin/start-stop-daemon

   The script itself was fine -- run in the FOREGROUND it wrote 51 452 bytes in
   3 s.  The failure was purely the launcher.

   Working recipe:

     $ start-stop-daemon -S -b -x /overlay/rpm10_launch.sh
     $ sleep 5; ps w | grep "[r]pmring"
     25898 root       240 S    /overlay/rpmring -t -n 2000000 -i 4
     $ ls -l /overlay/rpm10.txt
     -rw-r--r--    1 root     root        134601 Sep 21 13:42 /overlay/rpm10.txt
     $ head -2 /overlay/rpm10.txt
     RPM10 START uptime=8650.88 fatals=12
     # rpmring ring=0x29dc58 words=2048 hdr=0x29dc00 hwords=22 samples=2000000 interval=4ms

   *** A FAILED BACKGROUND LAUNCH HERE CREATES NO OUTPUT FILE AT ALL, WHICH IS
       INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM "THE EXPERIMENT RAN AND PRODUCED NOTHING". ***
   Confirm a launch by seeing BOTH the process in `ps` AND the output file
   growing.  (`-n 2000000` at ~4.5 ms/poll is ~2.5 h and ~200 MB; /overlay had
   1.4 G free, 55 % used.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
3. WHAT WAS CHECKED, SO THE REST OF THE CLAIM IS NOT ALSO AN ASSUMPTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Device-side, `ps w` at AP ~8665:

     logroll.sh          RUNNING  (2 procs, 24478 / 24484)
     beacon.sh           RUNNING
     dmesg_roll.sh       RUNNING
     ssr_ledger.sh       RUNNING
     q6trace_soak.sh     RUNNING
     rpmring             RUNNING  (restarted, pid 25898)

   Host-side:

     $ pgrep -af storm_sampler
     1183644 bash scratch/storm_sampler.sh 60
     $ tail -1 /tmp/storm_sampler2.log
     2026-09-21T19:27:10+05:45 up=8628.96 fatals=12 resync=81 lostedge=81
       pctimeout=79 quiesce_ms=657 pc_state=0 susp=1076/1042

   So the Doc 170 section 10 statement "the `storm_sampler` and `logroll.sh` are
   both still running" IS correct; only the RPM-capture claim was false.
   The sampler independently confirms the silence: `resync=81` flat while
   `susp` climbs 1043 -> 1076.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
4. THE STANDING RULE THIS PRODUCES (Doc 170 trap 22)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   * **"Still running" is an observation, not an assumption.** `ps w | grep
     "[r]pmring"` costs one round trip.  A long-lived instrument FEELS like a
     fact, which is exactly why it gets asserted unchecked.
   * **Read the tool's units.** `-n 500000` was polls, not records.
   * **On this device use `start-stop-daemon -S -b -x <script>`** (or `setsid`);
     `nohup` is absent, and its absence produces a silent no-op.
   * Confirm any launch with **both** `ps` and a growing output file.





================================================================================
PART 15 -- THE ONE POST-STORM COUNTER INCREMENT IS A THIRD CLASS OF
           `pc-ack timeout`, AND IT IS INEVITABLE (Doc 170 section 8.21.1)
================================================================================

1. THE OBSERVATION THAT STARTED IT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Doc 170 section 8.21 stated "pc_resync_count 81 and pc_timeout_count 78 both
   FROZE".  Re-read at uptime 8798.27:

     $ ssh root@192.168.8.1 'cat .../bam-dmux/rx_telemetry' | grep -E "resync|timeout|suspend"
     pc_resync_count: 81
     pc_timeout_count: 79          <-- MOVED (78 -> 79)
     pm_suspend_attempts: 1097

   The host sampler had already caught it and its log line is the witness:

     $ tail -1 /tmp/storm_sampler2.log
     2026-09-21T19:27:10+05:45 up=8628.96 fatals=12 resync=81 lostedge=81
       pctimeout=79 quiesce_ms=657 pc_state=0 susp=1076/1042

   So the increment happened between the uptime-8156.67 reading (78) and
   up=8628.96 (79) -- i.e. at or after fatal #12 (AP 8385.263928).

2. IT IS THE ONLY NEW HANDSHAKE EVENT, AND IT IS POST-FATAL
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ dmesg | grep -E "^\[ *838[0-9]\." | grep -vE "q6v5-trace|CMD_OPEN"

   [ 8385.263928] qcom-q6v5-mss: fatal error received: lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054:
   [ 8385.264036] remoteproc remoteproc0: crash detected ... type fatal error
   [ 8385.272813] remoteproc remoteproc0: handling crash #12
   [ 8385.287675] bam-dmux: SSR before shutdown: scheduling teardown work
   ...
   [ 8385.341150] bam-dmux: bam-dmux T7 power_off returned
   [ 8385.363705] wwan wwan0: port wwan0at0 disconnected
   [ 8385.365728] remoteproc remoteproc0: stopped remote processor 4080000.remoteproc
   [ 8385.410626] qcom-q6v5-mss: MBA booted without debug policy, loading mpss
   [ 8385.704199] bam-dmux: modem pc-ack timeout during resume      <== THE INCREMENT
   [ 8385.966467] bam-dmux: SSR after powerup: scheduling powerup work
   [ 8385.966522] remoteproc remoteproc0: remote processor ... is now up
   [ 8386.170598] bam-dmux: SSR powerup: modem pc_state=1 (waited 200 ms)
   [ 8386.407207] wwan wwan0: port wwan0at0 attached
   ...
   [ 8386.601333] bam-dmux: received CMD_OPEN (1) on channel 0
   ... (8 CMD_OPENs)

   The timeout sits BETWEEN "stopped remote processor" and "is now up".

3. THE ACCOUNTING IS EXACT AGAIN AT THE NEW VALUE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Source: openwrt/build_dir/.../drivers/net/wwan/qcom_bam_dmux.c
   pc_timeout_count has exactly TWO increment sites, both in
   bam_dmux_runtime_resume():

     :2066  ret = wait_for_completion_timeout(&dmux->pc_ack_completion,
     :2067                                    msecs_to_jiffies(250));
     :2068  if (!ret) {
     :2069          atomic64_inc(&dmux->pc_timeout_count);
     :2070          dev_warn(dev, "bam_dmux: modem pc-ack timeout during resume\n");

     :2074  if (!READ_ONCE(dmux->pc_state)) {
     :2075          ret = wait_event_timeout(dmux->pc_wait, ..., msecs_to_jiffies(1000));
     :2077          if (!ret || !READ_ONCE(dmux->pc_state)) {
     :2078                  atomic64_inc(&dmux->pc_timeout_count);
     :2079                  dev_warn(dev, "... pc_state wait timeout during resume\n");

   Measured on the full ring (dmesg is NOT wrapped -- its first line is
   "[    0.000000] Booting Linux on physical CPU ..."):

     $ dmesg | grep -c "pc-ack timeout"                 -> 77
     $ dmesg | grep -c "pc_state wait timeout"          ->  2
     $ cat rx_telemetry | grep pc_timeout_count         -> 79

     pc_timeout_count 79 == 77 pc-ack + 2 pc_state wait     (100.0 %, exact)

   The two pc_state wait lines:
     [  915.085520] ... modem pc_state wait timeout during resume   (fatal #1)
     [ 6111.869030] ... modem pc_state wait timeout during resume   (fatal #7)

   Doc 170 section 8.16 recorded 78 = 76 + 2; the +1 is fatal #12.

4. SIX OF THE TWELVE FATALS PRODUCE ONE -- ALL SIX INSIDE THEIR OWN DOWN-WINDOW
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ dmesg | grep "pc-ack timeout" | sed 's/.*\[ *\([0-9.]*\)\].*/\1/'
   914.071907 1816.628053 2718.871855 4119.470945 4176.057624 4297.231159
   4304.244365 4376.591183 ... 8385.704199      (77 values)

   $ dmesg | grep "fatal error received" | sed 's/.*\[ *\([0-9.]*\)\].*/\1/'
   913.640795 1816.046634 2718.436833 3477.969305 4304.991306 5208.721361
   6110.407029 6231.169902 6397.771058 6580.507441 7483.839750 8385.263928

   Nearest timeout within +/- 2 s of each fatal:

     fatal# 1    913.641  next<=2s:   +0.431   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 2   1816.047  next<=2s:   +0.581   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 3   2718.437  next<=2s:   +0.435   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 4   3477.969  next<=2s:       --   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 5   4304.991  next<=2s:       --   prev<=2s:   -0.747
     fatal# 6   5208.721  next<=2s:       --   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 7   6110.407  next<=2s:   +0.445   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 8   6231.170  next<=2s:       --   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal# 9   6397.771  next<=2s:   +0.440   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal#10   6580.507  next<=2s:       --   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal#11   7483.840  next<=2s:       --   prev<=2s:       --
     fatal#12   8385.264  next<=2s:   +0.440   prev<=2s:       --

     fatals with a recovery-window timeout: 6 of 12

   (fatal #5's -0.747 is a STORM timeout just before the crash, not a recovery
   one; it is excluded.)

   Down-window check -- "stopped remote processor" -> "is now up":

     fatal# 1  stopped 913.745683   up 915.042804   timeout 914.071907  INSIDE
     fatal# 2  stopped 1816.147432  up 1817.455214  timeout 1816.628053 INSIDE
     fatal# 3  stopped 2718.539856  up 2719.296525  timeout 2718.871855 INSIDE
     fatal# 7  stopped 6110.507629  up 6111.927153  timeout 6110.852136 INSIDE
     fatal# 9  stopped 6397.877615  up 6398.492369  timeout 6398.210656 INSIDE
     fatal#12  stopped 8385.365728  up 8385.966522  timeout 8385.704199 INSIDE

     6 of 6 inside.  Down-window lengths: 1.297 / 1.308 / 0.757 / 1.420 /
     0.615 / 0.601 s.

   The six fatals with NO timeout (#4, #5, #6, #8, #10, #11) are the control:
   no runtime resume was attempted during their down-window, so nothing changed.
   Down-window length does NOT separate the two groups (fatal #5 has 1.342 s and
   no timeout; fatal #12 has 0.601 s and one) -- what separates them is whether
   some entity re-requested the device inside the window.

5. CORRECTION TO Doc 170 SECTION 8.13.6
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Section 8.13.6 called this a "post-SSR artefact ... after MBA booted" and
   listed: +0.431112 / +0.581419 / +0.435022 / none / +0.302392

   Those five MIX TWO REFERENCE POINTS and OMIT fatal #9.  Matching each of the
   five listed values to its fatal and its anchor:

     listed value    fatal   matches  delta-from-FATAL   delta-from-MBA-booted
     +0.431112        #1      +0.431112 (fatal)          -0.416685
     +0.581419        #2      +0.581419 (fatal)          -0.273279
     +0.435022        #3      +0.435022 (fatal)          +0.286831
     "none"           #4      (no timeout within +/-2 s)  --
     +0.302392        #7      +0.445107                  +0.302392 (MBA)
     (absent)         #9      +0.439598  <-- OMITTED      +0.290005
     +0.293573       #12      +0.440271                  +0.293573 (MBA)

   So the first three are fatal-anchored, the last two are MBA-anchored -- and
   fatal #9 was never listed at all.

   Recomputed consistently, all six, DELTA FROM THE FATAL:

     fatal# 1  +0.431112
     fatal# 2  +0.581419
     fatal# 3  +0.435022
     fatal# 7  +0.445107
     fatal# 9  +0.439598        <-- omitted by 8.13.6
     fatal#12  +0.440271

     spread 150 ms.

   And DELTA FROM MBA BOOTED the same six:

     fatal# 1  -0.416685
     fatal# 2  -0.273279
     fatal# 3  +0.286831
     fatal# 7  +0.302392
     fatal# 9  +0.290005
     fatal#12  +0.293573

   THE SIGN CHANGES, because the two capture-ON recoveries push "MBA booted"
   LATER (the coredump copy) while the resume fires at a fixed offset from the
   fatal.  So the anchor is the FATAL (equivalently the SSR teardown), not the
   MBA boot.  The +0.302392 in 8.13.6's list is fatal #7 measured from MBA
   booted -- which is why it looked consistent next to three fatal-anchored
   values.

6. WHY IT IS INEVITABLE, AND WHY THAT GIVES THE SECTION 8.16 FIX A TARGET
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The modem is being reloaded inside the window.  It cannot ack a
   power-collapse vote, so the 250 ms wait must expire.  Android's constant is
   UL_WAKEUP_TIMEOUT_MS = 2000 (see reference_android_bam_dmux_comparison),
   and the whole "MBA booted" -> "is now up" mpss load is only:

     fatal#12: 8385.966522 - 8385.410626 = 0.555896 s

   so a 2000 ms window (or re-reading the ack state on timeout) would COVER this
   class entirely.  Falsifiable prediction: after the section 8.16 fix,
   pc_timeout_count should stop rising by ~1 per fatal, leaving only the storm's
   own resumes.

7. IT DOES NOT CONTAMINATE THE SECTION 8.13 A/B BAR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The metric's endpoints are "SSR before shutdown" -> "MBA booted".  The
   timeout lands BEFORE "MBA booted" for #1 and #2 and AFTER it for
   #3/#7/#9/#12, so it cannot be what separates the two populations -- which
   remain separated (0.119170-0.130237 s capture-OFF vs 0.824902-0.831559 s
   capture-ON).

8. WHAT THIS CHANGES IN THE DOCUMENT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   * Section 8.21: "both counters FROZE" -> only pc_resync_count froze; the
     timeout moved once and that increment is explained.  (The storm's silence
     is therefore CLEANER than written, not weaker.)
   * Section 8.16 / section 8.19(c): accounting extended to 79 = 77 + 2.
   * Section 8.13.6: reference points unified, fatal #9 added.
   * New section 8.21.1.

================================================================================
PART 17 -- "DOES DATA STILL STALL?" YES, BUT IT IS THE USERSPACE BEARER REBUILD
           (15-26 s per fatal), NOT THE DEFECT WE ROOT-CAUSED (Doc 170 section 8.22)
================================================================================

0. THE QUESTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   User: "so data still stalls".  Checked directly rather than assumed.

1. THE ROOT-CAUSED DEFECT DOES **NOT** REPRODUCE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   defertest shape: device sitting at runtime_status=suspended / pc_state=0,
   idle, then ONE ping.  Three rounds:

   START uptime=9793.15
   round 1 BEFORE pc_state: 0 tx_sweep_guard_hits: 0 tx_defer_queued: 1197
                 tx_defer_submitted: 1197 tx_defer_wiped: 0 tx_defer_wiped_live: 0
                 runtime_status: suspended
   round 1 ping: 64 bytes from 8.8.8.8: seq=0 ttl=113 time=97.462 ms
                 1 packets transmitted, 1 packets received, 0% packet loss
   round 1 AFTER  tx_defer_queued: 1198 tx_defer_submitted: 1198
                  tx_defer_wiped_live: 0 runtime_status: active

   round 2 BEFORE pc_state: 0 ... tx_defer_queued: 1202
   round 2 ping: time=102.379 ms, 1/1, 0% loss
   round 2 AFTER  tx_defer_queued: 1203 tx_defer_submitted: 1203

   round 3 BEFORE pc_state: 1 ... tx_defer_queued: 1207
   round 3 ping: time=49.849 ms, 1/1, 0% loss
   round 3 AFTER  tx_defer_queued: 1207 (no defer increment -- direct path)

   => 2/2 first-attempt deliveries FROM THE COLLAPSED STATE, against the
      recorded 5/5 FAILURES pre-patch-812.  Round 3 took the direct path.
      tx_defer_queued - tx_defer_submitted == 0 and tx_defer_wiped_live == 0
      throughout: section 8.16's stated invariant holds.

   Sustained + application-shaped tests:
     20 x 1 Hz ping: 20 transmitted, 20 received, 0% loss, 41.795-88.121 ms
     nslookup example.com 8.8.8.8 after 20 s idle: 2 A records returned
     wwan0 rx_packets/tx_packets: 48038 / 48243 (balanced)

2. WHAT ACTUALLY STALLS -- netifd's OWN ifdown/ifup PAIRS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   F=/overlay/logroll_a9fd907c.log
   grep -E "Interface .modem. is now|link is (up|down)" $F

   Counts: ifup=7 ifdown=7 linkup=7

   Boot wall-clock = 11:18:22 UTC (derived: the rpm9 header recorded uptime
   6343.17 at 13:04:05 UTC, so boot = 13:04:05 - 6343.17 s).

   fatal    AP fatal    ifdown     ifup       outage
   #9       6397.771    13:05:00   13:05:15   15 s

   => the kernel SSR recovery is 0.119-0.130 s; the userspace bearer rebuild is

3. WHERE THE TIME GOES (fatal #12, the 26 s worst case)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ grep -E "Sep 21 13:38:(0[7-9]|1[0-9]|2[0-9]|3[0-5])" $F | grep -v time-daemon

   13:38:12  [modem12] net/wwan0 .. net/wwan6 (data)                    <-- succeeds
   ...       (qcom-time-daemon hammers QMI TIME: txn 469..475+ within one second)
   13:38:33  Interface 'modem' is now up                              <-- OUTAGE ENDS (26 s)

   The rebuild is a ModemManager re-probe + modem-object recreation, and it FAILS
   at least once ("at least a QMI port is required") before succeeding -- the port
   is not ready when the first probe runs.

4. THE WATCHDOG'S STALL RECORD, AND ITS WINDOW
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ grep -iE "Unbalanced" $F
   13:02:04  TX+5,  RX+0 (10s/60s)
   13:02:34  TX+12, RX+0 (10s/60s)
   13:04:25  TX+8,  RX+0 (10s/60s)
   13:04:35  TX+11, RX+0 (20s/60s)
   13:04:45  TX+10, RX+0 (30s/60s)
   13:04:55  TX+11, RX+0 (40s/60s)     <-- escalates; fatal #9 at 13:04:59 ends it
   13:05:16  TX+2,  RX+0 (10s/60s)
   13:06:26  TX+9,  RX+0 (10s/60s)
   13:07:57  TX+10, RX+0 (10s/60s)
   13:08:58  TX+7,  RX+0 (10s/60s)

   count = 10, ALL inside the cascade window (AP ~6222-6636; cascade fatals #8
   6231.170, #9 6397.771, #10 6580.507), and NONE in the ~3260 s since.

   CONFLICT: the watchdog only fires when TX > 0, so its silence means "no
   DETECTED stall", not "no traffic".  The cascade's fatals are 120-183 s apart
   (frequent, long outages) vs the idle clock's 902 s (same 15-26 s rebuild) --
   which is why the events cluster there.

5. WHAT THIS CHANGES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   * The user-visible stall is NOT the baseband and NOT bam_dmux: it is the AP's
     userspace bearer recovery (ModemManager re-probe + netifd ifdown/ifup).
   * The section 8.13 A/B metric scores the cheap part of a fatal's cost; the
     user pays 15-26 s.
   * Two concrete next targets: (a) the failed first ModemManager probe
     ("at least a QMI port is required") -- the rebuild waits for a retry cycle
     instead of polling for the port; (b) whether netifd must tear the interface
     down at all when the ports return within a second.
   * NOT CLAIMED: this does not touch the 902 s fatal; it is an outage-DURATION
     finding; measured in ONE boot and only for the five fatals whose netifd
     pairs are inside the retained log.


================================================================================
PART 18 -- USER CLUE CONFIRMED: THE BEARER IS ALWAYS NEW, NEVER REUSED
           (Doc 170 section 8.22.1)  +  FATAL #14 (section 8.13.8)
================================================================================

0. THE CLUE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   User, from testing the data stall: "the data bearer which modemmanager tried
   after data stall was the new bearer, not the old one".  Checked; it is exact.

1. THE INVENTORY -- A NEW MODEM OBJECT AND A NEW BEARER EVERY TIME
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   $ grep -oE "Bearer/[0-9]+" $F | uniq -c
        1 Bearer/16
        1 Bearer/18
        1 Bearer/20
        1 Bearer/22
        1 Bearer/24
        1 Bearer/26
        1 Bearer/28
        1 Bearer/30

   $ grep -oE "\[modem[0-9]+\]" $F | uniq -c
        1 [modem6]
       56 [modem7]   ...  59 [modem14]        (9 modem objects in ONE boot)

   $ mmcli -L
       /org/freedesktop/ModemManager1/Modem/14 [1] 0
   $ mmcli -m any --output-keyvalue | grep bearers
       modem.generic.bearers.length   : 1
       modem.generic.bearers.value[1] : /org/freedesktop/ModemManager1/Bearer/30

   => the bearer index advances by 2 on EVERY recovery and NEVER repeats; the old
      objects are destroyed, so this is not a leak -- it is FULL RE-PROVISIONING.

2. WHY IT CANNOT BE REUSED -- THE PROTO HANDLER IS STATELESS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   /lib/netifd/proto/modemmanager.sh

   setup (:762-767) requires EXACTLY ONE bearer:
       bearercount=$(modemmanager_get_field "${modemstatus}" \
                     "modem.generic.bearers.length")
       [ -n "${bearercount}" ] && [ "$bearercount" -eq 1 ] || {
               proto_notify_error "${interface}" INVALID_BEARER_LIST
               return 1
       }

   teardown (:846-851) just disconnects when the path is gone:
       bearerpath=$(modemmanager_get_field "${modemstatus}" \
                     "modem.generic.bearers.value\[1\]")
       [ -n "${bearerpath}" ] || {
               echo "couldn't load bearer path: disconnecting anyway"
               mmcli --modem="${device}" --simple-disconnect >/dev/null 2>&1
               return
       }

   INVALID_BEARER_LIST occurrences in the whole boot: 0.  So the handler behaves
   as designed -- the DESIGN is what costs the time.  After an SSR the modem's WDS
   session is gone from its RAM, so the old bearer is CORRECTLY invalid; there is
   simply no code path that resumes one.

3. PHASE BREAKDOWN -- TWO INDEPENDENT REBUILDS, SAME SHAPE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   fatal #13  13:53:08 -> 13:53:23  = 15 s
     08  port 'wwan0qmi0' no longer controllable; hotplug remove;
         netifd "couldn't find modem" / "couldn't load bearer path: disconnecting
         anyway"; Interface 'modem' is now down
     09  hotplug add wwan0qmi0

         -> "could not recreate modem: Unsupported device: at least a QMI port is
            required"                                       <-- FIRST PROBE FAILS

     13  "running setup for device 'qcom-soc'..."

         up now"; "modem available at .../Modem/13"; enable modem
     19  enabled; modes set to '4g'; "starting connection with apn 'jionet'";
         simple connect state (4/10) (5/10)

         [modem13/bearer28] QMI IPv4 Settings: 10.114.175.212/29, gw .213,

     22  "successfully connected the modem at bearer .../Bearer/28"

   fatal #14  14:08:32 -> 14:08:50  = 18 s   -- identical phases:
     32  teardown -> Interface down
     33  hotplug add
     34  "had failed, will retry" -> creating -> FAILS ("at least a QMI port is
         required")
     36  probe step QMI -> done
     38  [modem14] created  ("running setup for device")

     49  [modem14/bearer30] QMI IPv4 Settings: 10.131.140.86/30, gw .85
     49  "successfully connected ... /Bearer/30"
     50  Interface 'modem' is now up

   Phase table (seconds):

     phase                                    #13    #14
     port drop -> netifd teardown -> down      0      0
     hotplug -> FAILED probe -> object creat.  5      6
     "running setup" -> SIM re-read            5      5
     enable -> modes -> connect -> bearer      4      5
     netifd address + route                    1      2

   => the cost is not ONE slow step: ALL FOUR provisioning stages are redone, and
      TWO exist only because the object is new -- the FAILED FIRST PROBE (~2-4 s of
      retry backoff) and the SIM RE-READ (~5 s).

4. FATAL #14 -- AND THE TEST TRAFFIC SUBSTITUTED THE FATAL
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   [10210.922046] fatal error received: a2_power.c:1189:      <-- NOT the clock
   SSR before shutdown 10210.944642 -> MBA booted 10211.063852 = 0.119210 s
   (inside the capture-OFF band 0.119170-0.130237; n = 10; no "port failed halt")

   Clock predicted AP ~10189.8 (fatal #13's modem boot 9287.428 + 902.35):
   #14 arrived 21 s LATE and with a DIFFERENT signature.  Between #13 and #14 I ran
   a burst of test traffic (3-round idle-then-ping defertest, 20 x 1 Hz pings, a
   DNS query, stall probes).  E1 found that under 1 Hz traffic a2_power.c:1189
   fires at 895.490 / 932.855 s and REPLACES the deterministic idle fatal.  That is
   exactly what happened: the traffic I generated to test the data stall swapped the
   idle timer for the activity-correlated fatal.  Cleanest confirmation of E1 so
   far -- and a standing hazard: a soak that generates traffic is not measuring the
   idle clock.

   §8.13 bar: 10 of 20, 0 AP reboots.  Remaining: 10 fatals.

5. CORRECTION -- pc_timeout_count IS NOT FROZEN (extends PART 15)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   pc_timeout_count  79 -> 81
   dmesg "pc-ack timeout"  77 -> 79   ;  "pc_state wait timeout"  2  (unchanged)
   => 81 = 79 + 2, STILL EXACT.

   But NEITHER new increment is an SSR-recovery event:
     10083.374717   standalone -- no fatal, no resync near it
     10210.200654   0.721392 s BEFORE fatal #14 (fatal at 10210.922046)

   So section 8.21.1's class list gains a FOURTH entry: a plain runtime resume under
   traffic whose 250 ms ack wait expires -- no resync, no SSR (the section 8.16
   latency defect in normal operation).

   And 10210.200654 is the SECOND pre-fatal timeout at delta -0.72/-0.75 s:
     fatal #5   4304.991306  timeout 4304.244365  delta -0.746941   a2_power.c:1189
     fatal #14 10210.922046  timeout 10210.200654 delta -0.721392   a2_power.c:1189
   2 of the 3 a2_power.c:1189 fatals.  n = 2: a CANDIDATE antecedent, not
   established.  Most economical reading: the modem stops answering ~0.72 s before
   it declares that fatal, i.e. the timeout is a SYMPTOM of the modem already going
   down, not a cause.

   pc_resync_count is still 81 (last lost edge [ 7417.446456]) -- resync silence
   2793.48 s at fatal #14, 3119.83 s at uptime 10537.28.  Only the TIMEOUT counter
   resumed.

1. WHAT ENDED THE BOOT

   Three consecutive misses followed (20:47:43, 20:48:11, 20:48:39) and

     20:47:16  usb 1-1:   USB disconnect, device number 4
     20:47:16  usb 1-1.1: USB disconnect, device number 5

   Both xHCI controllers were REMOVED -- a controller-level removal, not a
   peripheral disconnect -- and the host's own USB WiFi deauthenticated "by local
   choice".  "Lid closed" names the cause.  /sys/bus/usb/devices/ was empty
   afterwards (only the two root hubs returned, at 22:22:09, with no dongle).

   The HMU05 is a USB-powered dongle, so it lost power: it was OFF, not hung.
   A host power cycle is NOT an AP reboot and does NOT score against the bar.
   This is the same mis-attribution trap s8.14 already records (s8.10's "reset
   that survived 823" was the author's own USB-hub installation).

2. FINAL SCORING FOR BOOT A
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   boot_id              a9fd907c  (constant across all 585 host samples)
   boot began           ~17:03:21 local (uptime 1615.78 at the 17:30:17 first sample)
   ran                  3 h 44 m
   fatal error received 17        (dmesg, and dev_state_mon fatals=17)
   capture-OFF fatals   15        (= fatal #17 - 2; the coredump was disabled before #3)
   AP reboots            0
   dumps                 18       (FROZEN -- no new dump after the disable)

   The rule count = fatal# - 2 is verified five times in this file:

      after fatal #4    2 of 20   (PART 1)
      after fatal #5    3 of 20   (PART 6)
      after fatal #7    5 of 20   (PART 10)
      after fatal #10   8 of 20   (PART 13)

   *** s8.13 BAR: 15 OF 20 POST-DISABLE FATALS SCORED, 0 AP REBOOTS. ***
   Remaining: 5 fatals (~1.1 h at the ~13.2 min/fatal boot-A rate).

   The capture-OFF population is unchanged in kind: no "port failed halt" in any of
   the 15, against exactly 2 for the 2 capture-ON recoveries (13 MBA loads at the
   PART-13 count = 1 cold boot + 12 recoveries).

3. THE TRAP WAITING ON THE NEXT BOOT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The dongle will COLD BOOT when it is replugged -- new boot_id, uptime near zero.
   A watcher that keys only on "uptime decreased" or "unreachable" will score that
   as an AP reset and FALSELY FAIL the bar.

   Rule for boot B: the first sample must establish a NEW baseline boot_id and
   fatal count, and no reboot may be scored until a fatal has occurred after that
   baseline.  A boot_id change with fatal count 0 is a host-induced power cycle,
   never a scored reset.

4. WHAT MUST BE PULLED BEFORE RE-ARMING (the device was OFF, so nothing was lost)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   These live on /overlay (flash), keyed by boot_id, so the power loss did not
   destroy them and the next boot cannot overwrite them:

     /overlay/logroll_a9fd907c.log     <- unified logread -f + PMKNOB timeline
     /overlay/dmesg_roll_a9fd907c.txt  <- the 5 s rolling dmesg tail
     /overlay/ssr_ledger.csv           <- one row per SSR (a gap = a hang)
     /overlay/beacon_a.txt, /overlay/beacon_b.txt
     /overlay/coredump_watch.log

   Pull these FIRST on the next boot, before re-arming anything.

5. SCOPE NOTE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   This PART records a boot boundary, not a result about the fix.  The bar is
   unchanged and unmet: 15 of 20, 0 reboots.  It says only that the boot ended
   without a scored failure and that the ending was external to the device.

1. BOOT A's ARTIFACTS ARE OFF THE DEVICE (the device was OFF, so nothing was lost)

2. BOOT B BASELINE (measured, not assumed)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   boot_id              ea6401be-0b62-4d78-ab8c-128e48f0d1a6
   boot time            ~16:40:37Z (from clock 16:44:03Z at uptime 205.70)
   fatal error received  0 at baseline

   /overlay/coredump_ENABLE  ABSENT

   console loglevel     6 4 1 7   (KERN_INFO suppressed on console, ring buffer intact)

   Boot B is the CLEANER regime: because coredump_ENABLE is absent, rc.local
   disables the capture at boot, so EVERY fatal in boot B is post-disable.
   Boot A had to exclude fatals #1 and #2; boot B excludes nothing.

   Instruments confirmed running on the device (pidfiles present):
     beacon 3275, ssr_ledger 3298, q6trace_soak 3266, dmesg_roll 3323, logroll 3339
     coredump_watch: NOT running (correct -- capture is OFF)

3. THE POOLING IS PRE-REGISTERED HERE, BEFORE THE RUN, WITH ITS REASON
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The bar is "0 AP reboots across the next 20 fatals".  Boot A delivered 15 of
   those and was ended by a HOST power cycle, which is not a scored failure.
   Boot B supplies the remaining 5.

     pooled score = 15 (boot A) + fatals_seen(boot B), bar at 20
     => boot B needs 5 fatals, ~1.1 h at boot A's ~13.2 min/fatal

   Why pooling is legitimate rather than convenient:
     * Both counted sets are "the coredump was disabled at the time of the
       fatal".  Boot A's two capture-ON fatals are EXCLUDED from the count, so
       the two sets are identically treated.
     * Each fatal-triggered SSR is an independent trial of the failure mode
       (the AP hanging during the SSR teardown), and the pre-registration was
       about the FIX, not about a boot.
     * The bar's own justification is a rate argument: the pre-823 rate was
       order 1 reboot per 1-3 SSRs, so an ineffective fix would be caught long
       before 20.  Pooling does not weaken that.

   Stated BEFORE the run so it cannot be fitted afterwards.  The alternative
   (run boot B to 20 independently, ~4.4 h) is recorded here as the stronger
   variant if the pooled bar is met and time allows.

4. THE WATCHER WAS REDESIGNED, AND THE REASON MATTERS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   watch813.sh had two defects that this round exposed:

     (a) It BROKE on 3 consecutive misses, printing "POSSIBLE RESET".  But an
         AP hang is followed by a PMIC PON watchdog reboot (30 s), so the
         correct signal is a BOOT_ID CHANGE observed on a LATER poll.  Breaking
         on the unreachable streak throws away the exact event being scored --
         and it did: the 20:47 "POSSIBLE RESET" was a host power cycle.
     (b) It treated ANY boot_id change as a failure, which is wrong when the
         host itself power-cycles the dongle.

   scratch/watch813_bootB.sh keeps polling through unreachable windows and
   scores a failure ONLY when the boot_id changes AND the PREVIOUS sample had
   already seen a fatal -- i.e. the AP died during a fatal-triggered SSR, which
   is the phenomenon the bar is about.  A boot_id change with prev_fatals = 0 is
   re-baselined as a host power cycle, never scored.

5. HAZARD FOUND THIS ROUND: THE DEVICE CLOCK IS WRONG FOR THE FIRST MINUTE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Boot B's logroll header reads uptime=18.66 at 2026-09-21T12:56:55Z, but the
   true time was 16:40:56Z -- the clock was 3 h 44 m BEHIND.

   Measured from the PMKNOB lines (device clock vs uptime):

     uptime  56.42   12:57:33Z     <- still wrong
     uptime  68.58   16:41:46Z     <- corrected

   so the jump is between uptime 57 and 69 s, and the offset is -3h44m01s
   (= -13441 s).  The device has no RTC and syncs via sysfixtime / sysntpd /
   qcom-time-daemon (all three are installed; qcom-time-daemon runs `-r 0`).

   THIS IS NOT A BOOT-A PROBLEM.  Boot A's clock was CORRECT: its logroll
   header is uptime=5895.82 at 12:56:37Z, and boot A booted at 11:18:21Z, so
   uptime 5895.82 => 12:56:37Z exactly, and the clock tracked uptime to within
   0.2 s over the following 7516 s.  The offset is boot-B-specific.

   CONSEQUENCE FOR ANALYSIS: any wall-clock timestamp from the first ~70 s of a
   boot on this device is wrong by an unknown amount.  Anchor on /proc/uptime,
   which is monotonic and correct from t=0.  The §8.13 watcher already does
   (it uses uptime + boot_id), so the bar is unaffected -- but the early logroll
   timestamps for boot B must be corrected by +3h44m01s or read as uptime.

6. DOCUMENTATION DRIFT FOUND AND FIXED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   scratch/rc.local.device was STALE: it lacked the logroll block that the live
   /etc/rc.local has (8 references).  A reader trusting the repo copy would
   conclude logroll is not autostarted, and would then wonder how boot B has
   logroll from uptime 18.66 -- when rc.local in fact starts it.

   The live file was pulled and the repo copy resynced; both now hash
   66864d9f8ddf3f233c07e8155d58980c.  This is the same class as Doc 160's
   stale-live-tree incident: the repo copy is not the device.

7. SCOPE NOTE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Boot B has 0 fatals at the time of writing.  Nothing about the fix is
   claimed here; this PART records the baseline, the pre-registered pooling, the
   watcher's redesign, and two hazards (clock, drift) that would otherwise be
   discovered as anomalies later.

PART 21 -- THE WATCHER SCORED A FALSE FAILURE, AND THE FIX IS A PRE-REGISTERED
           TWO-PART TEST FOR "AP HANG vs POWER LOSS"
           (2026-09-21 ~23:04 local / 17:19 UTC)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. WHAT HAPPENED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Boot B (`ea6401be`) reached 2 fatals:

     920.32   lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054   coredumps=18
    1822.54   lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054   coredumps=18

   Both recovered.  At 23:04:29 the v1 watcher printed:

     *** FAILURE: AP RESET DETECTED -- boot_id ea6401be -> 48f567c0, and the
     *** previous sample had fatals=2, so the AP died during a fatal-triggered SSR.
     *** pooled score at the reset: 17/20

   THE USER REPORTED A LOOSE WIRE.  The user was right and the watcher was wrong.
   The host kernel log proves it, and the proof is a CONTROLLER-LEVEL event:

     23:03:41 usb 1-1: USB disconnect, device number 2
     23:03:41 cdc_ncm 1-1:1.2 enu1i2: unregister 'cdc_ncm'
     23:03:41 macsmc 23e400000.smc: RTKit: syslog message: aceElec.cpp:711:
                                            Elec: Elec Cause 0x0
     23:03:41 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: remove, state 4
     23:03:41 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: USB bus 2 deregistered
     23:03:41 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: remove, state 4
     23:03:41 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: USB bus 1 deregistered
     23:03:43 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: new USB bus registered   <- controller re-probed
     23:04:04 usb 1-1: new high-speed USB device number 2 ... Product: USB Gadget

   The host's Apple SMC reported an ELECTRICAL EVENT, and the host removed and
   re-registered its xHCI CONTROLLER.  **An AP reboot cannot do that**: an AP
   reboot disconnects and re-enumerates the USB GADGET on the bus that is
   already there; it never causes the host to deregister a controller.  The
   dongle lost VBUS, cold-booted, and came back as a new boot_id.

   So the 23:04 event is a HOST POWER EVENT.  It is NOT scored, the bar is
   intact, and the corrected pooled score is 17/20 with 0 AP reboots.

2. THE PRE-REGISTERED ADJUDICATION RULE (written before any further data)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The v1 rule -- "boot_id changed AND prev_fatals >= 1" -- is TOO COARSE: it
   does not require any evidence that the AP actually hung, so ANY power loss
   after any fatal scores as a failure.  v2 replaces it with two INDEPENDENT
   tests, both required for a failure:

   (A) HOST-LOG TEST.  A power loss removes the host's xHCI controller and makes
       the SMC report an electrical event; an AP hang does not.  On a boot_id
       change, search the host log over the outage window (last good sample
       -120 s .. now) for
           USB bus [0-9]+ deregistered | Elec Cause | xhci-hcd.*remove, state
       Any hit => POWER LOSS => not scored.
       Verified on the real event: 5 hits.  Verified on a control window
       (22:50-22:55, no outage): 0 hits.

   (B) MID-SSR TEST.  An AP hang happens INSIDE a fatal-triggered SSR -- Doc 159
       puts the site in the tens of ms after `port failed halt`.  So a real AP
       reset must leave the DEAD boot's `dmesg_roll_<boot_id>.txt` ending with a
       fatal and NO completed recovery after it.  That file is boot-keyed, so it
       survives the reboot.  Concretely: the line number of the last
       "fatal error received" must be GREATER than the line number of the last
       "is now up" / "successfully reinitialized BAM channels".
       Verified on boot B (a known power loss): last fatal at line 191, last
       completed recovery at line 239 => mid_ssr = 0 => NOT scored.  Correct.
       The dead tail shows both fatals recovered:
           131: [  914.431036] fatal error received: lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054
           177: [  915.582318] remote processor 4080000.remoteproc is now up
           191: [ 1818.344929] fatal error received: lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054
           237: [ 1819.430521] remote processor 4080000.remoteproc is now up
           239: [ 1819.645583] SSR powerup: successfully reinitialized BAM channels and rings

       This is the stronger of the two: it is device-side, it does not depend on
       the host, and it directly tests the mechanism (an AP hang requires an
       in-progress SSR).  If the dead file is unavailable, mid_ssr is recorded
       as UNKNOWN, not 0 -- an unavailable instrument is not a negative result.

   A boot_id change is scored as a FAILURE only if (A) shows no power event AND
   (B) shows mid_ssr = 1.  Anything else is re-baselined and not scored.

3. SCORING AFTER THE CORRECTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

     boot A (a9fd907c)   15 post-disable fatals   0 reboots   (host power cycle, PART 19)
     boot B (ea6401be)    2 post-disable fatals   0 reboots   (loose wire, this PART)
     ------------------------------------------------------------------------
     pooled              17 of 20                  0 reboots

   Boot C (`48f567c0`) is armed: baseline fatals=0, coredump=disabled,
   `/overlay/coredump_ENABLE` absent.  Watcher: `scratch/watch813_v2.sh 17`
   (pooled prior 17, bar 20, both tests ACTIVE).

   The clock self-corrected in boot C as well: at 17:20:21Z the device and the
   host agreed to the second, while boot C's early logroll mtime read ~22:35
   local.  Consistent with PART 20 item 5 -- the offset is boot-specific and
   transient, so /proc/uptime remains the only safe anchor.

4. TWO TOOLING TRAPS HIT WHILE BUILDING THE FIX (both silent)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   (a) `date -d "$t - 120 seconds"` DOES NOT SUBTRACT.  GNU date parses the
       "- 120" as a UTC OFFSET, not as an arithmetic term, and silently returns a
       time ~7 h in the FUTURE:

           $ date -d "2026-09-21 23:03:36 - 120 seconds" '+%F %T'
           2026-09-22 06:08:37          <-- wrong, and no error

       The consequence would have been a discriminator that ALWAYS found 0 power
       events (the window starts in the future), i.e. it would have confirmed
       every false failure.  Caught only because the window was tested against
       the known event BEFORE arming.  Use epoch arithmetic:
           date -d "@$(( $(date -d "$t" +%s) - 120 ))" '+%F %T'

   (b) `pkill -f "watch813_v2"` KILLED THE SHELL THAT ISSUED IT, because the
       pattern matched the tool's own command line.  Both the old watcher and
       the issuing shell died and the replacement never started -- a silent gap
       in monitoring.  Kill by PID read from `ps`, never by `pkill -f` on a
       pattern that appears in your own command line.

   Both are recorded because both would have corrupted the experiment silently
   rather than loudly.

5. SCOPE NOTE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   No fix is claimed.  The bar is 17 of 20 with 0 AP reboots, and the only two
   boot boundaries so far were both HOST-SIDE power events.  The device has not
   been observed to reset itself in this window.

PART 22 -- *** THE s8.13 BAR IS MET: 21 OF 20 POST-DISABLE FATALS, 0 AP REBOOTS ***
           -- AND WHY THAT IS WEAKER EVIDENCE THAN THE PRE-REGISTRATION IMPLIED
           (2026-09-21 23:27 local / 17:42 UTC)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. THE RESULT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

     boot A (a9fd907c)   15 post-disable fatals   0 AP reboots   (lid close = host power event)
     boot B (ea6401be)    2 post-disable fatals   0 AP reboots   (loose wire = host power event)
     boot C (48f567c0)    4 post-disable fatals   0 AP reboots   (running)
     ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
     pooled              21 of 20                  0 AP reboots

   *** BAR MET: 0 AP reboots across 21 fatal-triggered SSRs. ***
   The watcher fired at 23:27:25 (`scratch/watch813_v2.sh`, pooled prior 17).

   Boot C's four fatals, with the s8.13 recovery metric
   (`SSR before shutdown` -> `MBA booted`):

     #   fatal@AP     signature                    SSR->MBA     recovered
     1    541.457046  a2_power.c:1189              0.125977 s   yes
     2    578.581815  a2_power.c:1189              0.133399 s   yes
     3   1397.732455  lte_ml1_sm_idle.c:2533       0.137563 s   yes   <- NEW SIGNATURE
     4   1420.237624  a2_power.c:1189              0.123339 s   yes

   `rproc=offline` in the watcher's bar-met sample is NOT a failure: the sample
   landed at uptime 1420, i.e. between fatal #4 (1420.238) and its recovery
   (1420.941).  It is the watcher catching an SSR in flight.  Recorded because
   it reads as alarming and is not.

2. *** THE CAVEAT THAT MATTERS: THE BAR'S POWER ARGUMENT USED A RATE THE CORPUS LATER REVISED DOWN ***
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The bar's justification (PART 19 / README) was:

     "the pre-823 rate was order 1 reboot per 1-3 SSRs, and even a true 1-in-4
      rate gives ~99.7 % chance of seeing one in 20"

   Against a 1-in-4 rate, 0 reboots in 21 SSRs gives P = 0.75^21 = 0.0018, so
   the result WOULD reject it.  **But the corpus's own later estimate of the AP
   hang rate is 3-5 % (1 in 20-40 SSRs)** -- the standing statistics rule
   ("pre-register the sample size") records exactly this.  Against a 5 % rate:

     P(0 reboots in 21) = 0.95^21 = 0.34
     P(0 reboots in 21) = 0.97^21 = 0.53   (at 3 %)

   **So 21 clean SSRs does NOT establish that the fix works.**  The bar is met
   as PRE-REGISTERED, and that is worth stating plainly, but the pre-registration
   was powered against a rate the corpus subsequently revised downward by ~5x.
   The standing rule requires **n >= 60 SSRs (~18 h)** before "zero AP reboots"
   becomes evidence against a 3-5 % race.

   This is not a reason to discard the run; it is the reason the run does not
   close the question.  Stated here rather than discovered later.

3. THE RECOVERY-TIME A/B IS REFINED, AND IT SURVIVES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The capture-OFF band was 0.119170-0.130237 s (n = 9, spread 11.1 ms) against
   0.824902-0.831559 s for the two capture-ON recoveries.  Boot C adds four
   capture-OFF samples, and TWO of them exceed the old upper edge:

     0.123339   0.125977   (inside)
     0.133399   0.137563   (OUTSIDE the old band)

   New capture-OFF range: **0.119170-0.137563 s (n = 13, spread 18.4 ms)** --
   the band is 66 % wider than the n = 9 sample suggested.  **The A/B conclusion
   is unchanged**: the two populations are still non-overlapping and still ~6x
   apart (0.1376 vs 0.8249), on four signatures.  But the OFF edge is not as
   tight as previously stated, and a future sample above ~0.14 s would begin to
   matter.

4. A NEW FATAL SIGNATURE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   `lte_ml1_sm_idle.c:2533` (boot C fatal #3, AP 1397.732455) has **no prior
   occurrence anywhere in the corpus** -- the only hits are from this boot's own
   captures.  The ledger's cumulative signature census is now:

     lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054   16
     a2_power.c:1189               14
     a2_power.c:2949                3
     a2_task.c:3179                 2
     lte_ml1_sm_idle.c:2533         1   <- NEW
     ----------------------------------
     total                         36

5. AN ANOMALY LEFT OPEN: BOOT C's FATALS ARE NOT ON THE ~902 s CLOCK
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Boots A and B both opened with `lte_ml1_sleepmgr_stm.c:4054` at ~920 s of AP
   uptime -- the clock, at ~902 s of modem uptime.  **Boot C does not follow it**:
   its four fatals are at AP 541, 578, 1397 and 1420 s, i.e. modem uptimes of
   ~529, ~566, ~1385 and ~1408 s after the modem boots at AP 12.6 s.  None is
   near 902 s, and two of the four are `a2_power.c:1189` -- the
   ACTIVITY-CORRELATED signature (E1), not the clock.

   Two readings, not yet distinguished:

   (a) The 902 s is not a wall-clock timer but a DRX-cycle COUNT (~400 cycles at
       ~2.26 s).  A different paging/DRX cadence in boot C would then move the
       fatal in wall-clock time while leaving the mechanism intact.  This is
       consistent with the root-cause record (the Q6 power-collapse vote fails
       after ~400 DRX cycles).
   (b) Something in boot C generated activity that substituted the
       activity-correlated fatal for the idle clock -- E1's hazard, which the
       corpus records as a standing trap ("a soak that generates traffic is not
       measuring the idle clock").

   Boot C is the first boot after a HOST POWER LOSS, so (b) has an obvious
   candidate mechanism (a cold boot re-establishing bearers differently), but it
   is NOT established and must not be asserted.  What IS established: **the
   regime label matters -- boot C's 4 samples are not the idle-clock regime, so
   they cannot be pooled with A/B's 17 for any statement ABOUT THE CLOCK.**

   The s8.13 BAR itself is unaffected: the bar counts AP reboots across
   fatal-triggered SSRs, whatever the signature.  But any future claim about the
   902 s clock must say which regime it rests on.

6. SCOPE NOTE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The bar is met as pre-registered.  It does NOT establish that the AP hang is
   fixed (item 2), and the device has still never been observed to reset itself
   (all three boot boundaries were host power events).  The next honest step is
   to keep the soak running toward n >= 60, which is passive, while the
   non-flash parts of the Android differential (NV/EFS/mcfg hashes) proceed in
   parallel.
