PART 23 -- THE CORPUS'S ANDROID PARTITION MAP DOES NOT MATCH THE DEVICE'S GPT,
           SO ANY NV COMPARISON BUILT ON IT WOULD READ THE WRONG PARTITIONS
           (2026-09-21 23:35 local / 17:50 UTC)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Found while scoping the Android differential's Step 0 (hash modemst1/2, fsg,
   fsc, mcfg on both sides).  This is exactly the check that is supposed to
   invalidate the exercise before it is run, and it did.

1. THE DEVICE'S ACTUAL GPT (read from LBA 2, names at entry+56, UTF-16LE)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Raw name bytes, first 24 per partition, read with `dd if=/dev/mmcblk0`:

     p1    66 00 73 00 63 00 ...              -> "fsc"
     p2    66 00 73 00 67 00 ...              -> "fsg"
     p3    6d 00 6f 00 64 00 65 00 6d 00 ...  -> "modem"
     p4    6d 00 6f 00 64 00 65 00 6d 00 73 00 74 00 31 00  -> "modemst1"
     p5    6d 00 6f 00 64 00 65 00 6d 00 73 00 74 00 32 00  -> "modemst2"
     p6    70 00 65 00 72 00 73 00 69 00 73 00 74 00        -> "persist"
     p13   62 00 6f 00 6f 00 74 00              -> "boot"
     p14   72 00 6f 00 6f 00 74 00 66 00 73 00  -> "rootfs"
     p15   72 00 6f 00 6f 00 74 00 66 00 73 00 5f 00 64 00   -> "rootfs_data"

   OpenWrt's `/dev/block/by-name/` matches this EXACTLY
   (`modem`->p3, `modemst1`->p4, `modemst2`->p5, `persist`->p6, `sec`->p7,
   `hyp`->p8, `rpm`->p9, `sbl1`->p10, `tz`->p11, `boot`->p13, `rootfs`->p14,
   `rootfs_data`->p15).  So the kernel's view and the on-disk GPT agree.

2. THE CORPUS'S ANDROID MAP (Stock_Android_Live/01_ANDROID_LIVE_SESSION_FINDINGS.md s5)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

     modem    -> mmcblk0p1
     modemst1 -> mmcblk0p13
     modemst2 -> mmcblk0p14
     fsc      -> mmcblk0p16
     fsg      -> mmcblk0p20

   **These disagree with the GPT on every entry that matters, and the GPT has
   no p16 or p20 at all** (it stops at p15).  On the GPT, p1/p2 are fsc/fsg,
   p13/p14 are boot/rootfs -- so a dump taken from "modemst1 = p13" would have
   read the boot partition, and "modemst2 = p14" the squashfs rootfs.

3. WHY THIS MATTERS AND WHAT IT DOES *NOT* ESTABLISH
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   It does NOT establish that the Android session's reading was wrong at the
   time.  Two readings remain open, and this PART does not choose between them:

   (a) **The GPT was changed after the Android session.**  The GPT carries
       OpenWrt-specific names (`rootfs`, `rootfs_data`) that stock Android would
       not have, so the GPT HAS been modified for OpenWrt.  If the OpenWrt
       install rewrote it, then the stock layout the Android session saw is gone,
       and the stock Android NV partitions may have been relocated or
       overwritten.  **This would make a same-device Android-vs-OpenWrt NV
       comparison impossible, not merely mis-indexed.**
   (b) **The doc's map was recorded from a different device or a stale source.**
       The corpus covers more than one msm8916 board, and a p1-first layout is
       characteristic of other QCOM boards.

   Either way the ACTION is the same and is cheap.

4. WHAT IS ESTABLISHED, AND WHAT TO DO
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   ESTABLISHED:
     * the device's current GPT is p1=fsc, p2=fsg, p3=modem, p4=modemst1,
       p5=modemst2, p6=persist, p13=boot, p14=rootfs, p15=rootfs_data;
     * `/dev/block/by-name` agrees with it;
     * the corpus's Android index map cannot be used to locate NV partitions;
     * **p4 and p5 are NOT empty** -- p4's header reads `49 4d 41 47 45 46 53 31
       56` = ASCII "IMAGEFS1V" at offset 40, the QCOM EFS/FSG image magic, and
       the first 1 MB of each of p4 and p5 holds ~1.6 M non-zero bytes.  So the
       "OpenWrt's NV is uninitialised" hypothesis is DEAD -- both are real,
       populated EFS images.

   TO DO, BEFORE ANY ANDROID FLASH:
     * record the GPT by reading LBA 2 on BOTH sides (a 30-second check), and
       locate NV **by name**, never by the doc's index table;
     * establish whether the stock Android GPT survives anywhere -- if the
       OpenWrt install rewrote it, Step 0 of the Android plan is not "compare NV"
       but "recover or re-derive NV", which is a different and much larger task.

   This is recorded as a CORPUS CORRECTION, not merely a run note: the Android
   partition map in `Stock_Android_Live/01_ANDROID_LIVE_SESSION_FINDINGS.md` s5
   must not be cited as authoritative for this device.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 24 -- PART 23's OPEN QUESTION IS RESOLVED (lk2nd RE-PARTITIONS BUT CARRIES
           THE STOCK EFS OVER), THE v2 DISCRIMINATOR PASSES ITS FIRST LIVE TEST,
           AND THE DEVICE TIMEZONE CHANGED
           (2026-09-21 ~23:45 local / 18:00 UTC; port design supplied by the user)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. RESOLUTION OF PART 23 -- THE GPT IS CHANGED **BY DESIGN**, AND THE STOCK EFS IS CARRIED OVER
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   User-supplied, and it settles the open question:

     "as we port it from android to openwrt we use lk2nd bootloader ... so partition
      table changes but required partitions are like EFS and other thing are taken
      from original stock"   (postmarketOS wiki: Zhihe series LTE dongles (generic-zhihe))

   So reading (a) of PART 23 §3 is the correct one, and PART 23's fear was too
   pessimistic:

     * the GPT IS rewritten by the port (lk2nd / the OpenWrt install) -- which is
       exactly why it carries OpenWrt-specific names (`rootfs`, `rootfs_data`);
     * **the required partitions (EFS and friends) are CARRIED OVER FROM STOCK**,
       so the NV is NOT lost.

   **This also reframes the Android doc rather than convicting it.** Its map
   (`modem`=p1, `modemst1`=p13, `modemst2`=p14, `fsc`=p16, `fsg`=p20) is
   presumably CORRECT **for the stock layout**, which no longer exists on the
   device. The error is not the map; it is citing a pre-port map against a
   post-port device. PART 23's correction block stands in substance -- locate NV
   by NAME, never by index -- but "the doc is wrong" becomes "the doc is
   describing a layout that has been replaced".

   **CONSEQUENCE FOR THE ANDROID DIFFERENTIAL, AND IT IS A NEGATIVE RESULT:**
   since the OpenWrt NV is *derived from* the stock NV by design, NV is not an
   independent input and **is largely removed from the candidate list** for
   "why does Android not crash". Step 0 shrinks from "compare two independently
   evolved NVs" to "check whether the carry-over was faithful" -- worth one
   cheap look (a lossy or partial copy would still be a finding), but it is no
   longer the high-value check PART 23 assumed. **The remaining candidate is
   the AP->modem control stream (Phase 3), not NV.**

   Still unverified and stated as such: whether the carry-over was byte-exact.
   The user's description is of the port's DESIGN; it has not been measured here.

2. THE v2 DISCRIMINATOR PASSES ITS FIRST LIVE TEST
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   At 23:37:20 the host lost its USB controller again (third time), and at
   23:38:19 the watcher saw the boot_id change and adjudicated it CORRECTLY
   with no intervention:

     --- boot_id changed: 48f567c0 -> a5af0fc8 (prev_fatals=4) ---
     host-log discriminator since '2026-09-21 23:34:57': 6 controller/electrical event line(s)
       EVIDENCE: 23:37:20 usb usb1: USB disconnect, device number 1
       EVIDENCE: 23:37:20 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: USB bus 1 deregistered
       EVIDENCE: 23:37:20 xhci-hcd xhci-hcd.2.auto: remove, state 4
       EVIDENCE: 23:37:21 macsmc ... aceElec.cpp:711:  Elec: Elec Cause 0x0
     *** POWER EVENT (host removed its xHCI controller / SMC electrical event) ***
     *** NOT an AP hang: the dongle lost VBUS.  Re-baselining, NOT scored. ***
     *** pooled score stays at 25/60 ***

   **This is the first time the v2 rule ran against a live event, and it
   converted what v1 would have scored as a SECOND false FAILURE into a
   correctly-excluded power event.** The rule was pre-registered (PART 21 §2)
   before this event, so this is a genuine test, not a fitted explanation.

3. THREE HOST POWER EVENTS IN THREE HOURS -- A PATTERN, NOT AN ACCIDENT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

     20:47   lid closed          -> both xHCI buses removed, "Lid closed."
     23:03   loose wire          -> SMC Elec Cause, controller re-probed
     23:37   (cause not yet named) -> SMC Elec Cause x2, controller re-probed

   The 23:37 event came ~34 minutes AFTER the user reseated the wire.  Either the
   wire/connector is still intermittent, or something else is cutting VBUS.
   **This matters beyond convenience**: if the cause is the dongle's own power
   draw tripping the host's electrical protection, then the "power event" is
   DEVICE-TRIGGERED, and it would be a real phenomenon rather than an artefact.
   Not investigated; recorded so it is not mistaken for three unrelated accidents.

   The s8.13 score is unaffected in all three cases -- none is an AP hang.

4. THE DEVICE TIMEZONE CHANGED -- A NEW 15-MINUTE TRAP
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The user changed the device from UTC to Asia/Kolkata.  Measured immediately
   after:

     HOST   Mon Sep 21 23:40:12 +0545   date -u = 2026-09-21T17:55:12Z
     DEVICE Mon Sep 21 23:25:12 IST     date -u = 2026-09-21T17:55:12Z
     /etc/TZ = IST-5:30

   **UTC agrees to the second on both sides, so `date -u` and every `%FT%TZ`
   timestamp remain safe.  LOCAL time now differs by 15 MINUTES** -- the host is
   +05:45 (Nepal), the device is +05:30 (India).  So any log line carrying device
   LOCAL time is 15 min BEHIND the host's local clock, on top of the existing
   first-minute boot offset (PART 20 §5).

   Practical rule, unchanged in spirit: **anchor on `/proc/uptime` or on explicit
   UTC (`%FT%TZ`); never mix a device local timestamp with a host local
   timestamp.** The logroll/beacon/dmesg_roll writers all use `date -u`, so the
   existing evidence is unaffected.

5. SCOPE NOTE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The bar stands at 21 of 20 for the s8.13 question (PART 22) and the n >= 60
   counter is at 25/60 on the new boot `a5af0fc8`.  No fix is claimed, and no AP
   hang has been observed: all three boot boundaries were host power events.

   *** CORRECTED IN PART 25 s6: the n >= 60 counter should read 21, not 25. ***


PART 25 -- THE USER SUPPLIES THE POSTMARKETOS WIKI TEXT; IT CONFIRMS THE STOCK
           PARTITION MAP AND EXPOSES A CANDIDATE THE CORPUS HAS NEVER EXAMINED:
           THE TRUSTZONE FIRMWARE WAS REPLACED WITH DRAGONBOARD 410C's
           (2026-09-21 23:55 local / 18:25 UTC)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

0. WHY THIS PART EXISTS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The user posted the full text of
       https://wiki.postmarketos.org/wiki/Zhihe_series_LTE_dongles_(generic-zhihe)
   after the assistant could not fetch it (WebFetch returned empty; a direct
   curl hit an Anubis anti-bot challenge).  It is a PRIMARY source for this
   device family and it settles PART 23 AND opens s3 below.

1. IT CONFIRMS THE ANDROID DOC'S PARTITION MAP -- WHICH IS THE *STOCK* MAP
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The wiki's "Partition layout" section lists the stock symlinks verbatim:

       modem -> mmcblk0p1        sbl1 -> p2      sbl1bak -> p3
       aboot -> p4               abootbak -> p5  rpm -> p6       rpmbak -> p7
       tz -> p8                  tzbak -> p9     hyp -> p10      hypbak -> p11
       pad -> p12                modemst1 -> p13 modemst2 -> p14 misc -> p15
       fsc -> p16                ssd -> p17      splash -> p18   DDR -> p19
       fsg -> p20                sec -> p21      boot -> p22     system -> p23
       persist -> p24            cache -> p25    recovery -> p26 userdata -> p27

   That is EXACTLY the map in `Stock_Android_Live/01_...FINDINGS.md` s5:
   modem=p1, modemst1=p13, modemst2=p14, fsc=p16, fsg=p20.

   So PART 23's finding stands but its VERDICT must be softened a second time:
   the Android doc is not mis-indexed and never was -- it reproduces the
   upstream wiki's stock layout.  What changed is the DEVICE.  The error is
   citing a PRE-PORT map against a POST-PORT device.  (PART 24 reached the same
   place from the user's description; the wiki is the citation it lacked.)

2. THE POST-PORT PARTITION MAP, MEASURED (not inferred)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   Read from /sys/class/block/mmcblk0p*/uevent PARTNAME on the live device:

       p1  fsc          1 KB        p9   rpm          512 KB
       p2  fsg          1.5 MB      p10  sbl1         512 KB
       p3  modem        64 MB       p11  tz           1 MB
       p4  modemst1     1.5 MB      p12  aboot        1 MB
       p5  modemst2     1.5 MB      p13  boot         64 MB
       p6  persist      32 MB       p14  rootfs       128 MB
       p7  sec          16 KB       p15  rootfs_data  3.3 GB
       p8  hyp          512 KB

   Fifteen partitions, not twenty-seven.  `rootfs`/`rootfs_data` do not exist in
   the stock table, so the GPT was REPLACED wholesale -- consistent with the
   wiki's "replace the entire partition table" strategy.  The EFS partitions
   (fsc/fsg/modemst1/modemst2) survive BY NAME with the same roles, which is
   what the user described ("required partitions are like EFS ... taken from
   original stock") and what PART 24 concluded.

   NOTE the wiki's repartition warning is about `persist`, NOT about EFS:
   "the WCNSS nv items file is stored in it, which needs to be loaded during
   runtime for functionality of WCN36xx(WiFi)".  `persist` is present (p6,
   32 MB) and WiFi works, so it was handled.

3. *** THE FINDING: tz AND hyp ARE NOT THE STOCK FIRMWARE ***
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The wiki, under "flash firmware and bootloader":

       "The original Android image contains firmware starting the kernel in
        32-bit mode.  In order to boot a mainline arm64 kernel, use firmwares
        from Dragon Board 410c as replacement.  Also, you may need qhypstub as
        well as lk1st ...
        The minimum firmwares needed to be replaced are: for uz801, both sbl1
        and tz; for other models, only tz.  It is also required to flash hyp."

   MEASURED, and the measurement OVERTURNED the assistant's first reading.

   (a) ELF headers -- they discriminate the 32-bit/64-bit question:

       tz   (p11)  class=ELFCLASS64  e_machine=0x0028 (EM_ARM)  entry=0x86500000
       hyp  (p8)   class=ELFCLASS64  e_machine=0x00B7 (EM_AARCH64) entry=0x86400000
       sbl1 (p10)  class=ELFCLASS32  e_machine=0x0028 (EM_ARM)  entry=0x08006650

       `tz` is 64-bit while `sbl1` is 32-bit.  This device is not a UZ801, so
       the wiki's rule says sbl1 is untouched -- and sbl1 measures 32-bit.

   (b) The assistant first compared the device's tz ELF header against the
       DragonBoard 410c `tz.mbn` and found the first 0x30 bytes BYTE-IDENTICAL,
       and concluded the substitution was confirmed.  **THAT CONCLUSION WAS
       WRONG AND IS WITHDRAWN.**  A byte-level comparison shows **368,454 of
       483,468 bytes differ (76.21 %), in 12,751 scattered regions** -- the
       signature of a different build, not a different signature on the same
       build.  Identical ELF headers here are a shared-layout coincidence.

   (c) Build strings settle it.  Both images carry `QC_IMAGE_VERSION_STRING`:

       device tz   : QC_IMAGE_VERSION_STRING=TZ.BF.3.0-00714
                     build stamp 170920145236Z / 170920145237Z  (2017-09-20)
                     also present: " Unknown chip_version", "WCN Version 0x%x",
                     "*****Calling rfChIdToFreqCoversion***", "Fatal Error: TZ Abort"
       DB410c 17.09: QC_IMAGE_VERSION_STRING=TZ.BF.3.0.R2-00068
                     also present: "UIE: Calling TZBSP PIL Auth Reset",
                     "counter: RPMB not prov, turning off version anti-rollback",
                     "tzbsp_es_get_partition_hash", "tz_counter"

       **Different branches (`TZ.BF.3.0` vs `TZ.BF.3.0.R2`).  The device is NOT
       running the image the wiki recommends.**  The wiki warns about exactly
       this class of error: "Please do NOT use a later release from linaro, the
       tz firmware becomes too large to flash to tz partition."

   (d) `hyp` IS qhypstub -- PROVEN, not inferred: the literal string
       **`qhypstub`** is present in the device's hyp image and absent from the
       DB410c `hyp.mbn`.  The device's hyp is 524,288 bytes against DB410c's
       57,344, and its first 57,344 bytes do not match (3ef6bcd1... vs
       1492b630...).  This matches the wiki's "qhypstub is strongly recommended
       as a replacement instead of the hyp firmware from db410c".

   (e) `sbl1` reports `QC_IMAGE_VERSION_STRING=BOOT.BF.3.0-00288` and is a
       32-bit ELF -- consistent with the wiki's rule that sbl1 is NOT replaced
       on non-UZ801 models, i.e. stock.

   SUMMARY TABLE:

       partition  on this device                stock?          verdict
       ---------  ---------------------------   -------------   -------------------
       sbl1       BOOT.BF.3.0-00288, 32-bit     likely STOCK    not a candidate
       hyp        qhypstub (literal string)     REPLACED        ** non-stock **
       tz         TZ.BF.3.0-00714 (2017-09-20)  UNKNOWN         ** non-DB410c **

   **WHY THIS STILL MATTERS -- the candidate is real even though the provenance
   is not yet pinned down:**

   The corpus's AP-hang work (Doc 159) located the hang in the tens of ms after
   `port failed halt`, and its leading-but-unproven candidate is **one of three
   untimed TrustZone `qcom_scm_assign_mem()` transfers**.  `qcom_scm_*` calls
   are served BY THE TZ FIRMWARE.  And `hyp` boots Linux in EL2, i.e. it is the
   layer the SCM calls cross.

   The stock Android device runs the **ZTE tz and the stock hyp**.  This OpenWrt
   device runs **qhypstub (proven)** and a **TZ build that is demonstrably not
   the DB410c 17.09 image**.  **Neither image has ever been examined anywhere in
   this corpus.**  Both are AP-side, so both are consistent with (and do not
   contradict) the byte-identical-modem result.

   **IT IS ALSO A DIFFERENCE THE ANDROID DIFFERENTIAL WOULD AUTOMATICALLY
   EXPOSE**, and unlike NV it is NOT derived from stock -- so it survives PART
   24's negative result.  Candidate list after PART 24 + PART 25:

       NV ................. REMOVED (derived from stock; not independent)
       modem firmware ..... REMOVED (byte-identical, all 21 segments)
       hyp firmware ....... ** CONFIRMED NON-STOCK (qhypstub) **
       TZ firmware ........ ** NON-DB410c; stock-vs-not UNRESOLVED **
       AP->modem control .. still open (PART 24's remaining candidate)

   **WHAT IS STILL NEEDED, and it is one file:** whether the device's tz
   (`TZ.BF.3.0-00714`) equals the STOCK ZTE tz.  That requires the stock image
   -- from the user's EDL backup (`edl rl zhihe_backup`, which the wiki
   recommends and whose existence is UNKNOWN -- asked, not assumed), or from a
   stock firmware package.  **Until that comparison is made, the tz is
   "different from the wiki's image", NOT "different from stock".**  The hyp
   finding does not depend on it: qhypstub is non-stock by construction.

   md5 of the on-device images, for a later comparison:
       tz   = 1f5e33f0730add07c1be51ce25e96562   (whole 1 MB partition)
       hyp  = 73326f4c54e86c55cf3beb779e2ea8d8   (whole 512 KB partition)
       sbl1 = 6ca5bd8ce7e680ae4d6b1d3f624f10c7   (whole 512 KB partition)

   The DB410c 17.09 zip was downloaded from the archive.org mirror the wiki
   names and its MD5SUMS verified (tz.mbn aef2d23e..., hyp.mbn 1492b630...,
   sbl1.mbn 6a9556a3...), so the reference side is trustworthy.

4. THE WIKI CORROBORATES THE BEARER-REBUILD FINDING INDEPENDENTLY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The wiki, on this device family:

       "This device also does not work with ModemManager, nor ofono, straight
        up crashing the latter."

   Measured here: **ModemManager IS running** on the OpenWrt port --
   `/usr/sbin/ModemManager --log-level=INFO` (pid 2530), plus
   `ModemManager-wrapper`, two `ModemManager-monitor` instances, `mmcli -M`, and
   `modem-bearer-watchdog`.

   This is an INDEPENDENT, EXTERNAL corroboration of the bearer-rebuild
   finding: the userspace stack the port relies on is one the community
   documents as unreliable on exactly this hardware.  It is not proof, but it
   moves "the data stall is userspace" from a local inference to a documented
   property of the family.

   The wiki's qmicli notes are likewise consistent with the corpus's QMI work:
   "qmicli seems to be randomly unable to receive status back from the modem
   ... 'error: operation failed: Transaction timed out'", "--wds-go-dormant /
   --wds-go-active doesn't do anything", "--wda-set-data-format=raw-ip ... It
   falsely returns success".  The corpus independently recorded a QRTR
   time-daemon blind spot and a DORMANT/quiesce question.

5. A FALSE ALARM OF THE ASSISTANT'S OWN, RECORDED BECAUSE IT IS THE SAME TRAP
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The assistant claimed "the dmesg_roll is broken -- 412 s stale" on boot
   `a5af0fc8` (roll's last line at uptime 18.95 s, device at 430 s) and "571 s
   stale" on boot `48f567c0`.  **BOTH CLAIMS WERE WRONG.**

   The check that disproves them is one line: compare the roll's last line
   against `dmesg`'s own last line.  On `a5af0fc8` both are
   `[18.954173] br-lan: port 2(usb0) entered forwarding state`, and the ring
   holds 332 lines while the roll holds its last 250.  **The writer is
   faithful; the KERNEL simply emitted nothing after 18.95 s.**  The uptime gap
   was kernel SILENCE, not writer staleness.

   This is the "read the definition, not the name" trap in a new place: the
   assistant inferred a broken writer from an uptime gap without reading the
   thing the writer was copying.  Recorded rather than quietly dropped.

   CONSEQUENCE FOR THE MID-SSR TEST: it is SOUND, for the reason that matters.
   A hang inside a fatal-triggered SSR leaves the fatal as the last line with no
   recovery after it, and the fatal is near the END of the ring, so it is always
   within the roll's last 250 lines.  The earlier worry that the test could
   silently excuse a hang does not apply.  (The roll's 250-line window IS a
   real bound, but the fatal sits inside it.)

   Also confirmed while checking: `printk` is `6 4 1 7`, so console_loglevel=6
   is in force and KERN_INFO is stored-but-not-consoled, as intended.

6. THE n >= 60 COUNTER IS CORRECTED FROM 25 TO 21 -- TWO BUGS OF OPPOSITE SIGN
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   PART 24 recorded "the n >= 60 counter is at 25/60".  **That was wrong.**  The
   correct value is **21**, and the way it went wrong is worth recording because
   the two errors CANCEL, which is the most dangerous way for a counter to be
   wrong.

   * Bug 1 (+4, over-count): the n >= 60 watcher was started as
     `watch813_v2.sh 21 60 ...` while boot C (`48f567c0`) was still LIVE with 4
     fatals.  But the 21 ALREADY INCLUDES boot C's 4 (PART 22: A 15 + B 2 + C 4
     = 21).  So `pooled = 21 + 4 = 25` counted boot C twice.  The correct prior
     was 17 -- which is exactly what the BAR watcher used ("pooled prior 17"),
     and it is why the bar fired correctly at 21 while the n=60 counter read 25.

   * Bug 2 (-4, under-count): on every re-baseline the watcher logs
     `pooled score stays at $((POOL_PRIOR+prev_fatals))` but never assigns
     `POOL_PRIOR`, so the next poll computes `pooled = POOL_PRIOR + 0` and the
     dead boot's fatals vanish.  Observed live: it printed "stays at 25/60" and
     the very next line read `pooled=21/60`.

   The two cancel AT THIS MOMENT (25 - 4 = 21, which is the true value), so the
   displayed 21 is RIGHT BY ACCIDENT.  They will not cancel in general: bug 1
   is a one-time startup error and bug 2 recurs at every boot boundary, so the
   count drifts DOWN from here.

   Both are fixed in `scratch/watch813_v3.sh`: the prior is now persisted in a
   state file so a restart cannot re-add a live boot's fatals, and every
   re-baseline BANKS the dead boot's fatals before re-reading.

   This is the standing statistics rule's failure mode in miniature: the number
   the protocol reports was not the number the protocol had counted.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------


PART 26 -- THE USER SUPPLIES A CROSS-DEVICE CONTROL THAT RE-FRAMES PART 25:
           THE UFI001B, WITH THE SAME tz AND qhypstub, HAS NO CRASH AND NO
           DATA STALL -- SO THE FIRMWARE SUBSTITUTION IS NOT THE SOLE CAUSE
           (2026-09-22 00:02 local / 18:32 UTC)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

0. WHAT THE USER SAID, VERBATIM
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   "my device is HMUF02-v5 but shortened to hmu05, also ufi001b modem works
    perfectly without any crash or data stall in ufi001b device with same tz
    and qthyp"

   Two facts in one message:

   (a) NAMING: this device is an **HMUF02-v5**, which the user has been
       shortening to "hmu05" throughout.  The postmarketOS wiki lists a
       "HMUF02 V5" (in Ryjelsum's user entry: "the undocumented HMUF02 V5,
       lk2nd/lk1st PMOS works sans LEDs when built for UFI001c").  So
       "hmu05" in the corpus == HMUF02-v5, a real but less-documented
       member of the family.  Recorded so future citations do not treat
       "hmu05" as a separate model.

   (b) CONTROL: the **UFI001B device** running OpenWrt, with the SAME `tz`
       and the SAME `qhypstub`, has **no crash and no data stall**.

1. WHAT THIS CONTROL ELIMINATES, AND IT IS IMMEDIATE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   PART 25 raised the tz / qhypstub substitution as a NEW, UNEXAMINED candidate
   for the 902 s fatal and the AP hang.  **The UFI001B control weakens that
   candidate from "likely" to "not sufficient":**

       HMU05 + OpenWrt (this tz + qhypstub)   -> 902 s fatal + data stall
       UFI001B + OpenWrt (SAME tz + qhyp)    -> NO crash, NO stall

   The AP-side firmware is the SAME on both, yet only the HMU05 crashes.  So
   the tz/qhyp substitution **cannot be the sole cause** -- a sole cause would
   crash both.  It could still be a NECESSARY cofactor (the crash might need
   the OpenWrt tz AND the HMU05 modem), but it is not SUFFICIENT, and the
   Android-vs-OpenWrt differential already covers the AP side more directly.

   **The candidate list after PART 26:**

       NV ................. REMOVED (PART 24: derived from stock)
       modem firmware ..... REMOVED for Android-vs-OpenWrt (byte-identical),
                            BUT the HMU05 modem firmware DIFFERS from the
                            UFI001B's (different RF: WTR1605 vs WTR4905) --
                            so the MODEM-SIDE difference between HMU05 and
                            UFI001B is back on the table as the device-side
                            differentiator
       hyp/qhypstub ....... WEAKENED to "not sufficient" (same on UFI001B)
       tz ................. WEAKENED to "not sufficient" (same on UFI001B)
       AP->modem control .. STILL OPEN, and now the LEADING candidate

2. THE TWO DIFFERENTIALS INTERSECT, AND THEY NARROW THE CAUSE SHARPLY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   We now have TWO independent differentials, and the cause must lie in their
   INTERSECTION:

       D1 (Android-vs-OpenWrt, SAME modem):
           HMU05 + Android AP    -> no crash
           HMU05 + OpenWrt AP    -> 902 s fatal + stall
           => the cause is AP-side (and the modem firmware is byte-identical)

       D2 (HMU05-vs-UFI001B, SAME tz+qhyp, SAME OpenWrt kernel):
           HMU05  + OpenWrt AP   -> 902 s fatal + stall
           UFI001B + OpenWrt AP   -> no crash, no stall
           => the cause involves the MODEM (the two devices differ in modem
              firmware and RF transceiver)

   The cause must satisfy BOTH: it is something the OpenWrt AP does that the
   Android AP does NOT, AND something the HMU05 modem is sensitive to that the
   UFI001B modem is NOT.

   The corpus's bam_dmux differential (Doc 170 s8.16 / the Android-kernel
   comparison) sits EXACTLY at that intersection:
       -- OpenWrt bam_dmux gives the modem's pc-ack 250 ms; Android gives 2000 ms.
       -- that is an AP-side behaviour that differs (satisfies D1)
       -- and it targets the modem's power-collapse ack path, which is
          modem-firmware-specific (satisfies D2: the HMU05 modem's WTR1605 path
          may race under 250 ms where the UFI001B's WTR4905 path does not)

   So the UFI001B control does not merely weaken the tz candidate; it
   STRENGTHENS the bam_dmux pc-ack-timeout hypothesis, which was already scoped
   (s8.16: "a LATENCY defect, not a stability one -- it does NOT touch the
   902 s fatal").  That scoping note now needs revisiting: if the HMU05 modem
   treats the 250 ms timeout as fatal where the UFI001B does not, the latency
   defect CAN become a stability defect on the HMU05 specifically.

3. THE CONTROL IS CONFIRMED VALID -- AND IT IS STRONGER THAN FIRST READ
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The assistant flagged a caveat: "no crash" is decisive only if it means the
   UFI001B ran past the ~900 s boundary, and not if it meant a failed
   transplant.  **The user has now resolved it, and the strong reading holds:**

       "i have two device one is ufi001b and other one is hmu05, ufi001b has
        upgraded modem firmware and runs fine on ufi001b device without any
        crash for HOURS, so i thought why not try ufi001b modem firmware on
        hmu05, but it was a failure as hmu05 firmware is written in c and has
        different RF transceiver but ufi001b firmware is written in c++"

   So:
     (a) TWO PHYSICAL DEVICES, each running its OWN modem firmware, with the
         same OpenWrt AP-side stack (tz + qhypstub + kernel).
     (b) The UFI001B runs **for HOURS** without a crash.  The HMU05 faults at
         ~902 s (~15 min).  "Hours" is 4x+ the fault period, so the UFI001B
         does not merely miss the boundary -- it passes it repeatedly.
     (c) The transplant attempt FAILED, and for the reason the corpus already
         records independently: different RF transceiver (WTR4905 vs WTR1605)
         and disjoint transceiver driver sets.  The user's experiment therefore
         CORROBORATES the corpus's "transplant could not work" finding.
     (d) The C (HMU05) vs C++ (UFI001B) detail is a source-language observation
         about the two vendor firmware trees.  It is NOT causal on its own and
         is recorded as an observation, not as a mechanism.

   **THE CONTROL IS THEREFORE VALID AND DECISIVE FOR WHAT IT CLAIMS:** the same
   AP-side firmware, the same kernel, two different modems, and only the HMU05
   faults.  D2 stands.  The tz/qhyp candidate stays WEAKENED.

4. A PRACTICAL LEAD THE USER'S MESSAGE OPENS, RECORDED BUT NOT PURSUED
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

   The user says the UFI001B is running **UPGRADED** modem firmware.  That
   raises an obvious question the corpus has never asked: **is there an upgraded
   HMU05 / HMUF02-v5 modem firmware, and does it fix the 902 s fatal?**

   This is NOT the forbidden "binary-patch the baseband" route -- it is a
   vendor firmware update, which is a legitimate input, and the UFI001B is
   direct evidence that an upgraded image can run crash-free on this family.
   Two cautions before it is treated as a plan:

     * The corpus's standing directive is to solve the fault WITHOUT patching
       the modem firmware, and the corpus has already been burned once by a
       FABRICATED patch guide (see the trust index).  A vendor image is
       different in kind from a hand patch, but the provenance must be verified
       byte-for-byte before use.
     * The HMU05 modem firmware hashes IDENTICAL on Android and OpenWrt
       (all 21 segments), so whatever the fault is, Android tolerates it with
       the SAME modem image -- which means an upgrade is a WORKAROUND, not an
       explanation.  It would still be worth knowing whether one exists.

   Status: asked as a question, not investigated.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

                            HMU05-vs-UFI001B (WTR1605 vs WTR4905)
       AP->modem control .. still the leading candidate

   ALSO ESTABLISHED, and it changes what is SAFE: the user HAS a full stock dump

   PROCESS NOTE, recorded because it is the same failure as PART 25 s4/s5: the

================================================================================
PART 28 -- THE ANDROID AP-SIDE CONTROL IS MEASURED: THE pc-ack BUDGET HAS 39x
           HEADROOM, SO s8.16's 250 ms TIMEOUTS ARE CATEGORICAL, NOT A LATENCY
           TAIL -- AND THE PROPOSED 2000 ms A/B IS NOW PREDICTED TO REMOVE ONLY
           THE SSR-WINDOW SUBSET, NOT THE BULK
================================================================================

0. WHY THIS PART EXISTS

   s8.16 left a falsifier and a minimal comparative test:

     "FALSIFIER: if the `pc_state wait timeout` string never appears in dmesg
      while `pc_timeout_count` rises, the 250 ms ack wait is the only failing
      wait, and widening it to Android's 2000 ms is the minimal comparative
      test.  If BOTH strings appear, the fix is not a constant."

   The user then flashed stock Android onto the HMU05 to obtain the AP-side
   control, and asked for the AP-side bam_dmux instrumentation.  That control is
   now measured.  This PART reports it and re-scores the candidate.

   The comparison is only meaningful if the two paths are the same protocol
   step.  They are:

     Android  ul_wakeup()              bam_dmux.c:1840   (data-driven, on demand)
              INIT_COMPLETION(ul_wakeup_ack_completion)
              power_vote(1)                                    :1921-1922
              wait_for_completion_timeout(..., 2000 ms)         :1924-1925
              wait_for_completion_timeout(bam_connection_... )  :1932-1933

     OpenWrt  bam_dmux_runtime_resume()  qcom_bam_dmux.c:2049 (PM-driven)
              reinit_completion(&pc_ack_completion)             :2059
              bam_dmux_pc_vote(dmux, true)                      :2062
              wait_for_completion_timeout(..., 250 ms)          :2066-2067
              wait_event_timeout(pc_wait, pc_state, 1000 ms)    :2075-2076

   Both: clear the completion, set the SMSM pc bit, wait for the modem's ack.
   The trigger differs; the handshake step does not.  So the latency of that one
   step is comparable across the two.

   SOP COMPLIANCE (standing rule, 2026-09-20): this PART is EVIDENCE, not a
   patch.  Nothing was flashed, no modem firmware was touched, no driver was
   rebuilt for this measurement -- the Android instrumentation was already live
   (debugfs), so no kernel build was required.  The comparative protocol's
   "verify against ground truth before acting" step is satisfied by reading the
   Android driver's own DEFINITION of the wait (bam_dmux.c:1909-1938) rather
   than inferring it from the log.  The sample-size pre-registration is in s4.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. THE INSTRUMENT, AND THE ONE PARSE TRAP IT SET

   Source:   /d/ipc_logging/bam_dmux/log_cont     (Android debugfs, already live)
   Reader:   `log_cont` is the BLOCKING/continuous node, which is the correct
             one -- `log` is the consuming reader.  Confirmed from
             kernel/trace/ipc_logging_debug.c (create_ctx_debugfs()).
   Capture:  scratch/android_capture/bam_dmux.log      (host-held adb shell)
   Analyzer: scratch/android_capture/analyze_android_dmux.py
   Output:   scratch/android_capture/android_dmux_analysis.txt

   `dmesg` and `logcat -b kernel` are BOTH blocked by SELinux on this build even
   as root ("klogctl: Operation not permitted"), so the IPC logs are the SOLE
   AP-side source.  That is fine -- they are the better instrument anyway.

   *** TRAP, AND I WALKED INTO IT IN THE FIRST MINUTE. ***
   The kernel format string is (bam_dmux.c:337):

       "<DMUX> %c%c%c%c %c%c%c%c%d "

   -- the 8 state characters are split by a SPACE into two tokens, with the
   on-demand vote count glued to the second.  A line reads

       [  252.751697228] <DMUX> drpa vuWA0 ul_wakeup waiting for wakeup ack

   My first regex captured ONE state token, so the "message" came out as
   "vuWA0 ul_wakeup waiting for wakeup ack" and every startswith("ul_wakeup")
   test failed.  The analyzer then reported "no samples" for every metric --
   which looks like "the event never happened" rather than "the parser is
   wrong".  Fixed by reading the format string at :337.

   This is the SAME trap as s8.16's self-correction (GPIO-vs-SMSM) and the
   corpus's standing rule: read the DEFINITION, not the name -- and when a
   measurement returns zero, suspect the parser before believing the zero.
   A "zero" produced by a broken parser is indistinguishable from a real zero
   unless you have a positive control.  The positive control here is s3.

   STATE CHARACTER ORDER (bam_dmux.c:320-331):
     [D a2_pc_disabled][R in_global_reset][P power_state][A connection_active]
     [V uplink_vote][U is_connected][W wait_for_ack][A ack_completion.done]
     + on-demand vote count

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

2. THE SECOND TRAP: "timeout" IN THIS LOG USUALLY IS NOT A TIMEOUT

   A naive `grep -c timeout bam_dmux.log` returns 120+ hits, and they are
   ALMOST ALL THE GOOD PATH:

       [ 253.748061085] drPA VUwA0 ul_timeout: pkt written 1

   `ul_timeout()` (bam_dmux.c:1776) is the UL_TIMEOUT_DELAY=1000 ms recovery
   work.  When it finds the packet WAS written it logs "pkt written 1" and
   re-arms (:1805-1810).  It is a SUCCESS report.  Reading it as a timeout would
   manufacture ~120 phantom failures out of a clean boot.

   The three REAL ack-wait timeouts are the strings at:

       :1917  "ul_wakeup timeout previous ack"   (wait #1, 2000 ms)
       :1928  "ul_wakeup timeout wakeup ack"     (wait #2, 2000 ms)  <-- the pc-ack
       :1936  "ul_wakeup timeout power on"       (wait #3, 2000 ms)

   The analyzer now matches ONLY those three.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

3. RESULT 1 -- ZERO REAL ACK-WAIT TIMEOUTS

   In 1271 s of uptime spanning 55 UL wakeups:

       "timeout previous ack" : 0
       "timeout wakeup ack"   : 0
       "timeout power on"     : 0

   Android never hit the pc-ack timeout.  Not once.

   And the positive control that the parser works: it DOES find the real events
   -- 55 "waiting for wakeup ack", 55 "ul_wakeup complete", 54 "ul_powerdown",
   55 paired acks.  So the zero is a real zero, not a parser artefact.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

4. RESULT 2 -- THE LATENCY DISTRIBUTION, AND THE PRE-REGISTRATION IT SCORES

   PRE-REGISTERED BEFORE COMPUTING (stated so it cannot be fitted afterwards):

     P-A  "the constant is too small" (Reading L): the healthy Android ack
          distribution should have a HEAVY TAIL -- events in the tens-to-
          hundreds of ms, with occasional samples past 250 ms.  A 250 ms budget
          is only "too small" if the ack sometimes lands near or beyond it.

     P-B  "the failure is categorical" (Reading S): the distribution should be
          TIGHT AND UNIMODAL, with 250 ms far out in the tail, and no sample
          anywhere near it.

     FALSIFIER for P-B: any single Android event > 250 ms, or p99 > 50 ms.

   MEASURED (vote -> modem's ack; n = 55):

       min   0.066 ms
       p50   2.033 ms
       p90   4.211 ms
       p99   6.332 ms
       MAX   6.340 ms
       mean  2.153 ms

       > 250 ms : 0        (OpenWrt's budget)
       >  10 ms : 0
       >   5 ms : 4

       HEADROOM vs OpenWrt's 250 ms : 39.4x
       HEADROOM vs Android's 2000 ms:  315x

   The OTHER TWO directions, for completeness:

     previous-down-ack serialization (Android-only; OpenWrt has no such step)
       n=54  min 0.014  p50 0.032  p90 0.088  p99 0.234  max 0.375 ms
       -> it ALWAYS falls through instantly, exactly as the source comment at
          :1905-1907 predicts ("chances are it already came in and this will
          just fall through instead of waiting").  So the serialization OpenWrt
          lacks is NOT a latency cost; its value is the GUARANTEE that the prior
          down-ack has arrived, not the time it saves.

     down-vote -> ack (the mirror direction)
       n=54  min 0.091  p50 0.233  p90 0.587  p99 0.858  max 1.035 ms

   => **P-B IS SUPPORTED AND P-A IS FALSIFIED.**  The healthy Android handshake
      is a tight, unimodal, sub-7 ms distribution in BOTH directions.  250 ms is
      39.4x the worst ack Android ever produced.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

5. RESULT 3 -- n IS NOT AN ACCIDENT: THE WHOLE CYCLE IS CAUSALLY EXPLAINED

   This matters because "55 samples" is only meaningful if each sample is a real
   handshake, and if we know what produced it.

   `ul_timeout()` (bam_dmux.c:1789-1814) re-arms itself every UL_TIMEOUT_DELAY =
   1000 ms while `ul_packet_written || ul_ondemand_vote`, and calls
   `ul_powerdown()` when both are zero.  Measured, over 54 complete cycles:

       (ticks in cycle, ticks reporting "pkt written 1")   cycles
       (3, 3)                                                24
       (1, 1)                                                23
       (2, 2)                                                 5
       (6, 6)                                                 2

     -- in EVERY cycle the two numbers are EQUAL, i.e. every logged tick carried
        a packet, and the powerdown tick is the SILENT one (the else branch at
        :1812 has no log line).

       UL hold duration (complete -> powerdown):
         min 1.990 s   p50 3.005 s   max 6.999 s
         = exactly (ticks + 1) x 1.000 s:  3 ticks -> 4.0 s, 1 -> 2.0 s,
           2 -> 3.0 s, 6 -> 7.0 s.

   So the soak's `ping -c 3; sleep 27` burst delivers its packets one per second,
   each setting `ul_packet_written`, and the link powers down 1 s after the last
   one.  The causal chain from workload to handshake is CLOSED: 55 ping bursts
   -> 55 UL wakeups -> 55 pc-ack handshakes.  n is a counted denominator.

   CADENCE (recorded, and see s7 for why it is NOT the 10x the s8.16 table
   might be read to imply):
     55 UL wakeups in 1268 s = 2.60 wakeups/min
     inter-cycle: min 2.00, p50 29.09, max 114.29 s  (the 114 s gap is a lull)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

6. THE POWER STATEMENT -- WHAT n = 55 DOES AND DOES NOT EXCLUDE

   The OpenWrt baseline is NOT a single number.  Two boots in this corpus:

     Doc 148 boot (3322 s, 3 fatals)  pc_timeout_count 2  / 202 resumes = 0.99 %
     Doc 170 V boot (6118 s, 8 fatals) pc_timeout_count 44 / 766 resumes = 5.7 %

   A 5.8x spread -- the rate is NOT stationary.  So the comparison must be made
   against BOTH, and stated as a bound rather than a verdict:

     P(0 timeouts in 55 | rate = 5.7 %) = (1 - 0.057)^55 = 0.041   -> SIGNIFICANT
     P(0 timeouts in 55 | rate = 0.99 %) = (1 - 0.0099)^55 = 0.58  -> NOT

   n needed for P(0) < 0.05:
     at 5.7 %  ->  51 wakeups  (ALREADY MET, ~19 min)
     at 2 %    -> 148 wakeups  (~56 min)
     at 1 %    -> 298 wakeups  (~113 min)

   *** PRE-REGISTERED NOW, BEFORE THE DATA: the Android soak must reach n >= 300
   UL wakeups (~2 h at 2.60/min) before any claim is made about the 1 % regime.
   Until then the honest statement is exactly: "Android excludes a rate of 5.7 %
   at p < 0.05; it does NOT yet exclude 1 %." ***

   AND THE LIMIT THAT NO AMOUNT OF n WILL FIX:

     Android had ZERO fatals in this boot.  Therefore this control CANNOT
     measure a post-fatal phenomenon AT ALL -- it bounds the HEALTHY regime only.
     The corpus's own placement of the OpenWrt timeouts is in the ABNORMAL one:

       V s5.4      3 of the first 4 pc-ack timeouts are +0.43..0.58 s AFTER a fatal
       s8.21.1     6 of 12 fatals produce one INSIDE their own SSR down-window,
                   where the modem provably cannot ack

     So the comparison is: Android-healthy (0 in 55) vs OpenWrt-healthy (unknown,
     but the Doc 148 boot at 0.99 % is the nearest healthy proxy) vs
     OpenWrt-post-fatal (the bulk of the 44).  Only the first two are comparable.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

7. THE DELIVERABLE -- THIS RE-SCOPES THE 250 ms ITEM

   The arithmetic that matters:

     OpenWrt's budget ............ 250 ms
     Android's worst healthy ack . 6.340 ms
     ratio ....................... 39.4x

   A budget that is 39x the worst value the healthy path has ever produced does
   not expire 1-5.7 % of the time by being SLIGHTLY too small.  A latency tail
   that reaches 39x the observed maximum would be a phenomenon of its own, and
   Android's n=55 shows no trace of it (p99 = 6.332 ms, i.e. the 99th percentile
   is 1.001x the maximum -- the distribution is COMPRESSED, not heavy-tailed).

   THEREFORE:

     * The OpenWrt pc-ack failures are CATEGORICAL, not marginal.  In those
       events the ack is not "a bit late" -- it does not come.  That is
       consistent with the modem being mid-SSR / mid-firmware-reload, which is
       exactly where s5.4 and s8.21.1 place them.

     * s8.16's minimal comparative test -- widen 250 ms to Android's 2000 ms --
       is now predicted to remove ONLY THE SSR-WINDOW SUBSET, not the bulk.

       This is a REFINEMENT, not a reversal.  s8.21.1 already PROVED that 6
       events are of the "the modem needed >250 ms but <2000 ms" kind: they sit
       inside the SSR down-window and the mpss load is only 0.556 s, well inside
       2000 ms.  Widening SHOULD fix those.  But they are 6 of 44 in the V boot,
       so the other ~38 have no explanation at all yet -- and s5.4's four early
       events (3 of them +0.43..0.58 s post-fatal) are a different, tighter
       cluster again.

       PRE-REGISTERED PREDICTION FOR THE A/B (this is Task #98, currently BLOCKED
       until the HMU05 returns to OpenWrt):

         P1  widen 250 -> 2000 ms; `pc_timeout_count` per suspend cycle drops by
             roughly the SSR-window count (~1 per fatal) and NOT to zero.
             i.e. a PARTIAL drop is the prediction, and the residual should
             still sit in the 0.5-5 % band.

         P2  FALSIFIER, two-sided:
               - if it collapses to ~0, Reading L wins wholesale: the constant
                 WAS the lever, the "categorical" reading is wrong, and this
                 PART is FALSIFIED.
               - if it does not drop AT ALL, then even the s8.21.1 SSR-window
                 subset was not a latency effect, which would contradict
                 s8.21.1's own mpss-load arithmetic and must be re-read.

       Stating P2 is the point: the prediction is falsifiable in BOTH directions,
       and the A/B is still worth running BECAUSE it can falsify me.  It is a
       cheap test with a live falsifier, not a fishing expedition.

     * It does NOT change s8.16's other scoping: OpenWrt LOGS the 250 ms timeout
       and then PROCEEDS to the 1000 ms pc_state wait, so even when it fires it
       is a latency defect, not a stability one.  Nothing here touches the 902 s
       fatal.  The item stays off the stability candidate list.

   SCOPE CORRECTION TO THE s8.16 TABLE, recorded so a row is not over-read later:
   the "vote policy" row (Android reference-counted/on-demand vs OpenWrt
   unconditional-per-resume) is a REAL design difference, but it does NOT produce
   a large EXPOSURE difference under comparable load:

     Android        55 in 1268 s  = 2.60 /min
     OpenWrt Doc148 202 in 3322 s = 3.65 /min
     OpenWrt V boot 766 in 6118 s = 7.51 /min

   All the same order of magnitude (the workloads differ, so this is NOT a
   controlled rate comparison -- it is only enough to say "not 10x").

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

WHAT PART 28 CHANGES

  - The Android AP-side control s8.16 asked for is MEASURED, not assumed:
    0 real ack-wait timeouts in 1271 s / 55 wakeups; vote->ack max 6.340 ms,
    p99 6.332 ms, p50 2.033 ms; down-ack max 1.035 ms; previous-ack
    serialization max 0.375 ms (always falls through).
  - 250 ms is 39.4x the worst healthy Android ack => P-B (categorical failure)
    supported, P-A (latency tail) falsified.
  - A NEW PRE-REGISTRATION: the proposed 250->2000 ms A/B is predicted to remove
    only the SSR-WINDOW SUBSET (~1 increment per fatal, per s8.21.1's own mpss-
    load arithmetic), NOT the bulk.  A collapse to ~0 falsifies this PART; a
    ZERO drop would falsify s8.21.1 instead.
  - The soak's n is a COUNTED denominator, causally closed to the ping bursts
    (UL hold = (ticks+1) x 1.000 s, exact, 54/54 cycles).
  - n >= 300 wakeups (~2 h) pre-registered before any claim about the 1 % regime.
  - Two parse traps recorded: the two-token state prefix (bam_dmux.c:337), and
    "ul_timeout: pkt written 1" being a SUCCESS report, not a timeout.

WHAT PART 28 DOES NOT CLAIM

  - It does NOT say the pc-ack timeout is harmless.  It says it is a LATENCY
    defect whose observed events are categorical (modem not answering), so the
    constant is not the fix.
  - It does NOT establish the OpenWrt healthy-regime rate.  n=55 excludes 5.7 %
    and nothing smaller.
  - It does NOT compare like-for-like on workload: Android ran `ping -c 3` every
    30 s; the OpenWrt soaks did not.  The cadence numbers are recorded as
    "same order of magnitude", not as a controlled measurement.
  - It does NOT touch the 902 s fatal, the userspace bearer rebuild, or the
    n >= 60 AP-hang soak.  Those are unaffected.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

================================================================================
PART 29 -- ANDROID'S AP SIDE EXPOSES AN RPM OBSERVABILITY LAYER OPENWRT DOES NOT
           BUILD -- INCLUDING A DIRECT, PER-SUBSYSTEM MEASUREMENT OF THE MODEM'S
           OWN POWER-COLLAPSE RATE, WHICH WAS PREVIOUSLY ONLY ESTIMABLE BY
           COUNTING DIAG MESSAGES ACROSS TWO INCOMPARABLE TRANSPORTS
================================================================================

0. WHY THIS PART EXISTS

   PART 28 measured the bam_dmux handshake and closed that item.  The remaining
   live question is the intersection of the two differentials: the failure needs
   BOTH the OpenWrt AP AND the HMU05 modem.  The subsystem that connects them is
   the RPM -- the modem's Q6 PC vote is the "rpm" LPR (q6pcvote = rpm LPR
   +0x18) and the chain stalls in `rpm.sync` (0xc08bebd0), whose flush loops have
   no timeout.  And the RPM's "client 0", which appears in a 1.3 s window at the
   fatal (2/2), is not the modem.

   So the AP's RPM behaviour is the thing to compare.  This PART records what the
   Android AP side can observe that OpenWrt cannot, and the baseline it gives.

   SOP COMPLIANCE (standing rule): EVIDENCE, not a patch.  Nothing was flashed;
   no modem firmware was touched.  The Android measurements use only debugfs and
   tracefs that were already present.  The comparative protocol is satisfied by
   reading each driver's DEFINITION (rpm_master_stats.c, qcom_stats.c, rpm_log.c,
   bam_dmux.c) before quoting any number, and by recording the OpenWrt side's
   state as `not set` in the built .config rather than assuming.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. THE ROOT PRECONDITION: THE ANDROID SHELL IS uid 2000, NOT ROOT

   This cost real time and is worth one paragraph.  `adb shell id` returns

       uid=2000(shell) gid=2000(shell) ... context=u:r:shell:s0

   and in that state most of what follows is invisible: `devmem` gives
   "can't open '/dev/mem': Permission denied", ftrace `enable` files give
   "can't create ...: Permission denied", and writing the SELinux knob gives
   "Permission denied" -- all of which look like kernel policy but are simply
   "you are not root".

   `adb root` returned NO OUTPUT AND DID NOT WORK.  The recipe that does work is
   the one already recorded in the corpus (Stock_Android_Live/01 s9):

       adb shell 'setprop service.adb.root 1; busybox killall adbd'
       sleep 6 && adb wait-for-device && adb shell id     # uid=0(root)

   Consequence to remember: the recipe KILLS EVERY RUNNING CAPTURE (adbd
   restarts).  The pre-root captures were preserved as
   scratch/android_capture/*_pre_root.log before re-rooting.

   SECOND TRAP, walked into again: `pkill -f "rpm_master_stats"` MATCHED ITS OWN
   SHELL and killed it before the target.  The corpus already warns about
   `pkill -f`; it is still true.  Kill by explicit PID from
   `ps -eo pid,args | grep "[a]db shell"`.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

2. WHAT THE ANDROID AP SIDE EXPOSES

   All of the following exist on the stock Android 4.4.4 (kernel 3.10.28) build
   with NO instrumentation and NO kernel build:

     /d/rpm_master_stats                     per-master APSS/MPSS/PRONTO stats
     /d/rpm_stats                             RPM low-power MODE stats + votes
     /d/rpm_send_msg/message                  a WRITE interface to the RPM
     /d/rpm_log                               the RPM ulog, as a streaming reader
     /sys/kernel/debug/tracing/events/rpm_smd/rpm_send_message
     /sys/kernel/debug/tracing/events/rpm_smd/rpm_ack_recd
     /sys/kernel/debug/tracing/events/rpm/*   device runtime-PM events
     /sys/kernel/debug/clk/rpm_debug_mux
     /sys/kernel/debug/ipc_logging/{smem,smp2p,smd,smd_pkt,ipc_router,...}
     /d/bam_dmux/{stats,tbl,ul_pkt_cnt}       (PART 28)

   The two ftrace tracepoints are the most valuable, because they are DECODED.
   `rpm_send_message` (emitted at drivers/soc/qcom/rpm-smd.c:1141 and :433) prints

     ctx:%s set:%s rsc_type:0x%08x(%s), rsc_id:0x%08x, id:%d

   -- i.e. the resource NAME, not an opaque id -- and `rpm_ack_recd` prints the
   matching `id`, so the AP->RPM ROUND TRIP is measurable per message.

   OpenWrt's 6.12 kernel has NONE of the rpm_smd tracepoints (checked:
   no TRACE_EVENT(rpm_send_message)/rpm_ack_recd in drivers/soc/qcom/, and
   include/trace/events/ has only rpm.h).  So this tracepoint pair is an
   Android-only instrument.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

3. MEASURED -- THE AP -> RPM ROUND TRIP ON ANDROID IS ALWAYS SUB-MILLISECOND

   45 s window, both tracepoints enabled, n = 142 sends / 142 acks
   (0 unmatched):

     min   109.0 us
     p50   139.5 us
     p90   603.0 us
     p99   614.0 us
     MAX   617.0 us
     mean  216.5 us

     >  1 ms : 0
     > 10 ms : 0
     >100 ms : 0
     >  1 s  : 0

   **Zero events above 1 ms.**  The AP's RPM handshake never stalls in this
   window.  (The 45 s window is short; a longer capture is running, and this
   number is recorded as "n=142, one window", not as a stationary property.)

   RATE AND SHAPE:
     AP RPM send rate ...... 3.28/s (196.8/min)
     burstiness ............ 95.4% of 100 ms bins are EMPTY (max 24 sends in one
                             100 ms bin) -- the AP's RPM traffic is bursty, not
                             continuous
     inter-send gap ........ p50 0.285 ms, but max 13.378 s

   RESOURCE MIX (the names are the tracepoint's own decode):

       count  rsc_type   rsc_ids seen
          55  bslv       0x00(49), 0x2d, 0x84, 0x18      (bus slave)
          28  clk1       0x01(26), 0x00
          25  clk2       0x00(25)
          24  smpa       0x01(24)                        (SMPS regulator)
           6  bmas       0x1d, 0x65, 0x03                (BAM slave)
           4  ldoa       0x08(3), 0x05                   (LDO regulator)

   SENDER TASK -- the dominant AP RPM client is the DISPLAY:

       mdss_fb0        72   <- 51% of all AP RPM traffic
       kworker/3:1     24
       kworker/0:1     20
       kworker/u8:*    22
       irq/170-7824900  4

   This matters for the comparison: OpenWrt is a headless router with no
   `mdss_fb0`, so the AP's RPM resource mix on OpenWrt CANNOT be assumed similar.
   The comparison has to be made, not inferred.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

4. THE DELIVERABLE -- THE MODEM'S OWN POWER-COLLAPSE RATE, IN ONE `cat`

   `/d/rpm_master_stats` prints, per RPM master, the RPM firmware's own counters:

     APSS  shutdown_req / wakeup_ind / bringup_req / bringup_ack /
           last_sleep_transition_duration / last_wake_transition_duration /
           wakeup_reason / numshutdowns / active_cores
     MPSS      ... the same fields, for the MODEM
     PRONTO    ... the same fields, for WiFi/BT

   MEASURED on Android, THREE independent windows:

     MPSS  numshutdowns  3104 -> 3252  in 113 s  =  1.310 /s
     MPSS  numshutdowns  3024 -> 3103  in  52 s  =  1.523 /s
     MPSS  numshutdowns  3338 -> 3492  in  90 s  =  1.711 /s   (clean 30 s sampler)
     APSS  numshutdowns  35737 -> 35737 in 113 s =  0.000 /s
     APSS  numshutdowns  35737 -> 35737 in  90 s =  0.000 /s
     PRONTO numshutdowns    11 -> 11    in 113 s =  0.000 /s

     MPSS  active_cores = 0x0   (the modem is power-collapsed at sample time)
     APSS  active_cores = 0x1
     MPSS  last_sleep_transition_duration 0x2fd8, last_wake_transition_duration 0x4f6a

   => Android's modem RPM power-collapse rate is **1.31-1.71 /s over three
      windows** (mean ~1.5 /s), and the counter is FLAT for APSS and PRONTO
      throughout, so it isolates the modem cleanly.

   Note the APSS/PRONTO counters are FLAT while the AP is awake with adb and the
   display on -- only the modem's counter advances.  That is exactly the
   quantity of interest, and it is now separable from the AP's own sleep.

   WHY THIS IS THE DELIVERABLE AND NOT JUST ANOTHER COUNTER: the modem's
   power-collapse cadence is the single most-cited number in this whole
   investigation --

       corpus (Doc 02 s2):   Android 916 MCPM cycles / 488 s = 1.88 /s
                             OpenWrt 100 MCPM cycles / 121 s = 0.83 /s

   -- and the corpus DISMISSED that 2.3x gap in the same paragraph it reported
   it: "The lower OpenWrt rate is attributable to the 30x lower DIAG capture
   rate (rpmsg vs USB transport), not a behavioural difference."

   That dismissal is now testable rather than arguable.  Per DIAG RECORD the
   two captures are 0.00154 (Android) and 0.0206 (OpenWrt) MCPM messages -- so
   OpenWrt's capture carries 13x MORE MCPM per record, which is not what a
   transport that merely drops frames would look like.  Whether the 2.3x rate
   gap is real or an artefact has therefore been an open question this whole
   time, and it is the one that decides whether the modem behaves differently
   under the two AP stacks.

   `rpm_master_stats` answers it directly, from the RPM's own counters, with the
   SAME code path on both platforms, and needs no DIAG transport, no mask work,
   and no interpretation of two different log formats.  Android's baseline is
   now 1.31-1.52 /s (n=2 windows).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

5. THE OPENWRT GAP, AND HOW SMALL IT IS TO CLOSE

   The driver IS IN THE OPENWRT TREE:

     openwrt/build_dir/.../linux-6.12.94/drivers/soc/qcom/rpm_master_stats.c
     drivers/soc/qcom/Makefile:21:  obj-$(CONFIG_QCOM_RPM_MASTER_STATS) += rpm_master_stats.o

   but the build has it OFF:

     .config:4069:  # CONFIG_QCOM_RPM_MASTER_STATS is not set

   and it is **tristate**, so it can be built as a LOADABLE MODULE -- no kernel
   flash, the same deployment class as patch 823.

   WHAT ELSE IS NEEDED, and this is the only non-trivial part: a DT node.
   `master_stats_probe()` (rpm_master_stats.c:68-135) does

       count = of_property_count_strings(dev->of_node, "qcom,master-names");
       msgram_np = of_parse_phandle(dev->of_node, "qcom,rpm-msg-ram", i);
       of_address_to_resource(msgram_np, 0, &res);
       data[i].base = devm_ioremap(dev, res.start, resource_size(&res));

   and `master_stats_show()` then does

       memcpy_fromio(&stat, data->base, sizeof(stat));      /* 80 bytes */

   with NO per-master offset.  So the upstream driver wants ONE SRAM SLICE PER
   MASTER, and the per-master stride that the downstream driver applied
   internally must be expressed as three separate nodes.

   THE ADDRESSES ARE KNOWN, from the Android DT
   (GitIgnore/android_kernel_zte_msm8916/arch/arm/boot/dts/qcom/msm8916-pm.dtsi:332):

       qcom,rpm-master-stats@60150 {
               reg = <0x60150 0x2030>;
               qcom,masters = "APSS", "MPSS", "PRONTO";
               qcom,master-stats-version = <2>;
               qcom,master-offset = <4096>;
       };

   => APSS @ 0x60150, MPSS @ 0x61150, PRONTO @ 0x62150, 80 bytes each
      (sizeof(struct rpm_master_stats) = 4+4+8+8+8+8+4+4+4+4+8+8+8 = 80, __packed).

   And the upstream msm8916.dtsi ALREADY HAS the enclosing region
   (arch/arm64/boot/dts/qcom/msm8916.dtsi:541):

       rpm_msg_ram: sram@60000 { compatible = "qcom,rpm-msg-ram";
                                 reg = <0x00060000 0x8000>; };

   -- 0x60000..0x68000 contains 0x60150/0x61150/0x62150.  So the enabler is a
   small DT overlay plus CONFIG_QCOM_RPM_MASTER_STATS=m.

   PARTIAL COVERAGE ALREADY EXISTS: `CONFIG_QCOM_STATS=y` IS set, and
   qcom_stats.c handles `qcom,msm8916-rpm-stats` (upstream msm8916.dtsi
   `sram@290000`).  That exposes `/sys/kernel/debug/qcom_stats/{vmin,xosd,...}`
   with `Client Votes: %#x` -- the RPM low-power MODE stats, i.e. the equivalent
   of Android's `/d/rpm_stats`.  What it does NOT expose is the PER-MASTER
   `numshutdowns`, which is the number this PART is about.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

6. WHAT PART 29 CHANGES

  - Records that the Android AP side has a full RPM observability layer that
    needs no instrumentation, and that the two `rpm_smd` tracepoints are DECODED
    (resource names) and give a per-message round trip.  OpenWrt has neither.
  - MEASURED: the AP->RPM round trip on Android is 109-617 us, n=142,
    ZERO events > 1 ms.  The AP's RPM traffic is bursty (95.4% of 100 ms bins
    empty) at 3.28/s and is DOMINATED BY THE DISPLAY (mdss_fb0, 51%).
  - MEASURED: the MODEM's own RPM power-collapse rate on Android is
    **1.310 /s (113 s window) and 1.523 /s (52 s window)**, while APSS and
    PRONTO are flat -- i.e. the counter isolates the modem cleanly.
  - Identifies a concrete, no-flash OpenWrt enabler: CONFIG_QCOM_RPM_MASTER_STATS
    (tristate) + a 3-slice DT node at 0x60150/0x61150/0x62150, with the
    addresses and the 80-byte layout taken from the Android DT and the driver's
    own DEFINITION.
  - Re-frames the corpus's 1.88 vs 0.83 /s MCPM gap as TESTABLE rather than
    dismissed, and notes the per-record arithmetic that argues the dismissal was
    not soundly founded.

WHAT PART 29 DOES NOT CLAIM

  - It does NOT claim the RPM cadence difference is causal.  It says the
    quantity can now be measured the same way on both platforms, and gives the
    Android baseline.  The OpenWrt number is still the DIAG-derived 0.83 /s and
    must be re-measured with this instrument.
  - It does NOT claim the 45 s round-trip window is representative.  n=142 in
    one window; a longer capture is running.
  - It does NOT claim the AP's RPM behaviour is the cause of the fatal.  The
    fatal is modem-internal and ~902 s periodic; this PART is about the AP-side
    observability of the shared subsystem the modem's PC vote depends on.
  - It does NOT touch PART 28's conclusion, the userspace bearer rebuild, or the
    n >= 60 AP-hang soak.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

7. ADDENDUM -- THE POST-ROOT bam_dmux SEGMENT INDEPENDENTLY REPRODUCES PART 28

   Re-rooting adbd restarted the captures, so a second, post-root bam_dmux window
   exists (uptime 1999.5 -> 2463.8 s, 464 s).  It was analysed with the same
   analyzer and reproduces PART 28's result on a disjoint window:

     real ack-wait timeouts ........ 0            (again)
     vote -> ack  n=14 ............. min 1.733  p50 1.749  p99 2.240  MAX 2.291 ms
                                     >250 ms: 0   >10 ms: 0   >5 ms: 0
                                     headroom vs OpenWrt's 250 ms = 109.1x
     previous-ack serialization .... max 0.120 ms
     full cycle (prev-ack->complete) max 3.395 ms
     UL wakeups .................... 14 in 460 s = 1.83/min

   This window is TIGHTER than PART 28's (max 2.291 ms vs 6.340 ms), so the
   "39.4x headroom" figure in PART 28 is the CONSERVATIVE one; the pooled
   evidence is that the healthy Android ack never exceeds ~6.3 ms.  It is not
   pooled into a single n because the two windows are not independent samples of
   the same process state (the device was re-rooted between them) -- recorded as
   a REPRODUCTION, not as n=70.

   CORRECTION TO "0% PING LOSS", recorded because it is a claim this round made
   loosely.  Over the Android soak's 45 ping bursts, 44 were 3/3 and ONE was 2/3
   (33 %) -- so the honest figure is 44/45 bursts, 134/135 packets, ~0.7 %
   packet loss, NOT 0 %.  And the one loss is NOT the OpenWrt signature: it was
   icmp_seq=3, the LAST packet of the burst, in a burst whose RTTs were already
   elevated (209 / 118 ms against a baseline of 85-130 ms), i.e. ordinary
   cellular jitter.  The OpenWrt "first packet after idle" signature is
   replies=0/1 on the FIRST packet after a 120 s idle (Doc 147 s5.4) and it does
   not appear here.  Recorded so "Android has 0 % loss" is not carried forward
   as an exact number.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

================================================================================
PART 30 -- CORRECTION: THE PRE-ROOT CAPTURE WAS SCORED FROM A PARTIAL READ, AND
           THE TWO WINDOWS **ARE** POOLABLE (same boot, no reboot) -- POOLED
           n = 117 WAKEUPS, STILL ZERO TIMEOUTS
================================================================================

1. WHAT WAS WRONG

   PART 28 scored the Android control at n = 56, and PART 29's addendum then
   treated the post-root window as a separate "REPRODUCTION, not n=70" on the
   stated ground that "the two windows are not independent samples of the same
   process state (the device was re-rooted between them)".

   BOTH statements undercount, and the second is wrong on its reasoning:

   (a) The pre-root capture was still being written when PART 28 read it, so
       PART 28 scored a PARTIAL file.  The completed file is 232,837 B and its
       full analysis gives **n = 95**, not 56.  The distribution is materially
       the same (max 6.340 ms, p99 6.326 ms) -- so PART 28's CONCLUSION is
       unchanged; only its n was too small.

   (b) "The device was re-rooted between them" does NOT make the windows
       incomparable.  Re-rooting restarts adbd; it does NOT reboot the kernel
       and does NOT touch bam_dmux, the SMSM word, or the modem.  The two
       windows are DISJOINT IN TIME WITHIN ONE BOOT:

           pre-root   uptime    8.2 -> 1979.5 s   (1971 s, n = 95)
           post-root  uptime 1999.5 -> 2601.2 s   ( 602 s, n = 22)

       -- 20 s of gap, no overlap, one continuous kernel.  They POOL.

2. POOLED RESULT

     n = 117 pc-ack handshakes, ONE boot
     real ack-wait timeouts ........ 0
     vote -> ack  min 0.066   p50 ~1.98   p99 6.326   MAX 6.340 ms
                  > 250 ms: 0     > 10 ms: 0     > 5 ms: 5
     headroom vs OpenWrt's 250 ms .. 39.4x   (the pooled max is still 6.340 ms,
                                              so the headroom figure is unchanged)
     previous-ack serialisation .... max 0.375 ms
     down-vote -> ack .............. max 1.035 ms

   Note the two windows differ in CADENCE (pre-root 2.90 wakeups/min over 1971 s;
   post-root 1.83/min over 602 s) -- consistent with the post-root window
   starting after the ping soak was restarted, not with any state change.  For a
   RATE test (timeouts per handshake) the two are comparable; for a
   wakeups-per-second claim they are not, and none is made here.

3. THE POWER STATEMENT, UPDATED

     P(0 timeouts in 117 | rate = 5.7 %)  = 0.0010   -> decisive
     P(0 timeouts in 117 | rate = 2.0 %)  = 0.094    -> marginal (p < 0.10 only)
     P(0 timeouts in 117 | rate = 0.99 %) = 0.31     -> NOT significant

     n needed for P(0) < 0.05:
       at 5.7 %  ->  51   (met long ago)
       at 2.0 %  -> 148   (~50 min more at 2.9/min)
       at 1.0 %  -> 298   (~100 min more)   <-- the standing pre-registration
       at 0.5 %  -> 598

   So the honest standing statement becomes: **Android excludes a pc-ack-timeout
   rate of 5.7 % decisively and 2 % marginally; it does NOT yet exclude 1 %.**  The
   n >= 300 pre-registration is unchanged and is the number that matters.

4. WHAT PART 30 CHANGES

  - Corrects PART 28's n from 56 to 95 (partial read) and the pooled figure to
    117; PART 28's conclusion, its 39.4x headroom and its pre-registered A/B
    prediction are all UNCHANGED (same max, same p99).
  - Retracts PART 29's addendum reasoning that the two windows are not poolable.
    They are: same boot, disjoint, no reboot.  Recorded as a self-correction of
    the same class as PART 25's withdrawn ELF-header claim -- a comparability
    judgement made from the wrong variable (who ran the command) instead of the
    right one (did the kernel state change).
  - Records the CORRECT reason the two windows must not be pooled for CADENCE
    claims (2.90 vs 1.83 wakeups/min) while they may be pooled for RATE claims.

WHAT PART 30 DOES NOT CLAIM

  - It does NOT change any OpenWrt number, the 902 s fatal, the bearer rebuild,
    or the n >= 60 AP-hang soak.
  - It does NOT claim n = 117 settles the pc-ack question.  It excludes 5.7 %
    decisively and 2 % marginally, and nothing smaller.

================================================================================
PART 31 -- THE ANDROID RPM POWER-COLLAPSE SERIES, AT LAST MEASURED PROPERLY:
           MODEM 1.600 /s OVER 240 s (8 x 30 s INTERVALS, RANGE 1.133-2.067),
           APSS AND PRONTO FLAT AT 0.000 /s
================================================================================

1. WHY THIS SUPERSEDES PART 29 s4

   PART 29 s4 reported three ad-hoc windows (1.310 / 1.523 / 1.711 /s) taken from
   two samplers, one of which was a busy loop.  A clean 30 s sampler has since run
   continuously, and it gives a real SERIES rather than three point estimates.
   This PART records the series and replaces the point estimates as the Android
   baseline.

2. THE SERIES -- /d/rpm_master_stats, MPSS `numshutdowns`, 30 s cadence

     uptime   APSS     MPSS    PRONTO
     2389     35737     3338      11
     2419     35737     3386      11
     2449     35737     3448      11
     2479     35737     3492      11
     2509     35737     3538      11
     2539     35737     3590      11
     2569     35737     3637      11
     2599     35737     3671      11
     2629     35737     3722      11

     per-interval MPSS rate (/s):
       2389->2419  1.600      2509->2539  1.733
       2419->2449  2.067      2539->2569  1.567
       2449->2479  1.467      2569->2599  1.133
       2479->2509  1.533      2599->2629  1.700

     240 s window:  MPSS 1.600 /s   APSS 0.000 /s   PRONTO 0.000 /s
     intervals:     n = 8,  min 1.133,  max 2.067  (1.82x spread)

3. WHAT THE SERIES ESTABLISHES, AND WHY THE SPREAD MATTERS

   * **The modem's RPM power-collapse rate on Android is 1.60 /s** (240 s, n=8
     intervals), with a per-interval range of 1.13-2.07 /s.  The rate is NOT a
     constant, so a SINGLE pair of samples is not enough -- PART 29 s4's three
     point estimates (1.310/1.523/1.711) sit inside this range, which is the
     consistency check that they should.
   * **APSS and PRONTO are FLAT at 0.000 /s across all 240 s** while the AP is
     awake with adb and the display on.  So the counter isolates the MODEM, which
     is what makes it usable: no subtraction, no subtraction error, no assumption
     about the AP's own sleep.
   * The 1.82x spread sets the noise floor for the OpenWrt comparison.  A single
     OpenWrt sample that differs from 1.60 /s by less than ~2x is NOT evidence of
     a behavioural difference; the corpus's claimed gap (Android 1.88 /s vs
     OpenWrt 0.83 /s = 2.3x) is only just above this floor, so **the comparison
     will need several OpenWrt intervals, not one.**

   PRE-REGISTERED, BEFORE ANY OPENWRT SAMPLE EXISTS:
     P1  OpenWrt's MPSS rate, measured the same way over >= 240 s at a 30 s
         cadence, will be **below 1.0 /s** (i.e. the 2.3x gap is real).
     P2  FALSIFIER: if OpenWrt's MPSS rate lands inside Android's observed
         1.13-2.07 /s band, the corpus's gap was a DIAG-transport artefact after
         all, the MCPM cadence difference is CLOSED as a lead, and PART 29 s4's
         "re-frames the gap as testable" must be read as "tested and null".
     P3  A single OpenWrt interval is NOT sufficient to decide P1/P2 -- at least
         4 intervals (120 s) are required, because the within-Android spread is
         1.82x.

4. WHAT PART 31 CHANGES

  - Replaces PART 29 s4's three ad-hoc point estimates with a proper 240 s series:
    **MPSS 1.600 /s (n=8, range 1.133-2.067)**, APSS and PRONTO 0.000 /s.
  - Records that the modem's collapse rate is NOT stationary within a boot, which
    sets a 1.82x noise floor on the OpenWrt-vs-Android comparison.
  - Pre-registers P1/P2/P3 for the OpenWrt measurement, including the falsifier
    that would CLOSE the MCPM-cadence lead entirely.

WHAT PART 31 DOES NOT CLAIM

  - It does NOT claim the 2.3x gap is real; it pre-registers the test for it.
  - It does NOT claim the AP's RPM activity is causal.  PART 29 s3's finding that
    the AP->RPM round trip never exceeds 617 us stands unchanged.
  - It does NOT change PART 28/PART 30 (the bam_dmux control) or any OpenWrt
    number.
