================================================================================
PART 32 -- ANDROID'S ONLY CONNECTIVITY OUTAGE IN 45 MINUTES IS A ~77 s DATA-CALL
           LOSS WHOSE WINDOW CONTAINS THE MODEM-RELATIVE 902 s POINT
================================================================================

>>> RETRACTED IN PART -- READ PART 35 FIRST.  The device was on a MOVING BUS.
    Reading A (s6) is RETRACTED as unsupported: mobility fully explains the
    outage and removes the alignment's evidential weight.  The s1 differential
    control (one modem boot in 2723 s) STANDS -- a network outage cannot cause a
    modem fatal.  The empirical content of s2/s3 (the outage, its three agreeing
    instruments, its 0.6 s logcat end, the ENETUNREACH mechanism) is unaffected;
    only its INTERPRETATION changes.

0. HOW THIS AROSE

   Parts 28-31 were about the pc-ack control and the RPM.  This PART is a
   by-product: while re-scoring the Android soak I tabulated the bam_dmux
   SILENCE-GAP distribution instead of only the message census, and the two
   largest gaps in the whole capture turned out to be adjacent, at the one
   uptime the corpus cares about.

   Everything below is from ONE boot of stock Android (the same boot as PART
   28/29/30/31).  It is n = 1 and it is labelled as such throughout.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. THE CONTROL THAT MAKES THE COMPARISON MEANINGFUL: ANDROID HAD EXACTLY ONE
   MODEM BOOT IN 2723 s

   `adb shell dmesg` returns 1005 lines spanning `[ 0.000000]` to
   `[ 2723.448156]` -- i.e. the ring buffer holds the ENTIRE boot, it has not
   wrapped.  In it, for the modem (`4080000.qcom,mss`):

     [    6.588171] pil: MBA boot done
     [    7.214836] pil-q6v5-mss 4080000.qcom,mss: modem: Brought out of reset
     [    7.643287] apr_tal:Modem Is Up

   and `grep -c "Brought out of reset"` = 2, the second being
   `a21b000.qcom,pronto: wcnss` at 9.831959 s (WiFi, not the modem).

   So across 2723 s of Android uptime there is **exactly one modem bring-up,
   zero `subsys-restart` recoveries, zero `pil` reloads, zero fatals.**

   This is the cleanest possible statement of the differential, and it is
   stronger than the DIAG-based one of Doc 170's Android differential because
   dmesg is the AP's own view rather than an inference from modem DIAG density:
   the SAME byte-identical firmware, on the SAME device, runs 2723 s on Android
   without a single fatal, while OpenWrt's deterministic idle fatal lands at
   ~902.3 s of modem uptime.

   MODEM BOOT ANCHOR FOR THIS BOOT: AP 7.215 s.  (Used for every modem-relative
   conversion below.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

2. THE OUTAGE: THREE INDEPENDENT INSTRUMENTS AGREE

   (a) bam_dmux SILENCE GAPS.  The pre-root `log_cont` capture holds 3357
       events spanning AP 8.203 -> 1979.508 s.  Restricting to the soak regime
       (t >= 380 s, i.e. after the ping loop started -- see the trap in s5), the
       gap distribution is:

         n = 2833 gaps   median 0.0001 s   mean 0.5611 s   p99 25.125 s
         histogram:  0-0.1 s : 2394     5-20 s :   9
                     0.1-1 s :  325    20-26 s :  45
                     1-5 s   :   58    26-30 s :   0
                                      30-40 s :   2   <-- the only two

       The top two gaps, and nothing else is close:

         38.028 s    AP  874.309 ->  912.336
         37.183 s    AP  914.335 ->  951.518
         25.960 s    AP  465.671 ->  491.631   (third; normal cadence)

       **The two largest silences in 1600 s of soak are adjacent**, separated by
       a 2.0 s blip of activity at 912.336-914.335.  Together they are one
       episode: AP 874.309 -> 951.518 s, 77.209 s.

       Note the cadence structure: the ping loop is `ping -c 3 -W 2; sleep 27`,
       which produces a tight cluster of gaps at ~25.9 s (45 of them in
       20-26 s).  The 26-30 s bucket is EMPTY.  So the normal cadence gap is
       25.9 s and the two anomalous gaps are 1.47x that -- they are not an
       extreme of the cadence distribution, they are outside it.

   (b) PING.  Burst #19 of the pre-root soak (the 19th of 54, both loops
       started together and both are ~30 s) reported:

         3 packets transmitted, 0 received, 100% packet loss, time 2004ms

       immediately preceded by TWO `connect: Network is unreachable` lines.
       Burst #19 is at AP ~920 s, i.e. INSIDE the bam_dmux silence window.

       `Network is unreachable` is ENETUNREACH from the routing-table lookup --
       the AP could not even form the packet.  This is NOT a silent drop.

   (c) DRIVER COUNTERS.  The 60 s snapshot loop froze:

         uptime   skbRd   skbWr  rxq  ackOut  ackIn  pwrIn
             891   10814   10748    0      62     68     62
             921   10926   10748    0      64     68     64
             951   10926   10748    0      64     68     64   <-- identical to 921
             981   12632   13036    0      72     76     72

       `skb write cnt` is frozen at 10748 for 60 s (891 -> 951) and `skb read
       cnt` is frozen at 10926 for 30 s.  The `uptime` marker advanced 30 s
       between samples, so the shell loop was running -- only the driver's
       counters were not moving.  A stale-buffer artifact would have frozen the
       uptime marker too.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

3. THE OUTAGE ENDS WITH A MOBILE-DATA REBUILD, CONFIRMED TO THE SECOND

   `adb logcat -d -v time` retains 4403 lines; the oldest is
   `09-22 00:52:52.395`.  The device wall clock is wrong for the first ~60-70 s
   of a boot but is correct by this point, so wall<->uptime converts directly.
   Anchoring on uptime 2704.06 <-> 01:22:14 IST:

     00:53:02.037  ->  AP  952.1 s   modem-rel  944.8 s
     01:02:29.967  ->  AP 1520.1 s   modem-rel 1512.8 s
     01:08:50.607  ->  AP 1901.1 s   modem-rel 1893.8 s
     01:20:18.907  ->  AP 2589.1 s   modem-rel 2581.8 s
     01:23:06.577  ->  AP 2757.1 s   modem-rel 2749.8 s

   The first of these is `ConnectivityChange for mobile: CONNECTED/CONNECTED` +
   `handleConnect: E newNetType=0 thisIface=rmnet0`, and it is preceded in the
   same second by

     ConnectivityChange for mobile: CONNECTING/CONNECTING      (00:53:01.747)
     handleConnect: E newNetType=5 thisIface=rmnet0 isFailoverfalse
     newLp={InterfaceName: rmnet0 LinkAddresses: [10.81.148.167/28,]
            Routes: [0.0.0.0/0 -> 10.81.148.168,] DnsAddresses: [49.45.0.1,]}
     Adding 0.0.0.0/0 -> 10.81.148.168 for interface rmnet0    (00:53:02.017)

   **AP 952.1 s (logcat) vs AP 951.518 s (the bam_dmux silence ending) -- a
   0.6 s agreement between two unrelated instruments.**  The outage ended when
   Android rebuilt the mobile data call and re-added the default route.

   The mechanism of the ENETUNREACH is visible in the live routing state:

     $ ip route show table all | grep -i unreachable
     unreachable default dev lo  table 0  proto kernel  metric 4294967295
                                 error -101

     $ ip rule
     0:     from all lookup local
     98:    from 192.168.100.0/24 lookup 60
     99:    from all to <various> lookup main
     32766: from all lookup main
     32767: from all lookup default

   `error -101` IS ENETUNREACH.  With the network's table (60) and `main` both
   consulted before the fallback, removing the data call's default route makes
   traffic fall through to the kernel's `unreachable default` and fail fast --
   which is exactly what `connect: Network is unreachable` reports.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

4. THE OUTAGE IS ONE-SHOT PER MODEM BOOT -- TWO PRE-REGISTERED NON-RECURRENCES

   The modem-internal clock is ~902.3 s of MODEM uptime (Doc 148 s15, Doc 149
   s22/s45).  With the modem booted at AP 7.215 s, the multiples fall at:

     #1  modem-rel  902.3  ->  AP  909.5 s     <-- INSIDE the outage
     #2  modem-rel 1804.6  ->  AP 1811.8 s
     #3  modem-rel 2706.9  ->  AP 2714.1 s

   #2 and #3 were both checked and produced NOTHING:

     * AP 1811.8: the pre-root gap list in 1750-1880 s contains only the normal
       ~25.1 s cadence gaps (25.117 at 1804.718->1829.835, 25.109 at
       1775.608->1800.718, 25.147 at 1862.958->1888.105).  No silence.
     * AP 2714.1: the post-root capture, taken with its soak start set from the
       post-root snapshot's first uptime marker (`--soak-start 2110`, the
       discipline of s5), gives n = 1559 gaps over AP 2110.318 -> 3017.658 s
       with:

         median 0.0001 s   mean 0.5820 s   p99 25.125 s   MAX 25.957 s
         histogram:  0-0.1 s : 1303    5-20 s  :  2
                     0.1-1 s :  194   20-26 s  : 29
                     1-5 s   :   31   26-30 s  :  0    30-40 s : 0

       **The 26-30 and 30-40 buckets are EMPTY over 907 s**, i.e. in this whole
       regime the largest gap is the ordinary ping cadence (25.957 s).  No
       silence at 2714.1, and none anywhere else either.
       (Passing `--soak-start 1999` instead of 2110 exposes a 107.579 s gap at
       AP 2001.746 -> 2109.325 -- that is the re-root RESTART ARTIFACT, not an
       outage, and it is exactly the s5 trap: the post-root snapshot's first
       marker is uptime 2110 while the bam_dmux capture opens at 1999.5.)

   So the ~902 s alignment rests on ONE occurrence, and the event does NOT
   recur at the next two multiples.  Whatever it is, it is not a free-running
   902.3 s oscillator on this boot.

   Corroborating the "not a period" reading from the other side: the logcat
   window contains FIVE `ConnectivityChange for mobile: CONNECTED/CONNECTED`
   events, at modem-relative 944.8 / 1512.8 / 1893.8 / 2581.8 / 2749.8 s, i.e.
   intervals of 568 / 381 / 688 / 168 s.  **Data-call re-broadcasts are routine
   and irregular on Android.**  Only the FIRST of the five coincided with a
   bam_dmux silence, so "a data-call rebuild" by itself is not the signature --
   the signature is the 77 s silence, and the rebuild is how it ended.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

5. TRAP FOUND WHILE DOING THIS: THE PRE-SOAK BUFFERED HISTORY IS NOT SOAK DATA

   The first pass at the gap distribution produced a 111.684 s gap
   (141.068 -> 252.752) and a 95.984 s gap (42.476 -> 138.460), both LARGER than
   the event I was looking for.  They are an artifact of `log_cont`: it is a
   CONTINUOUS reader, so the capture opens with whatever the ipc_logging ring
   already held -- i.e. lines from BOOT, not from the capture's start.  The
   bam_dmux capture therefore begins at AP 8.2 s while the snapshot and ping
   loops in the same command block only begin at AP ~380 s (the snapshot's first
   `=== uptime` marker is 379.99).

   During AP 8-380 s the modem was idle because no ping soak was running yet, so
   the gaps are large and meaningless.  **Always establish the soak regime's
   start from an instrument that stamps its own start (here the snapshot's
   uptime markers), never from the first line of a `log_cont` capture.**

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

6. TWO READINGS, AND WHY NEITHER IS ESTABLISHED

   READING A -- "the ~902 s event is not OpenWrt-specific".  The modem has a
   periodic internal hiccup at ~902.3 s of modem uptime.  On Android the AP
   survives it: the data path stops, Android's watchdog eventually tears the
   call down and rebuilds it, and the user sees ~77 s of no connectivity.  On
   OpenWrt the same event escalates to `ERR_FATAL a2_power.c:1189` plus an SSR,
   and the user sees a 15-26 s bearer rebuild (Doc 170's userspace finding).
   Under this reading the two stacks see the SAME event and differ only in
   outcome, and the OpenWrt bug is a MISHANDLING, not a cause.

   READING B -- "coincidence".  The Android outage is an ordinary Android/
   network-side transient (a PDN conflict, a carrier detach, the provisioning
   check) that happens to overlap 902.3 s.  The outage is 77.209 s wide out of
   ~1600 s of soak, so a uniformly-timed unrelated event lands inside it with
   p ~= 4.8 %.  That is suggestive but not significant on its own.

   EVIDENCE THAT DOES NOT SEPARATE THEM:
     * The bam_dmux log shows NOTHING anomalous at the onset.  The last events
       before the silence are a textbook UL cycle: `ul_wakeup` at 870.310, three
       `ul_timeout: pkt written 1` at 871.308/872.308/873.308 (a 4 s uplink
       hold, the normal `(ticks+1) x 1.000 s` pattern), then a normal
       `ul_powerdown` at 874.308 followed by the normal `disconnect_to_bam`.
       So bam_dmux cannot distinguish "the AP had nothing to send" from "the
       modem stopped servicing the path".
     * The modem did NOT crash: no SSR in dmesg, one modem boot, and the SMSM
       handshake continued normally DURING the outage (a `reconnect` at 912.336
       and a `disconnect` at 914.335).  A dead modem cannot do that.
     * The 77.209 s duration is consistent with the corpus's DcTracker 60 s
       data-stall watchdog (60 s to detect + ~17 s to rebuild), which supports
       Reading A's *mechanism* but says nothing about its *trigger*.

   WHAT WOULD SEPARATE THEM: a second Android boot with
     (i)  BOTH `ping 8.8.8.8` and `ping -I rmnet0 8.8.8.8` in parallel
          (the second bypasses the routing table -- see PART 33 s2),
     (ii) a PERSISTENT `logcat -b all -v time > file` started at boot, so the
          DISCONNECTED event is retained instead of wrapping out, and
     (iii) a 10 s snapshot cadence so the onset is bracketed to +-10 s rather
          than +-30 s.
   PRE-REGISTERED: under Reading A, a >= 30 s bam_dmux silence, or a data-call
   DISCONNECTED/CONNECTED pair, occurs with onset at modem-relative
   902.3 +- 40 s on the second boot.  Under Reading B it does not.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

7. WHAT PART 32 CLAIMS AND WHAT IT DOES NOT

   CLAIMS
   - Android ran 2723 s with exactly one modem boot, zero SSRs, zero fatals
     (dmesg, full-boot coverage, no wrap).
   - Android had exactly one connectivity outage in that window: AP
     874.309 -> 951.518 s (77.209 s), modem-relative 867.1 -> 944.3 s.
   - Three independent instruments agree (bam_dmux gaps, ping ENETUNREACH,
     frozen driver counters), and its END is confirmed to 0.6 s by logcat.
   - The modem-relative 902.3 s point falls INSIDE that window.
   - The event did not recur at modem-relative 1804.6 or 2706.9 s.

   DOES NOT CLAIM
   - It does NOT claim the Android outage IS the 902 s event.  n = 1, the
     window is 4.8 % of the soak, and Reading B is live.
   - It does NOT claim Android is immune to the 902 s event, nor that OpenWrt's
     fatal is caused by it.  The two remain connected only by a coincidence of
     time.
   - It does NOT change PART 28/30 (n = 117, zero ack-wait timeouts) or PART 31
     (the RPM series), and it does NOT touch any OpenWrt number.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

================================================================================
PART 33 -- CORRECTIONS: THE POOLED ANDROID PING IS ~2 % NOT 0.7 %, AND THE
           CORPUS'S "0 % PING LOSS" USED `ping -I rmnet0`, WHICH STRUCTURALLY
           CANNOT SEE A ROUTE LOSS
================================================================================

1. THE POOLED PING, RE-SCORED AGAIN (PART 29's correction WAS ITSELF TOO LOW)

   PART 29's correction replaced "0 % loss" with "44/45 bursts, ~0.7 %, one
   ordinary-jitter loss".  Both files have grown since, and the pooled figure
   is now (soak still running, so the denominator is a LOWER BOUND and the rate
   an UPPER bound -- re-score at the end):

     pre-root   54 bursts
     post-root  33 bursts
     POOLED     87 bursts, 261 tx, 256 rx  ->  1.92 % loss
     (at the earlier scoring: 85 bursts, 1.96 %)

   with THREE lossy bursts, not one:

     pre-root  burst #1    3 tx / 2 rx   33 %   <-- first burst after start
     pre-root  burst #19   3 tx / 0 rx  100 %   <-- the outage of PART 32
     post-root             3 tx / 2 rx   33 %   <-- single-packet

   So: **~2 %, driven by one total-loss burst, not ~0.7 % driven by one jitter
   packet.**  The distinction matters because a 100 % burst is a categorical
   event, not tail behaviour, and it is the thing PART 32 is about.  Note also
   that the loss rate is NOT the interesting statistic here: 33 % and 100 % are
   different KINDS of event, and pooling them into one percentage hides that.

   (Same lesson as PART 30, one level down: a rate quoted from a file that is
   still growing is a lower bound on the DENOMINATOR and an upper bound on the
   loss.  PART 29's correction was made on a partially-read file for the second
   time.  Re-score from the completed file.)

2. THE CORPUS'S "0 % PING LOSS" IS WEAKER THAN IT READS -- `ping -I rmnet0`
   CANNOT OBSERVE A ROUTE LOSS

   `project_android_differential_900s` records "0 % ping loss (62 bursts x 3
   pings each, all received)" and states the method: "Data interface: rmnet0,
   `ping -I rmnet0 8.8.8.8`".

   `-I rmnet0` BINDS the socket to the interface, which bypasses the
   routing-table lookup entirely.  The failure PART 32 found -- ENETUNREACH
   from the `unreachable default dev lo table 0` fallback -- is produced BY that
   lookup.  **A soak using `ping -I rmnet0` therefore cannot, even in principle,
   observe the outage PART 32 found, no matter how long it runs.**

   This is not a contradiction between the two results; it is two different
   questions:
     `ping -I rmnet0 8.8.8.8`  asks "does the rmnet0 interface forward packets?"
     `ping 8.8.8.8`            asks "is there working connectivity?"

   The corpus's 0 % is a valid answer to the first.  It was read as an answer to
   the second.  **Any future Android control must run BOTH, side by side, or it
   will silently certify a connectivity outage as clean.**

3. DE-CONFUSION: `disconnect_to_bam` IS NOT AN SSR

   The Android bam_dmux log prints `disconnect_to_bam: disconnect tx / disconnect
   rx / device reset` and `bam_dmux_smsm_cb: ... disconnect`.  Counted naively
   these look alarming -- 86 cycles in the pre-root capture, 27 in the
   post-root -- and "device reset" invites the reading "the modem reset 86
   times".  It is nothing of the kind, and the decode is available from the
   tree:

     include/soc/qcom/smsm.h:44   #define SMSM_PROC_AWAKE         0x00001000
     include/soc/qcom/smsm.h:56   #define SMSM_A2_POWER_CONTROL   0x00000002
     bam_dmux.c:2509              smsm_state_cb_register_ptr(SMSM_MODEM_STATE,
                                      SMSM_A2_POWER_CONTROL, bam_dmux_smsm_cb, ...)
     bam_dmux.c:2435              bam_dmux_power_state =
                                      new_state & SMSM_A2_POWER_CONTROL ? 1 : 0

   The two words actually observed are `0x0800800b` and `0x08009009`.  They
   differ in exactly two bits: bit 1 (`SMSM_A2_POWER_CONTROL`) and bit 12
   (`SMSM_PROC_AWAKE`) trade places.  Everything else -- bit 0 `SMSM_INIT`,
   bit 3 `SMSM_SMDINIT`, bit 15, bit 27 -- is identical.

   So the transition is **the modem clearing `A2_POWER_CONTROL` and setting
   `PROC_AWAKE`**: "I am going to sleep."  The callback is registered on
   `SMSM_MODEM_STATE` (the MODEM's word), so the AP is reading the modem's own
   announcement.  `disconnect_to_bam()` is the AP's correct response (tear the
   BAM channels down); it is not a fault and not an SSR.

   Corroboration that it is the normal per-burst power dance, not a fault:
   the cycle count is 1:1 with the UL wakeup count in both windows --
     pre-root   86 disconnect/reconnect pairs over 1971 s = 1 per 22.9 s
                (95 UL wakeups over 1971 s = 1 per 20.7 s)
     post-root  27 pairs over 698 s = 1 per 25.9 s
                (26 UL wakeups over 694 s = 1 per 26.7 s)
   **A real SSR storm would not track the ping cadence.**

   And the state prefix is now pinned from the source (bam_dmux.c:320-337),
   which is what the earlier parse trap was about:
     D = a2_pc_disabled      V = bam_dmux_uplink_vote
     R = in_global_reset     U = bam_is_connected
     P = bam_dmux_power_state W = wait_for_ack
     A = connection_is_active A = ul_wakeup_ack_completion.done
     then one digit = the on-demand uplink vote.
   The format string is `"<DMUX> %c%c%c%c %c%c%c%c%d "` -- TWO space-separated
   tokens.  `drPA/VUwA` (449 occurrences) is the healthy active state;
   `drpA vuWA0` is the state at the instant of a sleep announcement.

4. THE RPM SERIES, EXTENDED: 300 s, MPSS 1.580 /s (n = 10)

   PART 31's series was 240 s / 8 intervals at 1.600 /s.  The same sampler has
   since produced a longer window:

     uptime   APSS     MPSS    PRONTO
     2389     35737     3338      11
     2419     35737     3386      11
     2449     35737     3448      11
     2479     35737     3492      11
     2509     35737     3538      11
     2539     35737     3590      11
     2569     35737     3637      11
     2599     35737     3671      11
     2629     35737     3722      11
     2659     35737     3761      11
     2689     35737     3812      11

     300 s window:  MPSS 1.580 /s   APSS 0.000 /s   PRONTO 0.000 /s
     intervals:     n = 10,  min 1.133,  max 2.067  (1.82x spread)

   This supersedes PART 31's headline number (1.600 /s, n=8) as the current
   Android baseline.  The interval spread is IDENTICAL (1.133-2.067), so the
   1.82x noise floor of PART 31 s3 stands unchanged, as do pre-registrations
   P1/P2/P3.

5. THE bam_dmux POOL, EXTENDED: n = 121

     pre-root   AP   8.2 -> 1979.5 s   n = 95
     post-root  AP 1999.5 -> 2697.4 s   n = 26
     POOLED                            n = 121

   Still **zero** real ack-wait timeouts (the three strings at
   bam_dmux.c:1917/1928/1936).  Latest post-root window: vote->ack n=26,
   min 0.140, p50 1.876, max 2.291 ms -- 109x headroom against OpenWrt's 250 ms
   wait.  The standing pre-registration is unchanged: n >= 300 for the 1 %
   regime, so n = 121 still excludes 5.7 % decisively and 2 % only marginally.

6. WHAT PART 33 CHANGES

  - Corrects PART 29's correction: the pooled Android ping is **85 bursts /
    1.96 %**, with one 100 % burst, not 44/45 at ~0.7 %.
  - Records the `ping -I rmnet0` trap: it bypasses the routing table and
    structurally cannot observe a route loss, so the corpus's Android "0 %
    ping loss" is not evidence about connectivity.
  - Decodes the bam_dmux `disconnect_to_bam` / `reconnect` pair from
    `smsm.h` + the registration site: it is the modem's
    `SMSM_A2_POWER_CONTROL` / `SMSM_PROC_AWAKE` sleep handshake, NOT an SSR.
  - Extends the RPM series to 300 s (1.580 /s, n=10) and the bam_dmux pool to
    n = 121.

  DOES NOT CLAIM
  - It does NOT change any OpenWrt number, the 902 s fatal, the bearer rebuild,
    or the n >= 60 AP-hang soak.
  - It does NOT retract the corpus's Android DIAG work; it narrows what that
    work's "0 % ping loss" line supports.

================================================================================
PART 37 -- BOOT-2 RESULT: NON-RECURRENCE.  READING A NOW HAS NO SUPPORTING
           OBSERVATION, AND THE DIFFERENTIAL CONTROL IS n = 2
================================================================================

Scored against the pre-registration filed in PART 36 (committed at uptime 265 s,
~644 s before the window).  Window: AP 868.9 -> 948.9 s (modem-relative
902.3 +- 40 s, modem boot anchor AP 6.604624 s).

1. THE WINDOW WAS COVERED, AND IT WAS EMPTY

   Every number in this PART comes from a FROZEN copy of the captures --
   scratch/android_capture/boot2/frozen_1232/ -- taken at device uptime
   1260.4 s, i.e. 311 s after the window closed.  See the note at the end of s2
   for why freezing was not optional.

   **AND THE SOAK DID NOT STOP AT THE FREEZE.**  All five captures were still
   growing when this was written -- checked over 60 s: ping +737 B, bam_dmux
   +7.7 KB, logcat +80 KB -- so every count below is a SNAPSHOT at uptime
   1260.4 s, not a total.  That is safe here for one specific reason: the verdict
   is a statement about events INSIDE AP 868.9-948.9 s, a region already in the
   past when the freeze was taken, so no later data can change what happened in
   it.  It would NOT be safe for a rate or a total.
   (Related trap, hit while writing this: a first liveness check concluded "the
   ping loops have stopped" from a 20 s size sample showing delta 0.  The loop is
   `ping -c 3 -W 2; sleep 27`, so a 20 s sample can fall entirely inside the
   sleep.  Android KitKat's `ps` also truncates arguments, so `ps | grep` for
   the loop's command line is not a liveness test either.  **Sample for longer
   than the loop's period, or not at all.**)

   The primary instrument, bam_dmux silence gaps, restricted to AP >= 206 s
   (see s4 for why 206):

     events after AP 206 s: 2141      span 206.338 -> 1260.448 s
     events INSIDE 868.9-948.9: 189
     gaps with ONSET inside 868.9-948.9: 189,  MAX 23.893 s
     MAX gap anywhere after AP 206 s:    24.941 s (at AP 326.286)

   **The maximum silence inside the window is 23.893 s -- the ordinary ping
   cadence.**  For comparison, boot 1's window contained 38.028 s and 37.183 s
   gaps against the same ~25 s cadence.  The histogram has NO bucket at or
   above 26 s anywhere in the whole 1054 s soak:

     0-0.1 s: 1748   5-20 s: 7
     0.1-1 s:  323   20-26 s: 31
     1-5 s:     31   >=26 s: 0     <-- NOT ONE gap of 26 s or more

   That last line is the strongest single statement in this PART: not one of
   the 2140 gaps in the soak is as long as a single missed ping cycle.

2. THE OTHER INSTRUMENTS -- AND ONE OF THEM NEEDED CORRECTING

   All three were re-scored from the frozen copies.  **The first pass of this
   section was WRONG, and the error changed a conclusion rather than a count.
   See the note at the end.**

   * **logcat: the mobile data call flapped THREE times, at AP 1082.6-1087.8 s
     -- 133.7 s AFTER the window closed.**

       AP    21.328  mobile  CONNECTED/CONNECTED      <- boot bring-up
       AP  1082.572  mobile  DISCONNECTED/DISCONNECTED
       AP  1083.612  mobile  CONNECTED/CONNECTED
       AP  1085.092  mobile  DISCONNECTED/DISCONNECTED
       AP  1086.192  mobile  CONNECTED/CONNECTED
       AP  1087.632  mobile  DISCONNECTED/DISCONNECTED
       AP  1087.772  mobile  CONNECTED/CONNECTED

     Seven `mobile:` events = one boot bring-up plus a **3-beat flap inside
     5.2 s**.  The `mobile_hipri:` network flaps on the same edges (AP 1082.372
     -> 1099.262) and is otherwise silent after AP 44 s.

     **SO BOOT 2 WAS NOT OUTAGE-FREE.**  The first pass said "exactly ONE
     ConnectivityChange ... ZERO further rebuilds"; that was scored from a
     logcat that was still being written, before AP 1082 existed in the file.
     Corrected above, and see s5 -- this fact changes the weight of the result.

   * **counters: 124 snapshot samples, AP 21 -> 1256 s, ZERO resets.**
     `skb read cnt` 312 -> 26743, `skb write cnt` 428 -> 27140 (monotonic:
     True), `a2 pwr cntl in` 5 -> 105.  A modem restart or SSR would reset
     these, and the AP 1082.6 flap does NOT reset them.

   * **ping, BOTH variants, 37 bursts each -- and they DISAGREE.**
       ping_plain:  37 bursts, 1 lossy  (#35 = 3 sent / 0 recv = 100 %)
       ping_iface:  37 bursts, 2 lossy  (#31 = 3/1 = 67 %, #33 = 3/2 = 33 %)
     Each variant has exactly ONE `Network is unreachable`, and it is on line 1
     of each file -- the first invocation, at AP ~22 s before the data call was
     up.  Not an in-run event.
     **This is the first time the two variants have been run side by side, and
     the result is the OPPOSITE of what PART 33 s2 expected.**  PART 33 argued
     that `-I rmnet0` is the BLIND one (it skips the routing lookup, so it
     cannot see a route loss).  Here `-I` is the one that LOST packets while
     plain `ping` lost fewer over the same period.  A plausible mechanism is
     that `-I` pins the socket to the interface and its address, so it is
     fragile to an interface-level rebind, whereas plain `ping` re-resolves the
     route on each invocation -- but **the cause is NOT established and this is
     recorded as an observation, not a finding.**
     **It does weaken one thing PART 33 s2 implied:** two independent
     `ping ... ; sleep 27` loops are NOT time-locked.  They drift apart by their
     own per-iteration adb overhead, so "both variants agree" is only
     approximate and a clean comparison needs them interleaved in ONE loop (or
     timestamped).  **For that reason no lossy burst is attributed to the
     AP 1082.6 flap**, even though the earliest candidate (iface #31) could land
     within ~7 s of it: at a 29.0 s cadence from AP 206 the three losses fall at
     AP ~1076, ~1134 and ~1192, spread over 116 s -- too wide to be one event.

   **AND THE IN-WINDOW BURSTS ARE CLEAN IN BOTH VARIANTS.**  At that same
   ~29.0 s cadence the bursts landing inside 868.9-948.9 s are #24, #25 and #26
   (AP ~873, ~902, ~931), and the bam_dmux record independently shows exactly
   four UL wakeup bursts inside the window -- AP 875.404, 904.525, 924.176 and
   933.629.  None of the three in-window ping bursts is lossy in EITHER
   variant.  The three lossy bursts are #31/#33/#35, all at AP >= ~1076 s,
   i.e. **at least 127 s AFTER the window closed.**  The verdict of s1/s5 is
   unchanged.

   *** A REAL LIMITATION OF THIS PART'S PRIMARY INSTRUMENT, FOUND HERE ***

   The AP 1082.6-1087.8 flap **produced NO bam_dmux silence gap.**  The gap that
   spans it is AP 1055.230 -> 1079.148 = 23.918 s -- the ordinary cadence -- and
   the modem is BUSY through the flap (a 16-event UL cluster, AP 1084.404 ->
   1095.477).  So the silence-gap instrument, the primary evidence in s1, is
   **structurally blind to a userspace/framework bearer flap**: the modem data
   channel keeps running while the IP-layer network is torn down and rebuilt.
   That is the same shape as the OpenWrt finding that the user-visible data
   stall is a USERSPACE bearer rebuild rather than a kernel data-plane outage
   -- and it is now seen on BOTH stacks.
   **Consequence for s1:** "no gap in the window" is NOT by itself proof of
   "no outage in the window".  The in-window verdict rests on the pings (clean)
   and on logcat (silent until AP 1082.4), which together DO cover this class.

   NOTE ON HOW THIS SECTION WAS SCORED -- **the fourth instance of the live-file
   rule in this document, and the first one that reversed a conclusion.**  The
   first pass read the ping files at 29 bursts and the logcat at a point before
   AP 1082, and reported "29 bursts each, ZERO lossy" and "exactly ONE
   ConnectivityChange ... ZERO further rebuilds".  By artifact-generation time
   the pings were at 33-34 bursts with two `-I` losses; by the freeze they were
   at 37 bursts with three losses, and the logcat had gained a 3-beat flap.
   **The captures had live writers, and one of them falsified a sentence that
   this PART was about to commit as a finding.**  Freeze first, then score.

3. THE DIFFERENTIAL CONTROL IS NOW n = 2 BOOTS

   Boot 2's kernel ring is COMPLETE and provably un-wrapped: its first line is
   `[0.000000]` and the ring never filled, so it contains every kernel message
   since boot -- and its LAST entry is `[  137.755410] diag: USB connected`.
   **Therefore no kernel message of any kind occurred after 137.755 s**, which
   includes any SSR or fatal.

     "Brought out of reset" count:        2   (modem at 6.604624 s + wcnss at 8.298620 s)
     "subsys-restart: Resetting" count:   0
     [    5.974147] pil: MBA boot done
     [    6.604624] pil-q6v5-mss 4080000.qcom,mss: modem: Brought out of reset
     [    7.004389] apr_tal:Modem Is Up

   So: **two independent Android boots have now each passed modem-relative
   902.3 s with zero modem bring-ups, zero SSRs and zero fatals** (boot 1 to
   2723 s, boot 2 to 1256 s and still running), against byte-identical firmware.
   That is the corpus's central differential, now n = 2 on the AP side.

4. THE TWO EARLY GAPS ARE A CAPTURE ARTIFACT, NOT AN OUTAGE

   93.724 s (AP 44.077 -> 137.801) and 65.308 s (AP 140.033 -> 205.341) are the
   two largest gaps in the raw boot-2 record, and BOTH sit before AP 206 s.
   They are the supervisor gap: after the re-root, adbd restarted a second time
   and killed the three earliest captures (PART 36 s5), so the ping loops did not
   run between AP ~44 s and the supervisor's restart at host 20:08:41-42.  With
   no ping there is no uplink traffic, hence no bam_dmux events.  Converting:
   reboot at host 20:05, device back at uptime 9.09 s, so 20:08:41 ~= AP 206 s --
   which is exactly where the gaps stop.

   **Reported here because it is the same trap as PART 32 s5 in a new costume:
   a gap in an instrument is evidence about the INSTRUMENT until proven
   otherwise.**  A capture that dies and is restarted leaves a silence that looks
   exactly like an outage.

5. VERDICT, AGAINST THE PRE-REGISTERED ASYMMETRY

   PART 36 s3 stated the asymmetry in advance:
     * a RECURRENCE would be STRONG evidence for Reading A;
     * a NON-RECURRENCE is WEAK evidence against it.

   **The result is NON-RECURRENCE.**  Scored honestly:

   * **Reading A is now unsupported by any observation.**  PART 35 retracted its
     original support (the bus fully explained boot 1's outage), and boot 2 did
     not reproduce it.  Reading B stands alone.
   * **But the non-recurrence is WEAK, as pre-registered -- and s2 changed the
     reason, so this is restated rather than left standing.**  Reading A's
     Android manifestation was "the data path stalls and the call is rebuilt".
     Boot 2 had no such event *inside the window* -- **but it DID have a 3-beat
     `mobile` flap at AP 1082.6-1087.8, so the first pass's "boot 2 had NO
     outage at all" is wrong.**  That cuts both ways, and both are worth
     stating rather than picking the convenient one:
       - it makes the "the bus was in better coverage" excuse WEAKER: the device
         was still on the same bus and still received a network-induced flap,
         just 133.7 s late, so an absent manifestation at 902.3 s is somewhat
         more meaningful than the first pass claimed;
       - but it is still n = 1 boot, the flap's timing is uncontrolled, and a
         manifestation that requires a network perturbation to be present at a
         *particular instant* can still be absent by luck.
     **Net: this does not prove Reading A false; it removes its last support.**
   * What is NOT weak is s3: the differential control is n = 2 and rests on a
     complete, un-wrapped kernel ring plus zero counter resets over 105 samples.

6. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT

   The same boot-2 instrumentation, run **STATIONARY**, on a boot that also
   reaches the 902.3 s point.  That removes the confound in both directions and
   would also produce the stationary RPM/MCPM baseline that PART 35 s3 says
   PART 31's P1/P2/P3 need.  One run serves both purposes.

================================================================================
PART 36 -- SECOND-ANDROID-BOOT PRE-REGISTRATION, FILED BEFORE THE WINDOW OPENS
================================================================================

(NOTE ON ORDER: this PART was written into the file ahead of PART 35 because it
had to be committed before its window opened; PART 35 follows immediately below
and is the PART 32 correction.  PART 37, the RESULT of this pre-registration,
appears ABOVE this one.  Read 35 -> 36 -> 37.)

Filed 2026-09-22 at device uptime 265 s, i.e. ~644 s BEFORE the window it
predicts.  The whole point of a pre-registration is that it is on record before
the result; this PART is committed before the window opens.

1. THE BOOT-2 ANCHOR (measured, not assumed)

   From `dmesg` on the new boot (first line `[0.000000]`, so full coverage):

     [    5.974147] pil: MBA boot done
     [    6.604624] pil-q6v5-mss 4080000.qcom,mss: modem: Brought out of reset
     [    7.004389] apr_tal:Modem Is Up
     [    8.298620] subsys-pil-tz a21b000.qcom,pronto: wcnss: Brought out of reset
                                                                  (wcnss, not modem)

   **MODEM BOOT ANCHOR = AP 6.604624 s.**  `Brought out of reset` count = 2, the
   second being wcnss, exactly as on boot 1.

   Boot-1 anchor for comparison: AP 7.214836 s.

2. THE PREDICTION

   The fatal clock is ~902.3 s of MODEM uptime, so on this boot it falls at

     AP  6.604624 + 902.3  =  AP 908.9 s
     pre-registered window  =  AP 868.9 -> 948.9 s   (+-40 s)

   **UNDER READING A** (the ~902 s event happens on both stacks and OpenWrt
   mishandles it): a bam_dmux silence of >= 30 s, OR a data-call
   DISCONNECTED/CONNECTED pair, with ONSET inside AP 868.9-948.9 s.

   **UNDER READING B** (coincidence; now the leading reading, and mobility makes
   an outage at any given moment likely): no alignment in MODEM-RELATIVE time.
   Outages may well occur -- the device is still on a bus -- but they should not
   cluster at modem-relative 902.3 s.

3. THE ASYMMETRY, STATED IN ADVANCE

   * A RECURRENCE at AP 868.9-948.9 s is **STRONG** evidence for Reading A,
     precisely BECAUSE the bus cannot produce an alignment in modem-relative
     time: the bus's position at modem-relative 902.3 s is uncorrelated with
     anything the modem does.
   * A NON-RECURRENCE is **WEAK** evidence against A.  It is the expected outcome
     under Reading B, but one boot is one bit, and A was already downgraded to
     "unsupported" (PART 35 s1) rather than shown false.

4. WHAT WILL *NOT* BE SCORED

   * **The marginal statistic.**  "Did a connectivity outage occur?" is answered
     by the bus, not by the modem.  It is not evidence either way and must not be
     reported as such.
   * **The pooled ping loss.**  Same reason (PART 35 s3).
   * **The RPM series from this boot as a PART 31 baseline.**  Boot 2 is ALSO a
     bus baseline.  PART 31's P1/P2/P3 need a STATIONARY Android baseline; two
     bus boots do not make one.  Boot 2's RPM series is recorded for consistency
     only.

5. INSTRUMENTATION ACTUALLY RUNNING (verified, not intended)

     logcat        adb logcat -v time                    (fresh buffer from boot)
     bam_dmux      cat /d/ipc_logging/bam_dmux/log_cont
     ping_plain    while true; do ping -c 3 -W 2 8.8.8.8; sleep 27; done
     ping_iface    while true; do ping -c 3 -W 2 -I rmnet0 8.8.8.8; sleep 27; done
     snapshot      10 s cadence: bam_dmux stats + rmnet counters + `ip route`
     rpm           30 s cadence: MPSS/APSS/PRONTO numshutdowns

   All six are supervised by `scratch/android_capture/boot2_supervisor.sh`, which
   re-checks every 20 s and restarts any that died.  Two things this boot already
   proved are necessary:

   * **adbd restarts MORE THAN ONCE after the re-root.**  The first attempt
     started six captures and THREE of them (the earliest three) were killed by a
     second adbd restart; the two started 4-5 s later survived.  Order the
     re-root strictly BEFORE any capture, and supervise afterwards.
   * **`adb shell while true; do ...` MUST be single-quoted.**  Without the
     quotes the HOST shell parses `while true` and chokes on `do`, so the process
     dies instantly and a naive supervisor restart-loops forever.  (This cost one
     cycle here.)

   Also noted: boot 2 attached on a DIFFERENT network (`10.155.13.73/30` vs boot
   1's `10.81.148.167/28`), consistent with a moving platform.

================================================================================
PART 35 -- PART 32 CORRECTION: THE DEVICE WAS ON A MOVING BUS.  READING A IS
           RETRACTED, AND THE MOBILITY CONFOUND NOW BOUNDS EVERY ANDROID NUMBER
================================================================================

0. HOW THIS WAS FOUND, AND WHY IT SHOULD NOT HAVE NEEDED FINDING

   PART 32 s6 built its whole case for Reading A on one alignment: Android's
   only connectivity outage (AP 874.309 -> 951.518 s = modem-relative
   867.1 -> 944.3 s) CONTAINS the modem-internal 902.3 s point.  That argument
   assumed the outage's time was essentially arbitrary, so that a 77.209 s
   window overlapping a fixed point was worth p ~ 4.8 %.

   The operator has now stated the physical context: **the Android device was
   travelling on a bus for the whole soak.**  Data outages are EXPECTED in that
   condition.  I never asked for the physical context before building the
   alignment argument; that is the error, and it is the same class as PART 25's
   withdrawn ELF-header claim -- a conclusion drawn from a variable I had not
   checked.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. READING A IS RETRACTED

   The bus does not merely weaken the alignment, it removes the argument:

   * On a moving platform, connectivity losses are (a) EXPECTED and (b) NOT
     uniformly distributed in time -- they cluster wherever coverage is bad.
     The 902.3 s point is fixed by the MODEM BOOT, not by anything the bus does.
     So an outage overlapping it is exactly what one would see whenever the bus
     happens to be in a poor stretch at that moment, and the overlap therefore
     carries **no evidential weight at all**.  The p ~ 4.8 % figure of PART 32 s6
     was computed under a uniformity assumption that the bus violates.
   * The ENETUNREACH mechanism (PART 32 s3) is fully explained without the modem:
     a coverage hole drops the data call, Android removes the default route,
     `ping` falls through to `unreachable default dev lo table 0 ... error -101`,
     and the call is rebuilt when coverage returns.
   * The 77.209 s duration is a plausible coverage-hole duration, and the
     `Network is unreachable` pair needs no modem-side story.
   * The five `mobile: CONNECTED/CONNECTED` rebuilds in the logcat window
     (PART 32 s4) are likewise explained: five re-attachments over 45 min on a
     bus is unremarkable, and their irregular spacing (568/381/688/168 s) is
     what mobility looks like.

   **READING A ("the ~902 s event happens on BOTH stacks and OpenWrt
   MISHANDLES it into the fatal") is RETRACTED as unsupported.**  READING B
   (coincidence) is now the leading reading, and is in fact favoured rather than
   merely permitted, because on a bus an outage at any given time is likely.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

2. WHAT IS *NOT* AFFECTED -- THE DIFFERENTIAL CONTROL STANDS

   The PART 32 s1 control does not depend on mobility at all:

     dmesg covers the full 2723 s with NO wrap, and shows exactly ONE modem
     bring-up, zero SSRs, zero fatals.

   **A network outage cannot produce a modem ERR_FATAL or an SSR.**  So the
   central differential -- same byte-identical firmware, same device, no fatal on
   Android while OpenWrt's deterministic idle fatal lands at ~902.3 s of modem
   uptime -- is untouched by the bus and remains the strongest statement in the
   corpus.  Only the "both stacks see the same 902 s event" rider is withdrawn.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

3. THE MOBILITY CONFOUND NOW BOUNDS EVERY ANDROID NUMBER IN PARTS 28-34

   This is the part with reach.  The Android soak was run on a MOVING PLATFORM;
   the OpenWrt soaks were run stationary.  Any Android-vs-OpenWrt comparison of a
   NETWORK-SENSITIVE metric is therefore confounded by the platform difference,
   and must be re-run or explicitly caveated:

   * **PART 31's RPM power-collapse series (MPSS 1.580 /s, n=10, 1.82x spread).**
     The modem's power-collapse cadence is driven by the paging/DRX cycle, which
     the RF environment and cell re-selection directly change.  A 1.82x
     per-interval spread on a bus is not necessarily the same 1.82x one would see
     stationary.  **P1/P2/P3 are NOT retracted but they are now UNTESTABLE
     against this baseline** -- the OpenWrt measurement must be compared against a
     STATIONARY Android baseline, not this one.
   * **The corpus's MCPM-cadence comparison (Android 1.88/s vs OpenWrt 0.83/s,
     = 2.3x).**  Same problem, and it may explain why the corpus could not make
     the gap behave: a moving Android will re-select cells and page differently.
     The 2.3x gap is NOT evidence of an AP-side difference until both sides are
     measured in a comparable RF environment.
   * **The pooled ping loss (PART 33 s1: 87 bursts, 1.92 %).**  This is now
     explained by mobility and is NOT a device finding.  It should not be cited
     as Android baseline loss.
   * **The 77.209 s outage and the ENETUNREACH event.**  Explained by mobility;
     not a device finding.
   * **NOT affected:** the pc-ack latency distribution (PART 28/30/33 s5 --
     vote->ack max 2.291 ms, zero real timeouts).  That is a loop-back timing
     between AP and modem over the same die, not an RF metric.  The conclusion
     that OpenWrt's 250 ms budget is 100x+ the worst healthy ack stands.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

4. THE SECOND-BOOT TEST SURVIVES, BUT ONLY ON THE CONDITIONAL STATISTIC

   The operator has authorised a second Android boot.  Mobility changes what that
   boot can decide, and the pre-registration must say so:

   * **The MARGINAL statistic is now uninformative.**  "Did a connectivity
     outage occur?" is answered by the bus, not by the modem.  On a bus an outage
     is likely; stationary it is not.  Do not score it.
   * **The CONDITIONAL statistic survives.**  The bus is essentially RANDOM with
     respect to MODEM-RELATIVE time (the modem clock restarts at each boot, and
     the bus's position at modem-relative 902.3 s is not correlated with anything
     the modem does).  So the question to score is:
       **does an outage recur at the SAME MODEM-RELATIVE time on a second boot?**
   * **The test is ASYMMETRIC and must be reported as such:**
       - A RECURRENCE at modem-relative 902.3 +- 40 s is STRONG evidence for
         Reading A, precisely BECAUSE the bus cannot produce an alignment in
         modem-relative time.
       - A NON-RECURRENCE is WEAK evidence against A: it is the expected outcome
         under Reading B, but one boot is one bit, and Reading A was already
         downgraded to "unsupported" rather than "false".
   * **If the bus returns to a stationary environment, the marginal statistic
     becomes scoreable again** and the original PART 32 s6 pre-registration
     applies unchanged.  The operator should say which condition the second boot
     is run under, and the answer must be recorded with the result.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

5. LESSON (also recorded in memory)

   **Establish the PHYSICAL/ENVIRONMENTAL context of a capture BEFORE
   interpreting its anomalies.**  A known confound -- mobility, RF environment,
   thermal state, a concurrent host operation -- can fully explain an anomaly and
   simultaneously drain an alignment argument of all its evidential weight, and
   it is invisible in every log.  Asking "where was this device and what was it
   doing?" is cheaper than any of the analysis it invalidates.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------



================================================================================
PART 34 -- LEAD, NOT A FINDING: ANDROID RUNS A 1 Hz IDLE-TIMER SERVICE THAT
           EXCHANGES MESSAGES WITH THE MODEM AND OPENTWRT HAS NO EQUIVALENT
================================================================================

STATUS: this is a LEAD recorded from a logcat census.  It is NOT a mechanism and
it is NOT claimed to cause or prevent anything.  It is written down because it is
an AP-side, IDLE-TIMERED modem control, which is the exact shape of the thing the
two differentials (D1 Android-vs-OpenWrt, D2 HMU05-vs-UFI001B) are looking for.

1. THE OBSERVATION

   The persistent logcat started in PART 32's follow-up contains 852
   `FastDormancyService` lines from pid 1118 (system_server), in 284 triplets:

     D/FastDormancyService(1118): detect mobile data activity! isAlarmExpired: false  mFDset: 3
     D/FastDormancyService(1118): sent:N received:M
     D/FastDormancyService(1118): scheduling timer:1000ms Alarm:15000ms

   i.e. a **1 Hz poll** of mobile data activity, rescheduling a **15000 ms
   alarm** each time, plus a **request/response exchange with the modem**:

     sent:0 received:0   243      sent:1 received:2     3
     sent:1 received:1    32      sent:2 received:2     1
     sent:1 received:0     4      sent:0 received:1     1

   `mFDset: 3`, `isAlarmExpired: false` and `timer:1000ms Alarm:15000ms` are
   CONSTANT across all 284 samples in this window, so the observed window is
   entirely in one state of this state machine; the 15 s alarm did not expire
   while it was being watched.  (Capture still running -- these counts grow.)

2. WHY IT IS WORTH RECORDING

   - It is **AP-side** (system_server), so it is on the correct side of D1.
   - It is **idle-timer-driven** (1 s poll, 15 s alarm), and the OpenWrt fatal is
     an idle-timer event (~902.3 s of modem uptime, deterministic when idle).
   - It **sends to the modem** (29 request/response pairs in the retained
     window), so it is a live AP->modem control channel, not passive telemetry.
   - **OpenWrt has no equivalent**: there is no fast-dormancy service, no 1 s
     mobile-data-activity poll, and (per the corpus) no periodic modem command
     during idle -- the corpus REMOVED OpenWrt's 2 s keepalive and its 60 s QMI
     write on 2026-09-20 precisely to make OpenWrt's idle look like Android's.

3. WHY IT IS ONLY A LEAD

   - `FastDormancyService` is a Java service; logcat shows it sends, but NOT what
     it sends, over which channel, or whether the modem acts on it.  No QMI/AT
     message has been correlated with a `sent:1` event.
   - This is a PATTERN, and the corpus has already been burned once by inferring
     a mechanism from a pattern without grepping it out of the running system
     (Doc 166 s5.4 blamed a logbuf lock deleted in v5.10).  Do not promote this
     to a cause on the strength of a log tag.
   - The corpus's own scoping stands: the fatal is NOT AP-runtime-PM-gated, and
     boot C's fatals were activity-CORRELATED (541/578/1397/1420 s), so an
     activity-driven AP control could move the fatal in wall-clock time without
     being its cause.

4. THE CHEAP FALSIFIER (runs on either stack, no flash)

   On OpenWrt, capture the AP->modem command channel during a long idle window
   (QMI over the `qmi_wwan`/`qmi-proxy` path, plus any AT traffic) and ask:
   **does OpenWrt emit ANY unsolicited modem command on an idle timer?**
     * If it DOES, and at a comparable cadence, the "OpenWrt has no equivalent"
       half of this lead is FALSIFIED and the lead is closed.
     * If it does NOT, the differential is CONFIRMED as a difference -- which
       still does not make it causal.  The causal test is then an A/B: emit an
       equivalent fast-dormancy signal from OpenWrt and see whether the ~902 s
       idle fatal moves, disappears, or is replaced by the activity-correlated
       signature (which is what happened under 1 Hz traffic, E1).

   PRE-REGISTERED: the falsifier is the FIRST step.  No A/B until the falsifier
   has been run, because "OpenWrt is silent during idle" is a claim the corpus
   already made once (and then had to walk back the 2 s keepalive), so it must be
   re-established by measurement, not by memory.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
